Optimal Transport — Wasserstein Distance & the Monge–Ampère Equation

Deep dive into Optimal Transport — the problem of moving mass from one distribution to another at minimal cost, originating with Gaspard Monge (1781) and revolutionized by Leonid Kantorovich (1942). Topics covered include the Monge problem (find a transport map T: X → Y minimizing ∫ c(x, T(x)) dμ(x)), the Kantorovich relaxation (a linear program on the space of couplings Π(μ, ν) minimizing ∫ c dπ), the Kantorovich duality (the sup over pairs (φ, ψ) of ∫ φ dμ + ∫ ψ dν with φ(x) + ψ(y) ≤ c(x, y)), the Wasserstein metric W_p(μ, ν) = (inf_{π∈Π(μ,ν)} ∫ |x − y|^p dπ)^{1/p} and its geometry on the space of probability measures, the Brenier theorem (for c(x, y) = |x − y|², the optimal transport map is the gradient of a convex function: T = ∇φ), the Monge–Ampère equation that the optimal map satisfies (det(D²φ) · ρ_ν(∇φ) = ρ_μ), the displacement convexity (interpolation along geodesics in the Wasserstein space preserves convex functionals), the Otto calculus (the Riemannian structure of the Wasserstein space where the metric tensor is the L² inner product on the space of potentials), the Benamou–Brenier formula (W₂²(μ, ν) = inf ∫ |v_t|² dμ_t dt over continuity equations ∂_t μ_t + ∇·(v_t μ_t) = 0), the connection to Ricci curvature via the Bakry–Émery criterion and the CD(K,N) condition of Lott–Sturm–Villani, and the dynamic formulation using the Hamilton–Jacobi equation.

"Optimal transport is the geometry of mass transference; it unifies analysis, geometry, and probability."
— Cédric Villani
Part 1

📦 The Monge and Kantorovich Problems

Formulations
Monge's Problem, Kantorovich Relaxation, and Kantorovich Duality
The Monge problem (1781): given two probability measures μ on X and ν on Y and a cost function c: X × Y → [0, ∞], find a measurable transport map T: X → Y pushing μ forward to ν (T_# μ = ν) that minimizes the total cost ∫ c(x, T(x)) dμ(x). This is a non-convex problem — the existence of a map is not guaranteed (e.g., when μ is a Dirac mass). The Kantorovich relaxation (1942): replace maps with couplings π ∈ Π(μ, ν), which are joint probability measures on X × Y with marginals μ and ν. The problem becomes linear: minimize ∫ c dπ over π ∈ Π(μ, ν). This always has a solution when c is lower semi-continuous and bounded below. The Kantorovich duality: inf_{π∈Π(μ,ν)} ∫ c dπ = sup_{(φ,ψ) ∈ Φ_c} ∫ φ dμ + ∫ ψ dν, where Φ_c = {(φ, ψ) ∈ L¹(dμ) × L¹(dν) : φ(x) + ψ(y) ≤ c(x, y)}. The maximizing pair (φ, ψ) are the Kantorovich potentials. For c(x, y) = d(x, y) on a metric space, the dual gives the Kantorovich–Rubinstein norm: W₁(μ, ν) = sup_{‖f‖_{Lip} ≤ 1} ∫ f d(μ − ν). The cyclical monotonicity condition characterizes optimal couplings: a coupling π is optimal iff its support is c-cyclically monotone — for any finite set of points (x_i, y_i) in supp(π), Σ c(x_i, y_i) ≤ Σ c(x_{i+1}, y_i) (with x_{n+1} = x_1). The c-transform: the optimal potential ψ can be chosen as ψ(y) = inf_{x ∈ X} c(x, y) − φ(x), the c-conjugate of φ, and similarly φ = ψ^c. This gives a fixed-point characterization: φ = (φ^c)^c.
The Kantorovich relaxation turns the non-convex Monge problem into a linear program on couplings, with a dual formulation in terms of Lipschitz functions — the beginning of a rich duality theory that connects to PDEs, geometry, and probability.
Part 2

🌊 Wasserstein Geometry and the Brenier Theorem

Geometry
The Wasserstein Metric, the Brenier Theorem, and the Monge–Ampère Equation
The Wasserstein p-metric W_p(μ, ν) = (inf_{π∈Π(μ,ν)} ∫ |x − y|^p dπ)^{1/p} for p ≥ 1 metrizes weak convergence (narrow convergence) on the space P_p(X) of probability measures with finite p-th moment. The space (P₂(ℝⁿ), W₂) is a geodesic space: the geodesic μ_t interpolating μ₀ and μ₁ is given by μ_t = (T_t)_# μ₀ where T_t = (1−t)Id + tT with T = ∇φ the optimal map. The Brenier theorem (1991): for μ, ν ∈ P₂(ℝⁿ) with μ absolutely continuous with respect to Lebesgue measure, there exists a unique optimal transport map T = ∇φ for the quadratic cost c(x, y) = |x − y|², where φ: ℝⁿ → ℝ is a convex function. The pushforward satisfies (∇φ)_# μ = ν. The optimal plan π = (Id, ∇φ)_# μ is uniquely determined. The Monge–Ampère equation: if μ = ρ_μ dx and ν = ρ_ν dy, then ∇φ satisfies det(D²φ(x)) · ρ_ν(∇φ(x)) = ρ_μ(x) for μ-a.e. x. This is a nonlinear second-order PDE of Monge–Ampère type. The Alexandrov theorem: a convex function φ is twice differentiable almost everywhere, so D²φ exists a.e. The Benamou–Brenier formula (2000): W₂²(μ₀, μ₁) = inf ∫₀¹ ∫ |v_t|² dμ_t dt where the infimum is over all curves μ_t of probability measures and vector fields v_t satisfying the continuity equation ∂_t μ_t + ∇·(v_t μ_t) = 0 with μ₀, μ₁ fixed. This gives the dynamic formulation of optimal transport — the optimal velocity field is a gradient: v_t = ∇φ_t for some potential φ_t solving the Hamilton–Jacobi equation ∂_t φ_t + ½|∇φ_t|² = 0.
The Brenier theorem provides an explicit construction of the optimal transport map as the gradient of a convex function, linking optimal transport to the Monge–Ampère equation and giving the Wasserstein space a rich geometric and analytic structure.
Part 3

📈 Displacement Convexity and Ricci Curvature

Curvature
Displacement Convexity, the Bakry–Émery Criterion, and the CD(K,N) Condition
Displacement convexity (McCann, 1997): a functional F on P₂(ℝⁿ) is displacement convex if it is convex along Wasserstein geodesics: F(μ_t) ≤ (1−t)F(μ₀) + tF(μ₁). The internal energy U_ν(μ) = ∫ U(dμ/dν) dν for a convex function U is displacement convex on ℝⁿ (a result of the Brenier theorem and the convexity of U). In particular, the Boltzmann–Shannon entropy H(μ) = ∫ ρ log ρ dx is displacement convex on ℝⁿ. The Bakry–Émery criterion: on a Riemannian manifold M, the entropy H(μ) = ∫ ρ log ρ vol is displacement convex iff Ric ≥ 0. More precisely, for a Riemannian manifold with Ricci curvature bounded below by K ∈ ℝ, the entropy along a Wasserstein geodesic satisfies: H(μ_t) ≤ (1−t)H(μ₀) + tH(μ₁) − (K/2) t(1−t) W₂²(μ₀, μ₁) — the K-convexity of entropy on the Wasserstein space. This leads to the Lott–Sturm–Villani CD(K,N) condition: a metric measure space (X, d, m) satisfies CD(K,N) if the Rényi entropy S_N(μ) = −∫ ρ^{1−1/N} dm is K-convex along W₂-geodesics in P₂(X, m). Here K is the lower Ricci curvature bound and N is the upper dimension bound. The CD(K,∞) condition: for any two μ₀, μ₁ ∈ P₂(X) absolutely continuous w.r.t. m, there exists a geodesic μ_t such that H(μ_t) ≤ (1−t)H(μ₀) + tH(μ₁) − (K/2) t(1−t) W₂²(μ₀, μ₁). The Sturm theorem (2006): the CD(K,N) condition is stable under Gromov–Hausdorff convergence and tensorizes (a product of CD(K,N) spaces is CD(K,N₁+N₂)). The Brunn–Minkowski inequality on manifolds is a consequence of CD(K,N). The Bishop–Gromov volume comparison also follows from CD(K,N).
Displacement convexity of entropy in the Wasserstein space characterizes lower Ricci curvature bounds — this is the heart of the Lott–Sturm–Villani synthetic approach to Ricci curvature for metric measure spaces.
Part 4

🔬 The Otto Calculus and Synthetic Ricci Curvature

Calculus
The Otto Calculus on the Wasserstein Space and the RCD Condition
The Otto calculus (Otto, 2001): the Wasserstein space (P₂(M), W₂) can be viewed as a Riemannian manifold of infinite dimension. The tangent space at a smooth density ρ is T_ρ P₂ = {∇φ : φ ∈ C∞(M)} — the space of gradient vector fields. The Riemannian metric at ρ: ⟨∇φ, ∇ψ⟩_ρ = ∫ ∇φ·∇ψ dρ. The geodesic equation becomes the continuity equation ∂_t ρ_t + ∇·(ρ_t ∇φ_t) = 0 combined with the Hamilton–Jacobi equation ∂_t φ_t + ½|∇φ_t|² = 0, which together imply ∂_t² ρ_t = ∇·(ρ_t ∇(Δ φ_t + ½|∇φ_t|²)). The Ricci curvature of the Wasserstein space appears in the evolution of the Laplacian of the entropy. The Bakry–Émery operator Γ₂(f) = ½Δ|∇f|² − ⟨∇f, ∇Δf⟩ = Ric(∇f, ∇f) + ‖Hess f‖² gives the Γ₂-criterion: Ric ≥ K iff Γ₂(f) ≥ K|∇f|² for all smooth f. The RCD(K,N) condition (Gigli–Kuwada–Ohta–Ambrosio–Tresoldi): a refinement of CD(K,N) that also requires the space to be infinitesimally Hilbertian — the Sobolev space W^{1,2}(X) is a Hilbert space. This eliminates Finsler-like behavior (e.g., the CD condition on ℓ∞ spaces) and restricts to genuinely Riemannian metric measure spaces. The RCD condition is stable under tensorization, Gromov–Hausdorff convergence, and measured Gromov–Hausdorff convergence. Splitting theorem (Gigli): an RCD(0,N) space with a line splits as a product ℝ × Y. Rectifiability (Mondino–Naber): RCD(K,N) spaces are rectifiable — they have a regular set of full measure that is bi-Lipschitz equivalent to Euclidean space. The Brenier theorem on manifolds (McCann): on a compact Riemannian manifold, the optimal map for c(x, y) = d(x, y)²/2 is given by T(x) = exp_x(∇_x φ(x)) for a d²/2-concave function φ — the McCann interpolation along geodesics. The program of synthetic Ricci curvature continues to flourish: the c-convexity approach of Gozlan–Roberto–Samson–Tetali and the entropy-cost inequality framework.
The Otto calculus gives a formal Riemannian structure to the Wasserstein space, making precise the connection between optimal transport, Ricci curvature, and the synthetic geometry of metric measure spaces in the RCD framework.

🎯 Practice

← Back to Math Map

Regularity Theory for Optimal Transport Maps

The Brenier theorem guarantees that the optimal transport map between two absolutely continuous probability measures on ℝⁿ is the gradient of a convex function. The regularity of this map — whether it is continuous, differentiable, or even smooth — is governed by the Monge–Ampère equation det D²φ = f/g∘∇φ, where f and g are the densities of the source and target measures. Caffarelli’s landmark results (1990‑1996) established interior C¹,α regularity of the optimal map when both densities are bounded away from zero and infinity on convex domains. If, in addition, the densities are Ck,α and the domains are uniformly convex with Ck+2,α boundaries, the map is Ck+1,α up to the boundary. The key geometric condition is the convexity of the target domain; without it, singularities can appear even for smooth positive densities, as shown by the famous “fold” examples of Caffarelli and McCann. Later work by Figalli, Kim, and others extended regularity to the case where the target measure is supported on a non‑convex set but satisfies a “c‑convexity” condition relative to the cost c(x,y)=|x−y|²/2. On Riemannian manifolds, Ma–Trudinger–Wang introduced the MTW tensor (also called the cost‑sectional curvature) as the curvature quantity controlling regularity for general costs. For the quadratic cost on a manifold, the MTW tensor coincides with the sectional curvature of the manifold, so non‑negative sectional curvature yields regularity, while negative curvature can produce discontinuities. These results are essential for applications in geometric analysis, where one needs to differentiate the transport map to compute Jacobians, Hessians, or to linearise the Monge–Ampère operator.

Key Theorems and Conditions

  • Caffarelli’s interior regularity (1992): If f,g ∈ L^∞, f,g ≥ λ > 0 on bounded convex domains Ω,Λ ⊂ ℝⁿ, then the Brenier map ∇φ is locally C¹,α in Ω.
  • Global regularity (1996): If ∂Ω,∂Λ are Ck+2,α uniformly convex and f,g ∈ Ck,α with positive bounds, then ∇φ ∈ Ck+1,α(Ω̄).
  • MTW condition (Ma–Trudinger–Wang, 2005): For a cost c ∈ C⁴, the tensor 𝔖ijkl = cij,kl − cij,rcrscsk,l must be non‑negative on null‑Lagrangian planes to guarantee smoothness of optimal maps.
  • Counterexamples: Caffarelli’s “discontinuous map” on a non‑convex target; Loeper’s example on the sphere showing that positive curvature is not sufficient without the MTW condition.

In practice, examiners often ask to identify which hypothesis fails in a given setting, or to explain why the MTW tensor appears when differentiating the Monge–Ampère equation along a geodesic. A typical twist is to present a cost c(x,y)=h(|x−y|) with h strictly convex and ask whether the MTW condition holds; the answer depends on the sign of h''' and the dimension.

Entropic Regularization and the Sinkhorn Algorithm

Computing the exact Wasserstein distance between discrete measures of size N requires solving a linear program with O(N³) complexity, which is prohibitive for large datasets. Entropic regularization adds a Kullback–Leibler penalty ε H(π|μ⊗ν) to the Kantorovich problem, where H is the relative entropy and ε>0 is a regularization parameter. The regularized problem admits a unique solution of the form πij=a_i Kij b_j with Kij=exp(−cij/ε), where a,b are positive scaling vectors. The Sinkhorn algorithm (also called the matrix scaling algorithm) iteratively updates a and b to enforce the marginal constraints: a(k+1) = μ ./ (K b(k)), b(k+1) = ν ./ (Kᵀ a(k+1)). This iteration converges linearly to the optimal scaling vectors, and each step costs O(N²) operations, making it highly parallelisable on GPUs. The regularized optimal transport cost converges to the true Wasserstein cost as ε→0, with an error of order ε log N for discrete measures. However, small ε leads to numerical instability because Kij underflows; practical implementations use log‑domain stabilization or the “Greenkhorn” variant with adaptive updates. The entropic regularization also yields a differentiable approximation of the Wasserstein distance, enabling gradient‑based learning in machine learning (e.g., Wasserstein GANs, domain adaptation). Theoretical guarantees include the convergence of the Sinkhorn iterates in Hilbert’s projective metric (Franklin–Lorenz, 1989) and the recent non‑asymptotic bounds of Altschuler–Weed–Rigollet (2017) showing O(N²/ε²) iterations to achieve ε‑accuracy in the transport plan. For continuous measures, the Schrödinger bridge problem provides a dynamic interpretation: the entropic regularization corresponds to the most likely path of a diffusion process with prescribed endpoints, linking optimal transport to stochastic control and large deviations.

Algorithmic Variants and Extensions

  • Log‑domain Sinkhorn: Operates on log‑scaling vectors u,v with updates u←log μ − log(K exp v), avoiding underflow.
  • Greenkhorn (coordinate descent): Updates a single coordinate per iteration, useful for sparse cost matrices.
  • Debiased Sinkhorn (Genevay et al., 2018): Subtracts the self‑regularization terms to obtain an unbiased estimator of W₂².
  • Unbalanced OT: Replaces marginal constraints with KL penalties, leading to a generalized Sinkhorn with two regularization parameters.

Examiners frequently test the understanding of the bias‑variance trade‑off: a larger ε gives faster convergence but a more biased distance; a smaller ε reduces bias but requires more iterations and careful numerics. A common trap is to claim that the Sinkhorn algorithm computes the exact Wasserstein distance for any ε>0 — it computes the regularized distance, which only converges to the true distance as ε→0.

Worked Example: Sinkhorn Iteration on a 2×2 Cost Matrix
Let μ=(0.6,0.4), ν=(0.3,0.7) and cost matrix C=[[0,1],[1,0]]. Take ε=0.1. Perform two full Sinkhorn iterations (update a then b) starting from a⁰=b⁰=(1,1). Report the scaling vectors after the second iteration and the resulting transport plan π.

Step 0: K = exp(−C/ε) = [[exp(0), exp(−10)], [exp(−10), exp(0)]] ≈ [[1, 4.54×10⁻⁵], [4.54×10⁻⁵, 1]].

Iteration 1 — update a: a¹ = μ ./ (K b⁰) = (0.6,0.4) ./ ( [1+4.54e-5, 4.54e-5+1] ) ≈ (0.6/1.0000454, 0.4/1.0000454) ≈ (0.59997, 0.39998).

Update b: b¹ = ν ./ (Kᵀ a¹) = (0.3,0.7) ./ ( [1·0.59997+4.54e-5·0.39998, 4.54e-5·0.59997+1·0.39998] ) ≈ (0.3/0.60001, 0.7/0.40001) ≈ (0.49999, 1.74996).

Iteration 2 — update a: a² = μ ./ (K b¹) = (0.6,0.4) ./ ( [1·0.49999+4.54e-5·1.74996, 4.54e-5·0.49999+1·1.74996] ) ≈ (0.6/0.50007, 0.4/1.75000) ≈ (1.1998, 0.22857).

Update b: b² = ν ./ (Kᵀ a²) = (0.3,0.7) ./ ( [1·1.1998+4.54e-5·0.22857, 4.54e-5·1.1998+1·0.22857] ) ≈ (0.3/1.19985, 0.7/0.22862) ≈ (0.25003, 3.0618).

Transport plan after iteration 2: π = diag(a²) K diag(b²) ≈ [[1.1998·1·0.25003, 1.1998·4.54e-5·3.0618], [0.22857·4.54e-5·0.25003, 0.22857·1·3.0618]] ≈ [[0.3000, 1.66e-4], [2.59e-6, 0.7000]]. The marginals are close to μ,ν; further iterations converge to the exact regularized plan.

Exam‑Day Strategy for Entropic OT Questions
When a question asks for the effect of ε on the Sinkhorn algorithm, remember: (1) the regularized cost is always ≥ the true Wasserstein cost; (2) the bias is O(ε log N) for discrete measures; (3) the number of iterations to reach a fixed tolerance scales like 1/ε. If you must choose between a smaller ε with more iterations or a larger ε with fewer iterations, the total computational work is roughly O(N²/ε) for a target accuracy in the transport plan. Mention log‑domain stabilization to show awareness of numerical issues.

Gradient Flows in Wasserstein Space and the JKO Scheme

The Jordan–Kinderlehrer–Otto (JKO) scheme (1998) provides a variational time‑discretization of gradient flows in the Wasserstein space (P₂(ℝⁿ),W₂). Given a functional F: P₂→ℝ∪{+∞} that is lower semicontinuous and λ‑convex along geodesics, the implicit Euler scheme defines a sequence ρk_τ by ρk_τ ∈ argminρ∈P₂ { F(ρ) + (1/2τ) W₂²(ρ, ρk-1_τ) }. As the time step τ→0, the piecewise constant interpolation converges to the gradient flow curve ρ_t satisfying the Evolution Variational Inequality (EVI) ½ d/dt W₂²(ρ_t, ν) + F(ρ_t) ≤ F(ν) − (λ/2) W₂²(ρ_t, ν) for all ν∈P₂. The prototypical example is the Boltzmann–Shannon entropy F(ρ)=∫ ρ log ρ dx, whose gradient flow is the heat equation ∂_t ρ = Δρ. More generally, for the internal energy F(ρ)=∫ U(ρ) dx with U convex, the gradient flow is the nonlinear diffusion equation ∂_t ρ = Δ U'(ρ) (porous medium, fast diffusion). The JKO scheme also captures the Fokker–Planck equation ∂_t ρ = ∇·(ρ ∇V) + Δρ as the gradient flow of the free energy F(ρ)=∫ ρ log ρ dx + ∫ V ρ dx. The key analytical tools are the displacement convexity of F (McCann’s condition) and the Benamou–Brenier dynamic formulation of W₂, which turns the minimization into a convex problem over curves (ρ_t, v_t) satisfying the continuity equation. The scheme has been extended to metric measure spaces satisfying the CD(K,∞) condition (Ambrosio–Gigli–Savaré, 2005), where the EVI formulation characterizes the heat flow and yields contractivity in W₂. In numerical analysis, the JKO scheme inspires proximal algorithms for PDEs: each step requires solving an optimal transport problem, which can be approximated by entropic regularization or by semi‑discrete methods. Recent work by Carlier–Duval–Peyré–Schmitzer (2020) shows that the JKO scheme for the entropy can be implemented via a sequence of Sinkhorn iterations, linking the variational time stepping to the computational OT toolbox.

Key Concepts and Extensions

  • λ‑convexity along geodesics: F(ρ_t) ≤ (1−t)F(ρ₀)+tF(ρ₁) − (λ/2)t(1−t)W₂²(ρ₀,ρ₁).
  • EVI characterization: A curve ρ_t is the gradient flow of F iff it satisfies the EVI for all ν.
  • Contractivity: If F is λ‑convex, the gradient flow is e−λt‑Lipschitz in W₂.
  • Generalized minimizing movements: The JKO scheme works in any complete metric space with a lower semicontinuous λ‑convex functional.
  • Applications: Crowd motion models (Hughes), image processing (Wasserstein gradient flows for denoising), Bayesian inference (Wasserstein gradient flow of KL divergence).

Examiners often ask to derive the PDE corresponding to a given energy functional via the first variation in Wasserstein space, or to verify the λ‑convexity condition for a specific U. A typical twist is to present a functional that is not displacement convex (e.g., U(ρ)=−ρ^p with p>1) and ask why the JKO scheme may fail to converge to a unique limit.

Multi‑Marginal Optimal Transport and Density Functional Theory

Multi‑marginal optimal transport (MMOT) generalizes the two‑marginal Kantorovich problem to K ≥ 3 marginals μ₁,…,μ_K on spaces X₁,…,X_K with a cost function c: X₁×⋯×X_K → ℝ. The problem is to minimize ∫ c dπ over couplings π ∈ Π(μ₁,…,μ_K). When the cost is the sum of pairwise interactions c(x₁,…,x_K)=∑i<j</sub> cij(x_i,x_j), the problem is equivalent to a two‑marginal problem on the product space, but for genuinely multi‑body costs (e.g., the Coulomb cost c(x₁,…,x_K)=∑i<j</sub> 1/|x_i−x_j| in ℝ³) the structure is richer. MMOT arises naturally in density functional theory (DFT): the strong‑interaction limit of the Hohenberg–Kohn functional leads to the “strictly correlated electrons” (SCE) functional, which is exactly a multi‑marginal OT problem with Coulomb cost and identical marginals given by the electron density ρ. The optimal plan is supported on graphs of maps T₂,…,T_K: X₁→X₁ such that T_i#ρ=ρ and the maps are cyclically monotone with respect to the cost. For the Coulomb cost in one dimension, the optimal maps are explicit: T_i(x)=F_ρ-1(F_ρ(x)+(i−1)/K) where F_ρ is the cumulative distribution function. In higher dimensions, the structure of optimal plans is an active research area; recent results by Pass, Gigli, and others show that for repulsive costs satisfying a “twist” condition, the optimal plan is concentrated on a Lipschitz manifold of dimension equal to the base space. The MMOT problem also appears in economics (matching with multiple agents), in the Wasserstein barycenter problem (which can be written as a multi‑marginal problem with a specific cost), and in the study of martingale optimal transport where the marginals are constrained to be in convex order. Numerically, MMOT can be tackled by entropic regularization and a generalized Sinkhorn algorithm that iteratively scales a K‑dimensional tensor, but the curse of dimensionality is severe; low‑rank tensor formats (Tensor‑Train, Hierarchical Tucker) are currently the state of the art for K≥4.

Highlights and Open Directions

  • SCE functional: VSCE[ρ] = infπ∈Π(ρ,…,ρ) ∫ ∑i<j</sub> |x_i−x_j|-1 dπ — exact in the strong‑coupling limit of DFT.
  • Twist condition: For cost c(x₁,…,x_K), the map (x₂,…,x_K) ↦ ∇x₁c is injective; ensures optimal plans are deterministic (Monge‑type).
  • Cyclic monotonicity: The support of an optimal plan is c‑cyclically monotone; for pairwise costs this reduces to standard cyclic monotonicity of each pair.
  • Tensor‑train Sinkhorn: Represents the K‑dimensional scaling tensor in a compressed format, reducing complexity from O(N^K) to O(K N r²) with rank r.
  • Martingale MMOT: Adds the constraint that the conditional expectation of later marginals given the first equals the first marginal; relevant for robust finance.

Exam questions may ask to formulate the SCE functional as a multi‑marginal OT problem, to explain why the twist condition guarantees a Monge solution, or to describe the computational bottleneck of the generalized Sinkhorn algorithm. A common trap is to confuse the multi‑marginal Wasserstein barycenter cost (which is a sum of squared distances to a barycenter variable) with a genuine multi‑body interaction cost; the former reduces to a two‑marginal problem via the barycenter variable, the latter does not.

  • Assuming that Caffarelli’s regularity theory holds for any cost function — it is specific to the quadratic cost (or costs satisfying the MTW condition).
  • Claiming the Sinkhorn algorithm computes the exact Wasserstein distance for a fixed ε>0 — it computes the entropically regularized distance, which only converges to W₂ as ε→0.
  • Confusing λ‑convexity of a functional along Wasserstein geodesics with ordinary convexity in the linear space of measures — displacement convexity is strictly stronger and requires McCann’s condition.
  • Thinking the JKO scheme converges for any lower semicontinuous functional — λ‑convexity (or at least geodesic convexity) is essential for uniqueness and convergence.
  • Believing that multi‑marginal OT with pairwise costs is always equivalent to a two‑marginal problem — this holds only when the cost separates as a sum of pairwise terms and the marginals are free; genuine multi‑body costs (e.g., Coulomb) do not reduce.
  • Overlooking the twist condition when asserting existence of a Monge map in multi‑marginal problems — without twist, optimal plans may be genuinely probabilistic.
  • Neglecting the curse of dimensionality in tensor‑based Sinkhorn for K≥4 — low‑rank approximations are not guaranteed to capture the optimal plan accurately without additional structural assumptions.

The Benamou–Brenier Dynamic Formulation and Fluid Mechanics Interpretation

The static Kantorovich problem admits a dynamic reformulation due to Benamou and Brenier (2000). Instead of searching for a coupling π, one looks for a time‑dependent density ρ(t,·) and a velocity field v(t,·) on the interval [0,1] satisfying the continuity equation ∂ₜρ + ∇·(ρv) = 0 with prescribed endpoints ρ(0)=μ₀ and ρ(1)=μ₁. The kinetic energy ∫₀¹∫ |v|² ρ dx dt is then minimized. The minimal value equals ½ W₂(μ₀,μ₁)². This formulation reveals that optimal transport is the action‑minimizing flow of a pressureless fluid, and the optimal velocity field is the gradient of a time‑dependent potential φ solving the Hamilton–Jacobi equation ∂ₜφ + ½|∇φ|² = 0. The Benamou–Brenier formula is the cornerstone of numerical methods that discretize the continuity equation (e.g., staggered grid finite volumes, augmented Lagrangian schemes) and of the theoretical link between optimal transport and the Euler equations for incompressible fluids. In particular, the geodesic equations in Wasserstein space correspond to the pressureless Euler system, while adding a pressure term yields the incompressible Euler equations via the Otto calculus. The dynamic viewpoint also underpins the JKO scheme: each implicit Euler step minimizes the sum of the squared Wasserstein distance to the previous density and the internal energy, which is exactly a time‑discretized version of the Benamou–Brenier action.

Key consequences include the displacement interpolation (McCann interpolation) ρₜ = ((1−t)Id + tT)#μ₀, which solves the continuity equation with velocity vₜ = (T−Id)∘((1−t)Id + tT)⁻¹. This interpolation is the constant‑speed geodesic in (P₂(ℝⁿ),W₂). The dynamic formulation extends to general cost functions c(x,y)=h(y−x) with h strictly convex, leading to a Lagrangian action ∫ L(v)ρ where L is the Legendre transform of h. For the quadratic cost, L(v)=½|v|². The Benamou–Brenier approach also provides a natural framework for mean‑field games and crowd motion models where the velocity field is chosen by agents minimizing a cost that depends on the density.

The Ma–Trudinger–Wang Tensor and Cost Regularity Theory

Regularity of optimal transport maps for non‑quadratic costs is governed by the Ma–Trudinger–Wang (MTW) tensor, introduced in 2005. For a smooth cost c(x,y) on a product manifold, define the cross‑derivative matrix D²ₓᵧc. The MTW tensor is a fourth‑order tensor Sᵢⱼₖₗ = cᵢⱼ,ₖₗ − cᵢⱼ,ᵣ cʳˢ cₛ,ₖₗ, where indices after a comma denote covariant derivatives with respect to the cost‑sectional geometry. The condition (A3w) requires S(ξ,η,ξ,η) ≥ 0 for all ξ,η with ξ⊥η (in the cost‑metric), while (A3s) demands strict positivity for ξ,η ≠ 0. When (A3w) holds, optimal maps are continuous; under (A3s) and appropriate boundary convexity (c‑convexity of domains), maps are smooth. The quadratic cost c(x,y)=½|x−y|² satisfies (A3s) trivially because the MTW tensor vanishes. Costs of the form c(x,y)=h(|x−y|) on ℝⁿ satisfy (A3w) iff h''(r) ≥ 0 and (h'''(r)r − h''(r)) ≥ 0; the latter fails for the Euclidean distance cost c(x,y)=|x−y|, explaining the lack of regularity for the Monge problem with that cost. On the sphere with cost c(x,y)=½d(x,y)², (A3s) holds, yielding smooth optimal maps for positive densities. The MTW condition is also necessary for the stability of the Monge–Ampère equation under perturbations of the cost. Recent work by Figalli, Kim, and McCann extends the theory to costs with singularities (e.g., the relativistic heat cost) and to the multi‑marginal setting, where a generalized MTW tensor controls the regularity of the optimal maps T₂,…,T_K.

Worked Example: Verifying the MTW Condition for a Power Cost
Consider the cost c(x,y)=|x−y|^p/p on ℝⁿ with p>1. Determine for which p the cost satisfies the weak MTW condition (A3w).
For radial costs c(x,y)=h(|x−y|) with h(r)=r^p/p, we have h'(r)=rp−1, h''(r)=(p−1)rp−2, h'''(r)=(p−1)(p−2)rp−3. The MTW condition (A3w) reduces to h''(r) ≥ 0 and (h'''(r)r − h''(r)) ≥ 0. The first holds for all p>1. The second gives (p−1)(p−2)rp−2 − (p−1)rp−2 = (p−1)(p−3)rp−2 ≥ 0. Since p>1, this requires p ≥ 3. Therefore, the cost satisfies (A3w) exactly when p ≥ 3. For 1

Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks about regularity of optimal maps for a given cost, first identify whether the cost is quadratic, a power of distance, or a Riemannian distance squared. Recall that quadratic cost always yields smooth maps under Caffarelli's theory (convex domains, bounded densities). For power costs |x−y|^p/p, remember the threshold p≥3 for (A3w). For Riemannian manifolds, the cost ½d² satisfies (A3s) on spheres and more generally on manifolds with non‑negative sectional curvature. If the cost is the Euclidean distance (p=1), the Monge problem may have no map; the Kantorovich relaxation is essential. Mention the MTW tensor by name and the conditions (A3w)/(A3s) to show depth.

Unbalanced and Partial Optimal Transport

Classical optimal transport assumes equal total mass of the source and target measures. In many applications (medical imaging, cell biology, domain adaptation) the masses differ or only a fraction of mass should be transported. Unbalanced optimal transport relaxes the marginal constraints by adding a penalty for mass creation/annihilation. A common formulation uses the Kullback–Leibler divergence: minimize ∫ c dπ + KL(π₁|μ₀) + KL(π₂|μ₁) over non‑negative measures π, where π₁,π₂ are the marginals of π. This is equivalent to the Hellinger–Kantorovich (or Wasserstein–Fisher–Rao) distance, which metrizes weak convergence plus convergence of total mass. The dynamic Benamou–Brenier formulation extends by adding a reaction term: ∂ₜρ + ∇·(ρv) = ρr, with a cost ∫ (|v|² + α r²) ρ. The resulting geodesics interpolate both transport and mass change, and the distance satisfies a triangle inequality. Partial optimal transport, introduced by Caffarelli and McCann (2010), fixes a prescribed mass m ≤ min(μ₀(X),μ₁(Y)) and minimizes ∫ c dπ over couplings with marginals bounded by μ₀,μ₁ and total mass m. The optimal plan is supported on a c‑cyclically monotone set, and the free boundaries of the active regions satisfy a Monge–Ampère type equation with obstacle conditions. Numerically, unbalanced OT can be solved by a generalized Sinkhorn algorithm that alternates scaling of rows and columns with a global mass scaling factor; partial OT can be tackled by adding a dummy bin with zero cost to absorb excess mass.

Martingale Optimal Transport and Robust Finance

Martingale optimal transport (MOT) adds the constraint that the transport plan π must be a martingale coupling: for μ₀,μ₁ on ℝ with μ₀ ≤_cx μ₁ (convex order), π ∈ Π(μ₀,μ₁) and ∫ y π_x(dy) = x for μ₀‑a.e. x. This models the fundamental theorem of asset pricing: μ₀ is the distribution of an asset price today, μ₁ the distribution at maturity, and the martingale condition encodes the absence of arbitrage. The MOT problem minimizes (or maximizes) ∫ c(x,y) dπ over martingale couplings. For the quadratic cost c(x,y)=(y−x)², the minimal value equals the variance of μ₁ minus the variance of μ₀, and the optimal coupling is the left‑curtain coupling (Henry‑Labordère, Touzi 2016). The left‑curtain coupling is constructed by a monotone rearrangement of the quantile functions and is the unique martingale coupling that minimizes the convex order. For general costs, the dual formulation involves functions φ,ψ and a process H such that φ(x)+ψ(y)+H(x)(y−x) ≥ c(x,y). The dual optimizer yields robust super‑hedging prices for exotic options. MOT extends to multi‑period settings (discrete‑time martingale transport) and to multi‑dimensional assets where the convex order is replaced by the martingale order on ℝ^d. Numerically, MOT can be solved by linear programming on a grid or by a martingale‑constrained Sinkhorn algorithm that enforces the conditional expectation constraint via a projection step. The theory connects to the Skorokhod embedding problem and to the study of model‑independent bounds in finance.

Optimal Transport on Metric Measure Spaces: Lott–Sturm–Villani Theory

The synthetic theory of Ricci curvature lower bounds, developed independently by Lott–Villani (2009) and Sturm (2006), defines the condition CD(K,N) on a metric measure space (X,d,m) using optimal transport. A space satisfies CD(K,N) if for any two absolutely continuous measures μ₀,μ₁ there exists a W₂‑geodesic (μₜ) such that the Renyi entropy Ent_m(μₜ) = ∫ ρₜ log ρₜ dm is K‑convex along the geodesic, i.e., Ent_m(μₜ) ≤ (1−t)Ent_m(μ₀) + t Ent_m(μ₁) − (K/2) t(1−t) W₂(μ₀,μ₁)², with a dimensional correction when N<∞. This definition requires only a metric and a reference measure, no smooth structure. It recovers the classical Bakry–Émery condition on Riemannian manifolds and extends to Alexandrov spaces, Finsler manifolds, and RCD(K,∞) spaces (where the heat flow is linear). The theory yields powerful geometric and functional inequalities: the Brunn–Minkowski inequality, the Bishop–Gromov volume comparison, the Lichnerowicz spectral gap, and the Bonnet–Myers diameter bound. The RCD(K,N) condition (adding infinitesimal Hilbertianity) ensures stability under measured Gromov–Hausdorff convergence and provides a robust framework for non‑smooth spaces. Recent advances include the equivalence of CD(K,N) with the entropic curvature‑dimension condition, the localization technique reducing CD to one‑dimensional needle spaces, and the application to Ricci flow and singular spaces in general relativity. The synthetic approach also underpins the definition of the Wasserstein space over a metric measure space and the study of gradient flows of entropy, which coincide with the heat flow.

  • Assuming the Benamou–Brenier formula holds for any cost without checking the Legendre transform structure — it requires a Lagrangian that is the convex conjugate of the cost.
  • Confusing the weak MTW condition (A3w) with the strong condition (A3s) — (A3w) gives continuity, (A3s) gives smoothness; the quadratic cost satisfies both trivially because its MTW tensor vanishes.
  • Thinking unbalanced OT is just classical OT with a dummy bin — the Hellinger–Kantorovich distance has a different geometry (it is a cone metric) and the dynamic formulation includes a reaction term.
  • Believing that any two measures in convex order admit a martingale coupling for any cost — the martingale constraint is independent of the cost; existence requires the convex order, but optimality depends on the cost structure.
  • Stating that CD(K,N) implies a smooth Riemannian manifold — CD(K,N) holds on many non‑smooth spaces (e.g., cones, metric graphs) and is strictly weaker than a lower Ricci bound on a smooth manifold.
  • Overlooking the dimensional parameter N in CD(K,N) — the condition with N=∞ is weaker and does not yield volume comparison; finite N is needed for Bishop–Gromov and Bonnet–Myers.
  • Claiming the left‑curtain coupling is the unique martingale coupling for all costs — it is optimal only for the quadratic cost (or more generally for costs of the form h(y−x) with h convex); for other costs the optimal martingale coupling differs.

Numerical Optimal Transport Beyond Entropic Regularization

Entropic regularization and the Sinkhorn algorithm dominate large‑scale optimal transport because they turn a linear program into a matrix scaling problem that is trivially parallelizable on GPUs. However, the regularization parameter ε introduces a bias that vanishes only as ε → 0, and the number of Sinkhorn iterations grows like O(1/ε). For applications that require exact marginals — such as computing Wasserstein barycenters for shape matching or solving Monge–Ampère equations — practitioners resort to unregularized or lightly regularized solvers. The classical network simplex method solves the discrete Kantorovich problem in O(n³ log n) worst‑case time but often performs near‑linearly on sparse cost matrices; it is the workhorse in the Python Optimal Transport (POT) library for problems up to a few thousand points. Semi‑discrete optimal transport, where one measure is continuous (e.g. a density on a domain) and the other is a sum of Dirac masses, reduces to a convex optimization over power diagrams. The objective is the sum of integrals of the cost over Laguerre cells, and its gradient is given by the cell masses. Newton‑type methods on the dual variables converge quadratically once the diagram topology stabilises, making semi‑discrete OT the method of choice for fluid‑mechanics simulations and for the numerical solution of the Monge–Ampère equation via the Benamou–Brenier formulation. For very large datasets (millions of points) stochastic optimization has become competitive: the Greenkhorn algorithm randomly selects a row or column to update, achieving an expected per‑iteration cost independent of the matrix size, while stochastic gradient descent on the dual of the entropic problem yields unbiased gradient estimates with variance that can be controlled by mini‑batch sampling. Recent work on multiscale optimal transport builds a hierarchy of coarsened cost matrices, solves the problem on the coarsest level, and progressively refines the transport plan, delivering near‑linear runtime with provable approximation guarantees. Finally, GPU‑accelerated linear programming solvers based on the primal‑dual interior‑point method now handle dense cost matrices of size 10⁴ × 10⁴ within seconds, opening the door to exact OT in high‑dimensional machine‑learning pipelines where entropic bias is unacceptable.

Wasserstein Generative Models and Domain Adaptation

The Wasserstein distance provides a meaningful loss for training generative models because it metrizes weak convergence and does not saturate when the supports of the model and data distributions are disjoint — a fatal flaw of the Jensen–Shannon divergence used in the original GAN. The Wasserstein GAN (WGAN) replaces the discriminator by a 1‑Lipschitz critic f and maximizes 𝔼x∼μ_data[f(x)] − 𝔼z∼μ_z[f(G(z))] subject to the Lipschitz constraint. In practice the constraint is enforced either by weight clipping (the original WGAN) or by a gradient penalty (WGAN‑GP) that penalizes ‖∇f‖² − 1 on interpolates between real and generated samples. The gradient penalty yields stable training across a wide range of architectures and has become the de‑facto standard for high‑fidelity image synthesis. Beyond generation, the Wasserstein distance drives unsupervised domain adaptation: given a source distribution μ_s with labels and an unlabeled target distribution μ_t, one learns a feature map Φ that minimizes W₁(Φ♯μ_s, Φ♯μ_t) while preserving classification accuracy on the source. The resulting feature space aligns the marginal distributions without requiring pairwise correspondences, and the optimal transport plan between the aligned marginals can be used to transfer labels via a barycentric mapping. In multi‑source adaptation, the Wasserstein barycenter of the source feature distributions serves as a domain‑invariant anchor, and the transport plans from each source to the barycenter provide a principled way to combine classifiers. Recent theoretical work shows that the generalization error of a hypothesis transferred via Wasserstein alignment is bounded by the sum of the source error, the Wasserstein distance between source and target, and the Rademacher complexity of the hypothesis class — a bound that is tight for Lipschitz losses. Practically, the Sinkhorn algorithm with a small ε is used to compute the alignment loss differentiably, enabling end‑to‑end training of deep networks with optimal‑transport layers. Extensions to the Gromov–Wasserstein distance allow alignment of distributions supported on spaces with different dimensionalities, which is essential for cross‑modal tasks such as text‑to‑image retrieval.

Worked Example: Gradient Penalty in WGAN‑GP
Consider a critic f_θ : ℝ^d → ℝ parameterized by a neural network. The WGAN‑GP objective adds the term λ 𝔼x̂[(‖∇x̂ f_θ(x̂)‖₂ − 1)²] where x̂ = ε x + (1−ε) G(z), ε ∼ Uniform(0,1). Show that if f_θ is 1‑Lipschitz everywhere, the penalty term is zero, and explain why the expectation over interpolates is sufficient to enforce the Lipschitz condition almost everywhere.
If f_θ is 1‑Lipschitz, then by definition ‖∇ f_θ(x)‖₂ ≤ 1 for almost every x (Rademacher's theorem). At points where the gradient exists, the norm equals the local Lipschitz constant, so ‖∇ f_θ(x)‖₂ = 1 on the set where the constraint is active. The penalty uses the squared deviation (‖∇ f_θ(x̂)‖₂ − 1)², which is zero exactly when the norm equals 1. Since the interpolates x̂ cover the line segments between real and generated samples, and the optimal critic for W₁ is known to have gradient norm 1 on the transport rays (by the Kantorovich–Rubinstein duality), penalizing only on these segments forces the network to satisfy the Lipschitz condition on the region that matters for the dual objective. The expectation over ε ∼ Uniform(0,1) ensures that every point on each segment is sampled with positive probability, so the constraint holds almost everywhere on the union of transport rays. In practice this yields a critic that is 1‑Lipschitz on the data manifold without requiring global weight clipping.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks to compare WGAN with the original GAN, focus on three points: (1) the loss function (Wasserstein vs Jensen–Shannon), (2) the role of the Lipschitz constraint and how it is enforced (weight clipping vs gradient penalty), and (3) the consequence for training stability (no mode collapse, meaningful learning curves). A typical trap is to claim that WGAN eliminates the need for a discriminator — it merely replaces it with a critic that is not a probability estimator.

Optimal Transport on Graphs and Discrete Metric Spaces

Many modern data sets — social networks, molecular graphs, point clouds — live on discrete metric spaces where the ground cost is the shortest‑path distance on a graph. The Kantorovich problem on a graph with n vertices becomes a linear program with n² variables, but the cost matrix inherits sparsity from the graph structure: c(i,j) = d_G(i,j) is zero only on the diagonal, yet the optimal plan often concentrates on a small number of edges. Specialized algorithms exploit this. The Earth Mover's Distance on a tree can be computed in O(n log n) by a bottom‑up aggregation of mass excesses, because the unique path between any two vertices forces the transport plan to follow the tree edges. For general graphs, the minimum‑cost flow formulation with capacities equal to the vertex masses reduces to a network simplex on a graph with O(m) arcs (m = number of edges), yielding near‑linear performance on sparse graphs. The Gromov–Wasserstein distance extends OT to compare metric measure spaces without a common ground metric: it minimizes ∑i,j,k,l |d_X(i,k) − d_Y(j,l)|² πij πkl over couplings π. This quadratic assignment problem is NP‑hard in general, but entropic regularization yields a differentiable objective that can be optimized by a Sinkhorn‑like fixed‑point iteration on the coupling tensor. Recent work on sliced Gromov–Wasserstein projects the graph distances onto random lines, turning the problem into a series of one‑dimensional OT problems that are solvable in O(n log n) each, providing a scalable approximation for graphs with millions of nodes. In the context of graph neural networks, the Wasserstein distance between node‑embedding distributions serves as a permutation‑invariant graph kernel, and the optimal transport plan can be used to define a soft alignment between graphs for tasks such as graph matching and molecular similarity. The discrete setting also clarifies the role of the Ma–Trudinger–Wang tensor: on a graph the cost is the geodesic distance, which fails the (A3w) condition at branching points, explaining why optimal transport maps on graphs can be discontinuous even for convex costs.

Stability and Sensitivity of Optimal Transport Plans

Understanding how the optimal transport plan π* changes when the marginals μ, ν are perturbed is crucial for statistical inference, robustness certification, and the design of differentiable OT layers. The classical stability result (Rüschendorf, 1985) states that if μ_n → μ and ν_n → ν weakly, then any limit point of optimal plans π_n* is an optimal plan for (μ, ν). Quantitative versions bound the Wasserstein distance between optimal plans in terms of the perturbation of the marginals. For the quadratic cost on ℝ^d, if μ, ν have densities bounded away from zero and infinity on convex domains, the optimal map T = ∇φ is the gradient of a convex potential solving the Monge–Ampère equation det D²φ = f/g∘∇φ. Caffarelli's regularity theory implies that φ is C¹,α, and the map T is Hölder continuous. Perturbing the densities in L^∞ by δ yields a change in the potential bounded by C δα/(d+α) in C¹ norm, which translates into a Wasserstein‑2 stability estimate W₂(π*, π̃*) ≤ C' δβ. For entropically regularized OT, the Sinkhorn potentials (u,v) satisfy a system of nonlinear equations that is strongly convex in the Hilbert metric; the contraction factor is tanh(ε/4) < 1, giving exponential sensitivity decay with respect to ε. This explains why small ε leads to ill‑conditioned gradients in back‑propagation through Sinkhorn layers. In the discrete setting, the optimal plan is a basic feasible solution of a linear program; its sensitivity is governed by the condition number of the basis matrix. When the cost matrix satisfies the Monge property (c(i,j)+c(i',j') ≤ c(i,j')+c(i',j) for i

Mean Field Games and Optimal Transport

Mean field games (MFG) describe the limit of N‑player stochastic differential games as N → ∞, where each agent interacts with the empirical distribution of the population. The MFG system couples a Hamilton–Jacobi–Bellman (HJB) equation for the value function u(t,x) with a Fokker–Planck (FP) equation for the density m(t,x). Lasry and Lions observed that when the Hamiltonian is quadratic in the momentum, the HJB equation becomes the viscous Hamilton–Jacobi equation ∂_t u + ½|∇u|² = V(x,m) + νΔu, and the FP equation is ∂_t m − ∇·(m∇u) = νΔm. This pair is precisely the optimality system for the Benamou–Brenier dynamic formulation of the Wasserstein‑2 distance with an additional potential V. In the deterministic limit ν → 0, the MFG reduces to a pressureless Euler equation whose solutions are displacement interpolations in Wasserstein space. The master equation, an infinite‑dimensional PDE on the space of probability measures, encodes the value function as a function of the initial distribution; its well‑posedness in the class of displacement‑convex Hamiltonians was established by Cardaliaguet, Delarue, Lasry, and Lions using the monotonicity method. Numerically, the MFG system can be solved by a primal‑dual algorithm that alternates between a forward FP step (transport of the density) and a backward HJB step (update of the value function), which is exactly the JKO scheme for the Wasserstein gradient flow of the MFG energy. This connection has spawned a family of algorithms for crowd motion, pedestrian dynamics, and financial systemic risk where the cost functional includes a congestion term that penalizes high density — a term that is displacement convex and therefore amenable to the Otto calculus. In the learning‑for‑games paradigm, the MFG equilibrium can be approximated by a neural network representing the policy, trained via a Wasserstein‑gradient descent on the space of policies, linking optimal transport, mean field control, and reinforcement learning.

  • Assuming the Sinkhorn algorithm converges for any ε without checking the scaling of the cost matrix — large costs cause numerical underflow and require log‑domain stabilization.
  • Confusing the Wasserstein‑1 critic in WGAN with a probability discriminator — the critic outputs a real‑valued score, not a probability, and its optimal value equals the Wasserstein distance.
  • Believing that Gromov–Wasserstein distance is a metric on the space of all metric measure spaces — it is only a pseudo‑metric; isometric spaces have zero distance but non‑isometric spaces can also have zero distance if they are measure‑preservingly isomorphic.
  • Overlooking the role of the Monge property in discrete OT — without it the optimal plan need not be monotone and the linear program can have exponentially many basic feasible solutions.
  • Thinking that the MFG master equation is a standard PDE on ℝ^d — it lives on the infinite‑dimensional Wasserstein space and its derivatives are functional derivatives with respect to the measure argument.
  • Claiming that entropic regularization always improves stability — for very small ε the Sinkhorn iterations become ill‑conditioned and the gradient of the regularized OT loss explodes, harming back‑propagation.
  • Neglecting the dimensional correction in the CD(K,N) condition when applying synthetic Ricci bounds to graphs — the parameter N controls volume growth and must be chosen according to the graph's intrinsic dimension.

These traps highlight the subtle points that examiners often test.

Kantorovich Duality and the Structure of Optimal Plans

The Kantorovich formulation replaces Monge’s deterministic map by a transport plan π on the product space X×Y. The primal problem minimizes ∫ c(x,y) dπ(x,y) over all couplings with marginals μ and ν. Its dual maximizes ∫ φ dμ + ∫ ψ dν over pairs (φ,ψ) of measurable functions satisfying φ(x)+ψ(y) ≤ c(x,y). The duality theorem, proved by Kantorovich and later refined by Kellerer, asserts that the infimum of the primal equals the supremum of the dual whenever the cost c is lower semicontinuous and the marginals are tight. Moreover, if the infimum is finite, an optimal plan exists and any optimal pair (φ,ψ) can be chosen c‑concave: φ = ψ^c, ψ = φ^c where the c‑transform is defined by φ^c(y)=inf_x [c(x,y)-φ(x)].

A central structural result is that every optimal plan is concentrated on a c‑cyclically monotone set. A set Γ⊂X×Y is c‑cyclically monotone if for any finite collection (x_i,y_i)∈Γ and any permutation σ, Σ c(x_i,y_i) ≤ Σ c(x_i,yσ(i)). Rockafellar’s theorem for convex costs extends to general lower semicontinuous costs: the support of an optimal plan is contained in the c‑subdifferential ∂^c φ of a c‑concave potential φ. When c(x,y)=|x-y|2/2 on ℝ^d, c‑concavity coincides with ordinary convexity after the quadratic transform, and ∂^c φ becomes the usual subdifferential of a convex function. This yields Brenier’s theorem: the optimal plan is induced by the gradient map ∇φ where φ is convex.

In practice, the dual formulation is the basis for numerical algorithms. The Sinkhorn algorithm solves the entropically regularized dual by alternating updates of the scaling vectors, which correspond to the c‑transforms of the potentials. For unregularized problems, the dual can be tackled by semi‑discrete methods where one marginal is discrete; the optimal potentials then satisfy a system of nonlinear equations that can be solved by Newton‑type iterations. The duality gap provides a certificate of optimality: if a feasible plan π and dual pair (φ,ψ) satisfy ∫ c dπ = ∫ φ dμ + ∫ ψ dν, then both are optimal. This certificate is used in verification of computed transport plans and in the analysis of approximation schemes such as the multi‑scale approach of Schmitzer.

Examiners often test the distinction between c‑cyclical monotonicity and ordinary cyclic monotonicity, the role of lower semicontinuity in guaranteeing attainment, and the fact that the dual supremum may not be attained if the cost is not continuous. A typical trap is to assume that any feasible dual pair gives a lower bound; the inequality φ(x)+ψ(y) ≤ c(x,y) must hold everywhere, not just μ⊗ν‑almost everywhere, for the duality theorem to apply without additional regularity assumptions.

Displacement Interpolation and McConvexity on Riemannian Manifolds

McCann’s displacement interpolation constructs a constant‑speed geodesic (μ_t)t∈[0,1] in the Wasserstein space P₂(M) over a Riemannian manifold (M,g). Given two absolutely continuous measures μ₀, μ₁ with densities ρ₀, ρ₁, let T be the optimal transport map for the quadratic cost c(x,y)=d²(x,y)/2, where d is the Riemannian distance. The interpolation is defined by μ_t = (T_t)_# μ₀ with T_t(x)=exp_x(t ∇φ(x)) where φ is the Kantorovich potential. This curve satisfies the continuity equation ∂_t μ_t + ∇·(μ_t v_t)=0 with velocity field v_t = ∇φ_t, and it is a geodesic for the Wasserstein distance W₂.

A functional F on P₂(M) is displacement convex if t↦F(μ_t) is convex for every displacement interpolation. McCann identified three fundamental classes: internal energy U(μ)=∫ U(ρ) dx with U convex and U(0)=0; potential energy V(μ)=∫ V dμ with V convex along geodesics; and interaction energy W(μ)=½∫∫ W(x,y) dμ(x)dμ(y) with W convex in the sense of displacement. On a manifold with Ricci curvature bounded below by K, the Boltzmann entropy Ent(μ)=∫ ρ log ρ dx is K‑displacement convex: Ent(μ_t) ≤ (1-t)Ent(μ₀)+t Ent(μ₁) - K/2 t(1-t) W₂²(μ₀,μ₁). This inequality is equivalent to the curvature‑dimension condition CD(K,∞) of Lott–Sturm–Villani.

The displacement convexity of entropy underlies the gradient flow interpretation of the heat equation: the solution of ∂_t ρ = Δρ is the Wasserstein gradient flow of Ent. Otto’s formal calculus identifies the tangent space at μ with the space of gradients of functions modulo constants, equipped with the metric ⟨∇ψ,∇φ⟩_μ = ∫ ∇ψ·∇φ dμ. The gradient of Ent is ∇(δEnt/δρ)=∇(log ρ+1), and the gradient flow equation ∂_t μ + ∇·(μ ∇(δEnt/δρ))=0 recovers the heat equation. On manifolds with Ricci ≥ K, the flow is contractive in W₂ with rate e-Kt, a fact proved rigorously by von Renesse and Sturm using the Evolution Variational Inequality (EVI) formulation.

Examiners may ask to verify displacement convexity of a given functional, to derive the EVI for the heat flow, or to explain why the quadratic cost on a manifold requires the cut‑locus to be negligible for the Brenier map to be well defined. A common mistake is to confuse displacement convexity with ordinary convexity of the density functional; the former involves the geometry of the underlying space through the transport map, while the latter is a pointwise property of the integrand.

Technique
Show that the functional F(μ)=∫ ρ^m dx with m>1 is displacement convex on ℝ^d for the quadratic cost, and compute its displacement convexity constant.
Let μ_t be the displacement interpolation between μ₀, μ₁ with densities ρ₀, ρ₁. By the change‑of‑variables formula ρ_t(T_t(x)) det DT_t(x) = ρ₀(x). For the quadratic cost on ℝ^d, T_t(x) = (1-t)x + t∇φ(x) and DT_t = (1-t)I + t D²φ. Using the arithmetic‑geometric mean inequality, det DT_t ≥ (1-t)^d det(I)1-t det(D²φ)^t = (1-t)^d (ρ₀/ρ₁∘∇φ)^t. Then ρ_t(T_t) = ρ₀ / det DT_t ≤ ρ₀ / [(1-t)^d (ρ₀/ρ₁∘∇φ)^t] = (1-t)-d ρ₀1-t (ρ₁∘∇φ)^t. Raising to power m and integrating yields ∫ ρ_t^m ≤ (1-t)-d(m-1) ∫ ρ₀m(1-t) ρ₁mt ∘∇φ. By Hölder’s inequality with exponents 1/(1-t) and 1/t, the right side is bounded by (1-t)-d(m-1) (∫ ρ₀^m)1-t (∫ ρ₁^m)^t. Taking logs gives log F(μ_t) ≤ (1-t) log F(μ₀) + t log F(μ₁) - d(m-1) log(1-t). The extra term shows that F is not displacement convex in the strict sense unless m=1, but the functional G(μ)=∫ ρ^m / m-1 is displacement convex with constant 0 after a suitable normalization. The precise displacement convexity constant for the internal energy U_m(ρ)=ρ^m/(m-1) is K=0 on ℝ^d.
Exam‑day strategy
When a question asks for displacement convexity, first identify the functional class (internal, potential, interaction). Write the interpolation formula, apply the change‑of‑variables, and use the determinant bound det((1-t)I+t D²φ) ≥ (1-t)^d (det D²φ)^t. Then invoke Hölder or Prékopa–Leindler to separate the integrals. Remember that on a manifold with Ricci ≥ K the entropy gains a K‑term; on ℝ^d the constant is zero.

Schrödinger Bridges and Entropic Optimal Transport

The Schrödinger bridge problem, introduced by Erwin Schrödinger in 1931, asks for the most likely evolution of a diffusion process between two prescribed marginal distributions μ₀ and μ₁ over a fixed time horizon T. In modern language, it is the entropy‑regularized optimal transport problem where the reference measure is the law of a Brownian motion (or more generally a reversible diffusion) on the state space. The static formulation minimizes the relative entropy H(π|R) over couplings π with marginals μ₀, μ₁, where R is the joint law of the reference process at times 0 and T. The dynamic formulation minimizes the relative entropy of the path measure with respect to the reference diffusion, subject to the marginal constraints. Both formulations are equivalent by the disintegration theorem.

The solution of the static problem has the form dπ*(x,y) = f(x) g(y) dR(x,y) where f,g are positive measurable functions determined by the marginal constraints: ∫ g(y) dR(x,y) = 1/f(x) and ∫ f(x) dR(x,y) = 1/g(y). This is precisely the Schrödinger system, which can be solved by the iterative proportional fitting procedure (IPFP), also known as the Sinkhorn algorithm when R is a product measure. For a general reference kernel R, the updates become fk+1(x) = 1/∫ g_k(y) dR(x,y) and gk+1(y) = 1/∫ fk+1(x) dR(x,y). Convergence is guaranteed under mild conditions (e.g., R has full support and the marginals are absolutely continuous with respect to the marginals of R).

In the limit of vanishing noise (the diffusion coefficient ε→0), the Schrödinger bridge converges to the deterministic optimal transport plan for the cost c(x,y) = infγ:γ(0)=x,γ(T)=y ∫0^T L(γ,γ̇) dt, where L is the Lagrangian of the reference diffusion. This large‑deviation principle, proved by Léonard, connects entropic regularization to the classical Monge–Kantorovich problem. Conversely, for fixed ε>0, the entropic regularization yields a strictly convex dual problem whose solution provides a smooth approximation of the Kantorovich potentials. The regularized transport cost is differentiable with respect to the marginals, and its gradient is given by the optimal potentials, a property exploited in gradient‑based learning of generative models.

Applications include trajectory inference in single‑cell genomics, where the reference process models gene expression dynamics, and the marginals are observed snapshots at different time points. In machine learning, the Schrödinger bridge provides a principled way to interpolate between distributions while preserving the geometry of the underlying diffusion, leading to improved sample quality in diffusion‑based generative models. Examiners may test the equivalence of static and dynamic formulations, the form of the optimal coupling, the IPFP updates, and the large‑deviation limit. A frequent trap is to confuse the Schrödinger bridge with the entropic optimal transport where the reference measure is the product μ⊗ν; the former uses a dynamical reference (heat kernel), the latter a static product reference.

Sliced Wasserstein Distances and High‑Dimensional Approximation

The sliced Wasserstein distance SW_p(μ,ν) between probability measures on ℝ^d is defined by integrating the one‑dimensional Wasserstein distance over all directions in the unit sphere Sd-1: SW_p^p(μ,ν) = ∫Sd-1 W_p^p(θ_# μ, θ_# ν) dσ(θ), where θ_# denotes the pushforward by the linear map x↦⟨θ,x⟩ and σ is the uniform measure on the sphere. This definition leverages the fact that in one dimension the optimal transport map for any convex cost is the monotone rearrangement given by the quantile functions, which can be computed in O(n log n) time for empirical measures. By contrast, the exact Wasserstein distance in dimension d≥2 requires solving a linear program with O(n3) complexity.

The sliced distance is a metric on P_p(ℝ^d) and metrizes the same weak topology as W_p. Moreover, it satisfies the inequality SW_p(μ,ν) ≤ W_p(μ,ν) because projecting onto a line is a 1‑Lipschitz map. The reverse inequality holds up to a dimension‑dependent constant: W_p(μ,ν) ≤ C_d SW_p(μ,ν) for measures with bounded support, where C_d grows like √d. This makes sliced Wasserstein a practical proxy in high dimensions. Monte Carlo approximation replaces the integral over the sphere by an average over L random directions θ1,…,θ_L drawn uniformly. The empirical sliced distance converges almost surely to the true sliced distance as L→∞, with a rate O(L-1/2) by standard concentration inequalities.

Several variants improve statistical efficiency. The max‑sliced Wasserstein distance replaces the average by a supremum over directions, yielding a tighter upper bound on W_p. The generalized sliced Wasserstein distance uses non‑linear projections (e.g., via a neural network) to capture more structure. In generative modeling, sliced Wasserstein generators minimize the sliced distance between the model distribution and the data distribution; the gradient with respect to the generator parameters can be back‑propagated through the sorting operation using the differentiable sorting network or the quantile function representation. This avoids the critic network required in WGANs and provides a more stable training signal.

Examiners often ask to derive the one‑dimensional optimal transport map (the monotone rearrangement), to prove that SW_p is a metric, or to bound the Monte Carlo error. A common mistake is to assume that the sliced distance equals the Wasserstein distance for Gaussian measures; in fact, for centered Gaussians with covariances Σ₀, Σ₁, the sliced distance involves the average of the one‑dimensional Wasserstein distances of the projected Gaussians, which can be computed in closed form but does not simplify to the Bures–Wasserstein distance unless the covariances commute.

Wasserstein Barycenters and Multi‑Measure Averaging

Given probability measures μ1,…,μ_K on a metric space (X,d) and weights λ_i>0 with Σ λ_i=1, a Wasserstein barycenter is a minimizer of the functional μ ↦ Σ λ_i W_p^p(μ, μ_i). For p=2 on ℝ^d with quadratic cost, the barycenter problem is equivalent to finding a measure whose optimal transport maps to the inputs satisfy Σ λ_i T_i = Id, where T_i is the optimal map from the barycenter to μ_i. This characterization follows from the first‑order optimality condition in the Wasserstein space: the gradient of the objective at μ is Σ λ_i ∇φ_i, where φ_i are the Kantorovich potentials for the transport from μ to μ_i. Setting the gradient to zero yields the fixed‑point equation.

Existence and uniqueness of barycenters hold under mild conditions: if the input measures are absolutely continuous with respect to Lebesgue measure, the barycenter is unique and also absolutely continuous. For discrete measures, the barycenter may not be unique; the set of minimizers forms a convex polytope in the space of transport plans. The problem can be formulated as a multi‑marginal optimal transport problem on the product space XK+1 with cost Σ λ_i d^p(x0,x_i). This formulation reveals that the barycenter is the first marginal of an optimal multi‑marginal plan, and it enables the use of entropic regularization and the Sinkhorn algorithm for computation. The regularized barycenter problem minimizes Σ λ_i OT_ε(μ, μ_i) where OT_ε is the entropic OT cost; the optimal regularized barycenter has a density of the form exp(Σ λ_i f_i/ε) with respect to the reference measure, where f_i are the Sinkhorn potentials.

Algorithms for barycenters include the fixed‑point iteration μ(k+1) = (Σ λ_i T_i(k))_# μ(k) where T_i(k) are optimal maps from the current estimate to μ_i. This iteration converges linearly under strong convexity assumptions. An alternative is the iterative Bregman projection (IBP) algorithm, which alternates projections onto the marginal constraints in the multi‑marginal formulation; it is equivalent to the Sinkhorn algorithm for the regularized problem and enjoys a geometric convergence rate in the Hilbert metric. In practice, the sliced Wasserstein barycenter replaces W_p by SW_p, reducing each iteration to one‑dimensional quantile averaging, which is extremely fast and scales to millions of samples.

Applications span histogram averaging in image processing, domain adaptation where the barycenter of source and target distributions serves as a common reference, and Bayesian inference where the Wasserstein barycenter of posterior samples from distributed data provides a consensus posterior. Examiners may test the optimality condition Σ λ_i T_i = Id, the equivalence to multi‑marginal OT, the effect of entropic regularization, and the computational complexity of sliced versus exact barycenters. A typical trap is to assume that the barycenter of Gaussian measures is Gaussian with covariance equal to the weighted arithmetic mean of covariances; the correct formula involves the matrix geometric mean: Σ = (Σ1/2 (Σ_i) Σ1/2)1/2 solved by a fixed‑point equation.

  • Confusing the Kantorovich dual with the Monge formulation — the dual provides a lower bound but requires c‑concave potentials; the primal may have no Monge map if the marginals are not absolutely continuous.
  • Assuming displacement convexity of entropy holds on any metric space — it is equivalent to the CD(K,∞) condition, which fails on spaces with negative Ricci curvature.
  • Treating the Schrödinger bridge as identical to entropic OT with product reference — the reference kernel is the heat kernel, not the product measure, leading to different scaling limits.
  • Believing sliced Wasserstein equals Wasserstein for all distributions — it is only an upper bound; equality holds only for measures supported on a line.
  • Thinking the Wasserstein barycenter of Gaussians is the arithmetic mean of covariances — the correct barycenter covariance solves a nonlinear matrix equation involving the geometric mean.
  • Neglecting the role of the cut‑locus in Brenier maps on manifolds — the optimal map may be multivalued on the cut‑locus, requiring a measurable selection.
  • Overlooking the need for lower semicontinuity of the cost in the duality theorem — without it the dual supremum may not be attained even if the primal has a solution.

Quantitative Stability of Optimal Transport Maps under Measure Perturbations

Stability theory quantifies how much an optimal transport map changes when the source or target measure is perturbed. For quadratic cost on ℝⁿ, Brenier’s theorem guarantees a unique gradient map ∇φ pushing μ to ν. If μₖ → μ and νₖ → ν narrowly, the corresponding potentials φₖ converge uniformly on compact sets to φ, and the maps ∇φₖ converge in L²(μ) to ∇φ. The rate of convergence can be expressed in terms of the Wasserstein distance between the perturbed and original measures: W₂(μₖ,μ) + W₂(νₖ,ν) controls the L² error of the maps. A sharp estimate due to Figalli and Gigli states that for absolutely continuous measures with densities bounded away from zero and infinity on convex domains, ‖∇φₖ − ∇φ‖L²(μ) ≤ C (W₂(μₖ,μ) + W₂(νₖ,ν))1/2. The exponent 1/2 is optimal; counterexamples with densities vanishing at the boundary show that no linear rate holds in general. When the cost is not quadratic but satisfies the Ma–Trudinger–Wang condition, stability still holds but the exponent depends on the cost’s regularity. In practice, this theory underpins the convergence analysis of sample‑based approximations: empirical measures μₙ = (1/n)∑ δX_i satisfy W₂(μₙ,μ) = Oₚ(n-1/2) in dimension ≤ 3, leading to a map error of order n-1/4. Examiners often test the distinction between narrow convergence and Wasserstein convergence, and the necessity of density bounds for polynomial rates.

Optimal Transport on Manifolds with Boundary and Reflection Couplings

On a compact Riemannian manifold (M,g) with smooth boundary ∂M, the quadratic cost c(x,y)=½ d²(x,y) is no longer globally smooth because geodesics may hit the boundary. The Monge problem remains well‑posed if one restricts to measures that charge no mass to the cut‑locus of the boundary. The optimal map is then given by the gradient of a c‑concave potential that satisfies a Neumann‑type boundary condition: the transport rays reflect off ∂M according to the law of geometric optics. This reflection coupling was introduced by Figalli and Rifford to prove regularity up to the boundary. The associated Monge–Ampère equation becomes a boundary value problem of the form det(D²φ − D²xc(x,∇φ)) = f(x)/g(∇φ) with the oblique derivative condition ⟨ν, ∇φ − x⟩ = 0 on ∂M, where ν is the outward normal. Existence of a C¹,α solution requires the domain to be uniformly c‑convex, a condition that generalizes convexity in Euclidean space. If the domain fails c‑convexity, the optimal map may develop singularities at the boundary, and the Kantorovich dual may have a gap. In applications to shape matching, one often works with the squared geodesic distance on surfaces with boundary; the reflection coupling provides a natural way to transport mass across the boundary without leaving the surface. Examiners may ask to identify the correct boundary condition for the potential or to explain why c‑convexity is necessary for regularity.

Worked Example: Stability Estimate for Empirical Measures
Let μ be the uniform measure on the unit square [0,1]² and let μₙ be the empirical measure of n i.i.d. samples from μ. Using the known bound W₂(μₙ,μ) ≤ C n-1/2 (with high probability) and the Figalli–Gigli stability estimate, derive the convergence rate of the optimal transport map Tₙ = ∇φₙ pushing μₙ to ν = μ.
The quadratic cost satisfies the Ma–Trudinger–Wang condition on the convex domain. The densities of μ and ν are bounded above and below by 1. By the Figalli–Gigli theorem, ‖Tₙ − Id‖L²(μ) ≤ C (W₂(μₙ,μ) + W₂(ν,ν))1/2 = C W₂(μₙ,μ)1/2. Substituting the empirical bound gives ‖Tₙ − Id‖L²(μ) ≤ C' n-1/4 with high probability. This rate is sharp for the uniform distribution on a square.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks for a convergence rate of optimal maps, first identify the cost regularity (quadratic, MTW condition) and the density bounds. Then recall the exponent 1/2 for quadratic cost on convex domains with bounded densities. If the domain is non‑convex or densities vanish, state that only Hölder‑type rates with smaller exponents can be guaranteed.

Wasserstein Gradient Flows for Nonconvex Interaction Energies

The Jordan–Kinderlehrer–Otto (JKO) scheme constructs gradient flows of functionals F on the Wasserstein space (P₂(ℝⁿ),W₂). For convex F, the minimizing movement scheme yields a unique curve of maximal slope. When F contains a nonconvex interaction term, e.g. F(ρ) = ∫ Vρ + ½ ∫∫ W(x−y) ρ(dx)ρ(dy) with a nonconvex potential W, the JKO scheme still produces a discrete sequence, but the limit curve may not be a gradient flow in the classical sense. Ambrosio, Gigli and Savaré showed that if W is λ‑convex (i.e. W + λ|x|²/2 is convex), the functional is λ‑geodesically convex and the flow is well‑posed. For genuinely nonconvex W, one can still obtain a curve of maximal slope provided the functional satisfies a one‑sided Lipschitz condition on sublevel sets. The resulting evolution equation is the nonlocal continuity equation ∂ₜρ + ∇·(ρ ∇(V + W*ρ)) = 0. This equation appears in models of collective behavior (swarming, opinion dynamics). Numerical experiments reveal that nonconvexity can cause finite‑time blow‑up or pattern formation, phenomena absent in the convex case. Examiners often test the distinction between λ‑convexity and full convexity, and the role of the interaction potential’s Fourier transform in determining stability of homogeneous states.

Optimal Transport in Generative Adversarial Networks: Training Dynamics and Mode Collapse

Wasserstein GANs (WGANs) replace the Jensen–Shannon divergence with the Kantorovich–Rubinstein dual formulation of W₁. The critic (discriminator) approximates a 1‑Lipschitz function f, and the generator minimizes 𝔼[f(G(z))] − 𝔼[f(x)]. In practice, the Lipschitz constraint is enforced by weight clipping, gradient penalty, or spectral normalization. The training dynamics can be analyzed as a discrete‑time approximation of a Wasserstein gradient flow on the generator’s parameter space. Recent work shows that if the critic is optimal at each step, the generator follows the steepest descent of W₁(μ_θ, ν) with respect to the parameter metric induced by the pushforward map. However, the critic is never fully optimal; the resulting bias leads to a modified vector field that may fail to converge to the true data distribution. Mode collapse corresponds to the generator’s pushforward measure concentrating on a lower‑dimensional subset, which makes the Wasserstein distance insensitive to missing modes because W₁ metrizes weak convergence plus first moments. Adding an entropic regularization (Sinkhorn loss) yields a smoother landscape and mitigates collapse, but introduces a bias that vanishes only as the regularization parameter ε → 0. Examiners may ask to derive the gradient of the Sinkhorn loss with respect to generator parameters or to explain why W₁ alone cannot detect missing modes when the support dimension drops.

Computational Complexity and Approximation Algorithms for Exact Optimal Transport

The exact optimal transport problem between two discrete measures with n atoms each is a linear program with n² variables and 2n constraints. The best known strongly polynomial algorithms (e.g. network simplex) run in O(n³ log n) time in practice, but the theoretical worst‑case complexity of linear programming is O(n3.5) using interior‑point methods. For large n, this is prohibitive. Approximation algorithms exploit the fact that the cost matrix often has low‑rank structure or satisfies a metric property. The Sinkhorn algorithm solves the entropically regularized problem in O(n²/ε) iterations, each costing O(n²) for dense costs, yielding an ε‑approximate transport plan in the sense of the regularized objective. For unregularized OT, the Greenkhorn algorithm (coordinate ascent on the dual) achieves a (1+δ)‑approximation in O(n² δ-2) time. When the cost is the squared Euclidean distance, the problem can be reduced to a minimum‑cost flow on a geometric graph using the fact that optimal transport maps are monotone in one dimension; in higher dimensions, the problem is equivalent to a multi‑commodity flow and remains NP‑hard for general costs. Recent breakthroughs show that for costs satisfying the Monge property (e.g. squared Euclidean distance on a grid), the assignment problem can be solved in O(n²) using the auction algorithm. Examiners frequently test the distinction between exact and regularized complexity, the role of the Monge property in enabling faster algorithms, and the fact that Sinkhorn’s iteration count scales as 1/ε rather than log(1/ε).

  • Assuming the Figalli–Gigli stability exponent 1/2 holds without density bounds — it fails when densities vanish at the boundary.
  • Confusing the Neumann boundary condition for the Monge–Ampère equation on manifolds with boundary with the Dirichlet condition — the correct condition is oblique derivative reflecting the transport rays.
  • Believing that any nonconvex interaction energy yields a well‑posed Wasserstein gradient flow — λ‑convexity or a one‑sided Lipschitz condition is required.
  • Thinking that the Wasserstein‑1 loss in WGANs detects all modes — W₁ metrizes weak convergence, so missing modes on a lower‑dimensional set may not increase the loss.
  • Assuming Sinkhorn’s algorithm converges in log(1/ε) iterations — the iteration complexity is O(1/ε) for a fixed regularization parameter.
  • Overlooking that the exact discrete OT problem is NP‑hard for general cost matrices — polynomial‑time algorithms exist only for special costs such as Monge or metric costs.
  • Review these traps before the exam to avoid common misconceptions.

Talagrand’s Transportation Cost Inequality and Concentration of Measure

Talagrand’s inequality links the quadratic Wasserstein distance W₂ to the relative entropy with respect to a reference measure satisfying a log‑Sobolev inequality. If a probability measure μ on ℝⁿ satisfies a logarithmic Sobolev inequality with constant C > 0, then for every probability measure ν absolutely continuous with respect to μ one has

W₂(ν, μ)² ≤ 2C Ent(ν | μ),

where Ent(ν | μ) = ∫ log(dν/dμ) dν is the Kullback–Leibler divergence. The inequality was proved by Michel Talagrand in 1996 using a martingale argument on product spaces and later extended to Riemannian manifolds with non‑negative Ricci curvature by Otto and Villani (2000). The constant 2C is sharp for the standard Gaussian measure, for which C = 1/2 and the inequality becomes W₂² ≤ Ent.

The transportation‑cost inequality is a powerful tool for concentration of measure. By the dual formulation of W₂, the inequality implies that any 1‑Lipschitz function f satisfies a sub‑Gaussian tail bound under μ: μ(|f − ∫f dμ| ≥ t) ≤ 2 exp(−t² / (4C)). This recovers the classical Gaussian concentration and extends it to any measure satisfying a log‑Sobolev inequality, including the exponential measure on ℝ and the uniform measure on the sphere. In the context of optimal transport, Talagrand’s inequality provides a quantitative version of the fact that the heat flow is the gradient flow of the entropy in Wasserstein space: the entropy decays along the heat flow at a rate controlled by the Fisher information, and the inequality integrates this differential estimate to a global bound.

Examiners often test the equivalence between the log‑Sobolev inequality, the Talagrand inequality, and the HWI inequality (see next section). A typical question asks to derive the Gaussian isoperimetric inequality from Talagrand’s inequality by applying it to the indicator of a half‑space approximated by smooth functions. Another common trap is to confuse the constant in Talagrand’s inequality with the Poincaré constant; the log‑Sobolev constant is always smaller or equal to the Poincaré constant, and the factor 2 in front of C is essential for sharpness.

The HWI Inequality and Its Role in Functional Analysis

The HWI inequality, named after its three components — entropy (H), Wasserstein distance (W), and Fisher information (I) — was introduced by Otto and Villani in 2000 as an interpolation between the logarithmic Sobolev inequality and Talagrand’s inequality. For a probability measure μ = e−V dx on ℝⁿ with V ∈ C² and Hess V ≥ K Id (in the sense of quadratic forms), the inequality reads

Ent(ν | μ) ≤ W₂(ν, μ) √I(ν | μ) − (K/2) W₂(ν, μ)²,

where I(ν | μ) = ∫ |∇ log(dν/dμ)|² dν is the relative Fisher information. When K = 0 the inequality reduces to Ent ≤ W₂ √I, which by Young’s inequality yields Talagrand’s inequality with constant 2/K when K > 0. Conversely, applying the HWI inequality to the heat flow at time t and optimizing over t recovers the logarithmic Sobolev inequality with constant 1/K.

The proof uses the displacement convexity of the entropy along Wasserstein geodesics (McCann’s theorem) and the Benamou–Brenier dynamic formulation. One considers the geodesic (μs)s∈[0,1] connecting μ to ν and differentiates the entropy functional s ↦ Ent(μs | μ) at s = 0. The second derivative involves the Hessian of V and the Fisher information, leading to the stated bound after integration.
