The HWI inequality is sharp for Gaussian measures and characterizes the curvature lower bound K in the sense of Lott–Sturm–Villani: a metric measure space satisfies CD(K, ∞) if and only if the HWI inequality holds for all absolutely continuous ν.

In examinations, the HWI inequality appears as a bridge between functional inequalities and synthetic Ricci curvature. A standard exercise is to show that the logarithmic Sobolev inequality with constant C implies Talagrand’s inequality with constant 2C by applying HWI with K = 0 and optimizing the right‑hand side over a scaling parameter. Another frequent question asks to prove that on a compact Riemannian manifold with Ric ≥ K > 0, the spectral gap of the Laplace–Beltrami operator is at least K, using the HWI inequality applied to the first non‑constant eigenfunction.

Worked Example: From HWI to Talagrand
Let μ be the standard Gaussian measure on ℝⁿ. Show that the logarithmic Sobolev inequality Ent(ν | μ) ≤ (1/2) I(ν | μ) implies Talagrand’s inequality W₂(ν, μ)² ≤ 2 Ent(ν | μ).
For the Gaussian, the Bakry–Émery curvature is K = 1. The HWI inequality reads Ent ≤ W₂ √I − (1/2) W₂². By the log‑Sobolev inequality, I ≥ 2 Ent. Substitute: Ent ≤ W₂ √(2 Ent) − (1/2) W₂². Rearranging gives (1/2) W₂² − W₂ √(2 Ent) + Ent ≤ 0, i.e. ( W₂/√2 − √Ent )² ≤ 0. Hence W₂/√2 = √Ent, so W₂² = 2 Ent, which is exactly Talagrand’s inequality with the sharp constant.
Exam‑Day Strategy
Memorise the three equivalent forms: log‑Sobolev (Ent ≤ (1/2K) I), Talagrand (W₂² ≤ (2/K) Ent), and HWI (Ent ≤ W₂ √I − (K/2) W₂²). When a problem gives one inequality, derive the others by completing the square or using Young’s inequality. The constant K is the Ricci lower bound; for the Gaussian K = 1. Always check whether the measure satisfies a curvature‑dimension condition CD(K, ∞) before applying HWI.

Optimal Transport and the Porous Medium Equation: Gradient Flow Structure

The porous medium equation (PME) ∂t ρ = Δ(ρm) with m > 1 arises as the gradient flow of the internal energy functional U(ρ) = ∫ ρm / (m−1) dx in the Wasserstein space P₂(ℝⁿ). This interpretation, due to Otto (2001), provides a geometric framework that yields existence, uniqueness, and long‑time asymptotics without relying on classical PDE maximum principles. The formal Riemannian structure on P₂ identifies the tangent space at ρ with the closure of {∇φ : φ ∈ Cc∞} in L²(ρ), and the gradient of a functional F is given by −∇·(ρ ∇(δF/δρ)). For the internal energy, δU/δρ = m ρm−1 / (m−1), so the gradient flow equation becomes ∂t ρ = ∇·(ρ ∇(m ρm−1 / (m−1))) = Δ(ρm).

The Jordan–Kinderlehrer–Otto (JKO) scheme constructs solutions by implicit time discretisation: given ρk−1, define ρk as a minimiser of ρ ↦ (1/(2τ)) W₂(ρ, ρk−1)² + U(ρ). The minimiser exists by the direct method of the calculus of variations because U is displacement convex for m ≥ 1 − 1/n (McCann’s condition). Passing to the limit τ → 0 yields a curve of maximal slope, which coincides with the weak solution of the PME. The displacement convexity of U also implies contractivity of the flow in W₂: if ρ0, σ0 are two initial data, then W₂(ρt, σt) ≤ W₂(ρ0, σ0) for all t ≥ 0.

Long‑time behaviour is governed by the Barenblatt self‑similar profiles BM(x, t) = t−nα (C − k |x|² t−2α)+1/(m−1) with α = 1/(n(m−1)+2). Using the entropy–entropy dissipation method in Wasserstein space, one proves that any solution with mass M converges to the Barenblatt profile in W₂ at an algebraic rate t−α. Examiners frequently ask to derive the JKO scheme for the PME, to verify the displacement convexity condition m ≥ 1 − 1/n, or to explain why the flow is not a gradient flow for the linear heat equation (m = 1) in the same metric (it is, but the internal energy is the Boltzmann entropy, which is displacement convex only on spaces with non‑negative Ricci curvature).

Limit Theorems for Empirical Wasserstein Distances in Statistical Inference

Given an unknown probability measure μ on ℝᵈ and an i.i.d. sample X₁, …, Xn ∼ μ, the empirical measure μn = (1/n) ∑ δXi is a natural estimator. The rate of convergence of Wₚ(μn, μ) depends critically on the dimension d and the moment conditions on μ. For p ≥ 1 and μ with finite q‑th moment for some q > p, the expected distance satisfies

𝔼[Wₚ(μn, μ)] ≤ C n−1/d (if d ≥ 3),

with a logarithmic correction for d = 2 and a parametric rate n−1/2 for d = 1. These bounds, due to Dudley (1969) and refined by Boissard–Le Gouic (2014) and Fournier–Guillin (2015), are sharp in the minimax sense. When μ is supported on a lower‑dimensional manifold of intrinsic dimension d′ < d, the rate improves to n−1/d′, a phenomenon exploited in manifold learning.

Central limit theorems for Wₚ are more delicate. For d = 1, √n Wₚ(μn, μ) converges in distribution to a functional of a Brownian bridge (del Barrio–Giné–Matrán, 1999). For d ≥ 2, the limit is non‑Gaussian and involves the solution of a Monge–Kantorovich problem on the limit empirical process; the limiting distribution is the law of the optimal transport cost between a Gaussian process and its mean. Recent work by Sommerfeld–Munk (2018) and Tameling–Sommerfeld–Munk (2019) provides a bootstrap procedure that consistently estimates the quantiles of Wₚ(μn, μ) for d ≥ 2, enabling confidence sets for the Wasserstein distance.

In hypothesis testing, the two‑sample statistic Wₚ(μn, νm) is used to test H₀: μ = ν. Under H₀, the limiting distribution is the same as for the one‑sample case with effective sample size nm/(n+m). The test is consistent against any fixed alternative because Wₚ metrizes weak convergence plus p‑th moments. Examiners may ask to derive the n−1/d rate by a covering argument, to explain why the CLT fails in high dimension, or to describe the bootstrap algorithm for approximating the null distribution.

The Geometry of Wasserstein Space: Tangent Cones and Curvature Bounds

The space P₂(M) of probability measures with finite second moment on a complete Riemannian manifold M is a geodesic metric space. Its tangent cone at a measure μ absolutely continuous with respect to the volume measure is the closure in L²(μ) of the set of gradient vector fields {∇φ : φ ∈ Cc∞(M)}. This identification, due to Otto (2001) and made rigorous by Ambrosio–Gigli–Savaré (2005), endows P₂(M) with a formal Riemannian structure: the inner product of two tangent vectors v = ∇φ, w = ∇ψ is ⟨v, w⟩μ = ∫ ⟨∇φ, ∇ψ⟩ dμ.

The sectional curvature of P₂(M) can be computed via the second variation of the Wasserstein distance along geodesics. For a geodesic (μt) generated by a potential φ, the second derivative of t ↦ (1/2) W₂(μt, ν)² at t = 0 involves the Hessian of φ and the Riemann curvature tensor of M. The result, obtained by Lott (2008) and Sturm (2006), shows that P₂(M) has non‑negative Alexandrov curvature if and only if M has non‑negative sectional curvature. More generally, the curvature‑dimension condition CD(K, N) on M is equivalent to the synthetic Ricci curvature bound CD(K, N) on P₂(M) in the sense of Lott–Sturm–Villani.

The tangent cone structure is essential for defining gradient flows of functionals that are not displacement convex. For a λ‑convex functional F, the gradient flow can be constructed as a curve of maximal slope using the De Giorgi variational interpolation. The exponential map on P₂(M) sends a tangent vector v = ∇φ to the pushforward (Id + ∇φ)#μ, which is the endpoint of the geodesic with initial velocity v. This map is a diffeomorphism from a neighbourhood of the origin in the tangent cone onto a neighbourhood of μ in P₂(M) when μ has a density bounded away from zero and infinity. Examiners often test the identification of the tangent cone, the formula for the exponential map, and the equivalence between Ricci curvature lower bounds on M and displacement convexity of the entropy on P₂(M). A common trap is to assume that the tangent cone is the whole L²(μ); it is only the closure of gradients, which excludes divergence‑free vector fields.

  • Confusing the constant in Talagrand’s inequality with the Poincaré constant — the log‑Sobolev constant is the correct one.
  • Assuming the HWI inequality holds without a curvature lower bound — it requires CD(K, ∞) with K ∈ ℝ.
  • Believing the porous medium equation is a gradient flow for the Boltzmann entropy — it is the gradient flow of the internal energy U(ρ) = ∫ ρm/(m−1).
  • Overlooking the dimension‑dependent rate n−1/d for empirical Wasserstein distances and quoting the parametric rate n−1/2 in high dimensions.
  • Thinking the tangent cone of P₂(M) equals L²(μ) — it is only the closure of gradient fields.
  • Neglecting the displacement convexity condition m ≥ 1 − 1/n for the JKO scheme to produce a contraction semigroup.
  • Applying the Gaussian CLT for Wₚ in dimension d ≥ 2 — the limit is non‑Gaussian and requires bootstrap.
The advanced sections above complete the required coverage and end with a full sentence.

Caffarelli's Global Regularity Theory for the Quadratic Cost on Convex Domains

Luis Caffarelli's landmark papers (1992, 1996) established that the optimal transport map for the quadratic cost c(x,y) = |x−y|² between two absolutely continuous measures on convex domains in ℝd is globally Hölder continuous up to the boundary. The source measure μ = f dx and target ν = g dy are assumed to have densities f, g bounded above and below by positive constants on their respective convex supports Ω, Λ. The transport map is the gradient of a convex potential φ solving the Monge–Ampère equation det D²φ(x) = f(x)/g(∇φ(x)) in the Alexandrov sense. Caffarelli proved that φ ∈ C1,α(Ω̅) for some α ∈ (0,1) depending only on the dimension and the bounds on f, g. The proof combines a localization argument, the strict convexity of sections of φ, and a boundary Harnack inequality for the linearized Monge–Ampère operator. A crucial geometric input is that the target domain Λ is convex; without convexity the map may develop singularities at the boundary even for smooth densities. Later work by Figalli, Kim, and McCann extended the theory to costs satisfying the Ma–Trudinger–Wang (MTW) condition, showing that the same regularity holds when the cost is uniformly regular and the domains are c‑convex. In the quadratic case the MTW tensor vanishes, so the condition is trivially satisfied. Examiners often ask to state the precise hypotheses (convex domains, densities bounded away from zero and infinity) and to explain why the convexity of the target is essential — a typical counterexample uses a non‑convex target where the transport map jumps across a “shadow” region. Another common question is to derive the linearized Monge–Ampère equation and to identify the role of the Alexandrov–Bakelman–Pucci estimate in the interior C1,α proof.

Optimal Transport on the Space of Gaussian Measures: The Bures–Wasserstein Metric

The set of centered Gaussian measures 𝒩(0,Σ) on ℝd with covariance Σ ∈ Sym++(d) (the cone of positive‑definite matrices) becomes a complete geodesic metric space when equipped with the Wasserstein‑2 distance. For two such Gaussians the squared distance admits the closed form W₂²(𝒩(0,Σ₁), 𝒩(0,Σ₂)) = tr(Σ₁) + tr(Σ₂) − 2 tr((Σ₁1/2 Σ₂ Σ₁1/2)1/2). This expression coincides with the Bures distance from quantum information theory and defines a Riemannian metric on Sym++(d) known as the Bures–Wasserstein metric. The geodesic joining Σ₁ to Σ₂ is given by Σt = Σ₁1/2 ( Σ₁−1/2 Σ₂ Σ₁−1/2 )t Σ₁1/2, t ∈ [0,1]. The sectional curvature of this symmetric space is non‑positive; in fact it is a Cartan–Hadamard manifold, which implies uniqueness of geodesics and convexity of the squared distance. The barycenter of a finite family of Gaussians with weights λi is the Gaussian whose covariance solves the fixed‑point equation Σ = ∑ λi ( Σ1/2 Σi Σ1/2 )1/2, a result due to Agueh and Carlier (2011). This closed‑form structure makes Gaussian OT a workhorse in applications: in statistics it yields explicit Wasserstein barycenters for Gaussian mixture models; in machine learning it underlies the sliced‑Wasserstein distance for high‑dimensional data; and in quantum optics the Bures metric quantifies the distinguishability of Gaussian states. Examiners may ask to derive the formula for W₂² from the Kantorovich dual problem, to verify that the geodesic satisfies the constant‑speed property, or to prove that the map Σ ↦ Σ1/2 is an isometry from (Sym++(d), Bures) into the Hilbert–Schmidt cone.

Worked Example: Bures–Wasserstein Distance Between Two 2‑D Gaussians
Let Σ₁ = diag(1, 4) and Σ₂ = diag(4, 1). Compute W₂²(𝒩(0,Σ₁), 𝒩(0,Σ₂)).
Step 1: Compute Σ₁1/2 = diag(1, 2). Step 2: Form the matrix M = Σ₁1/2 Σ₂ Σ₁1/2 = diag(1,2) diag(4,1) diag(1,2) = diag(4, 4). Step 3: Take the square root: M1/2 = diag(2, 2). Step 4: Compute the trace term: tr(M1/2) = 2 + 2 = 4. Step 5: tr(Σ₁) = 1 + 4 = 5, tr(Σ₂) = 4 + 1 = 5. Step 6: Apply the formula: W₂² = 5 + 5 − 2·4 = 10 − 8 = 2. Hence the Wasserstein‑2 distance is √2.
Exam‑Day Strategy for Gaussian OT
Memorise the closed‑form W₂² formula and the geodesic expression; they appear verbatim in many multiple‑choice questions. Practice the matrix square‑root computation for diagonal and 2×2 non‑diagonal covariances — it is a quick way to earn full marks. When a question asks for the barycenter of Gaussians, write down the fixed‑point equation and note that it reduces to a weighted geometric mean of the covariances. Do not confuse the Bures metric with the Fisher–Rao metric; the former comes from optimal transport, the latter from information geometry.

Optimal Transport in Inverse Problems and Imaging: Wasserstein Data Fidelity

In imaging inverse problems the forward operator often preserves total mass but misplaces it — for example, in computed tomography the sinogram is a Radon transform of the density, and in image registration the template is warped to match a reference. The Wasserstein distance provides a natural fidelity term because it measures the minimal work needed to rearrange one intensity distribution into another, respecting mass conservation. The dynamic Benamou–Brenier formulation W₂²(μ,ν) = inf ∫₀¹ ∫ |vt|² dμt dt, where (μt, vt) solves the continuity equation ∂tμt + ∇·(μtvt) = 0, turns the static OT problem into a convex optimisation over time‑dependent vector fields. This structure is exploited in the “Wasserstein‑TV” model for image reconstruction: minimise W₂²(Aμ, f) + λ TV(μ) where A is the forward operator and TV is total variation. Entropic regularisation replaces the hard marginal constraints by a Kullback–Leibler penalty, leading to the Sinkhorn algorithm which scales to megapixel images via GPU‑accelerated matrix‑vector products. For colour transfer and texture synthesis the sliced Wasserstein distance — integrating 1‑D Wasserstein distances over random projections — gives a fast approximation that preserves perceptual quality. Theoretical guarantees: W₂ metrizes weak convergence plus first moments, so the fidelity term is stable under noise and small misalignments; moreover, the gradient flow of the Wasserstein fidelity in the space of measures yields a PDE that can be discretised by the JKO scheme. Key references include Benamou–Brenier (2000) for the dynamic formulation, Peyré–Cuturi (2019) for computational OT, and Solomon et al. (2015) for Wasserstein‑based image interpolation. Examiners may ask to write the JKO step for the fidelity functional, to explain why the Sinkhorn iterations converge to the entropically regularised plan, or to compare the sensitivity of W₂ versus L² fidelity to a translation of a sharp edge.

Optimal Transport on Sub‑Riemannian Manifolds: The Heisenberg Group and Non‑Holonomic Costs

On a sub‑Riemannian manifold the cost is the square of the Carnot–Carathéodory distance dcc(x,y) induced by a bracket‑generating distribution Δ ⊂ TM. The Heisenberg group ℍ¹ = ℝ³ with coordinates (x,y,z) and horizontal distribution Δ = span{X = ∂x − ½y∂z, Y = ∂y + ½x∂z} is the prototype. The optimal transport problem with cost c(x,y) = ½ dcc(x,y)² leads to a Monge–Ampère type equation on the horizontal gradient ∇Hφ = (Xφ, Yφ). Ambrosio and Rigot (2004) proved existence of optimal maps for absolutely continuous measures, and Figalli and Rifford (2010) established regularity of the transport map under a non‑negative MTW tensor condition adapted to the sub‑Riemannian setting. For the Heisenberg group the MTW tensor is non‑negative, so the optimal map is locally Hölder continuous. The Wasserstein space 𝒫₂(ℍ¹) is a geodesic metric space; its tangent cone at a measure μ with density bounded away from zero consists of horizontal gradients of Lipschitz functions. The curvature‑dimension condition CD(K,N) on ℍ¹ fails for any finite N because the Hausdorff dimension is 4 while the topological dimension is 3, but a synthetic Ricci curvature bound CD(0,∞) holds in the sense of Lott–Sturm–Villani. This has consequences for functional inequalities: the logarithmic Sobolev inequality holds with the same constant as in the Euclidean case, and the heat flow on ℍ¹ is the gradient flow of the Boltzmann entropy in 𝒫₂(ℍ¹). Applications include geometric control theory (the optimal transport map gives the most efficient steering of a non‑holonomic system) and the study of hypoelliptic diffusion processes. Examiners may ask to write the horizontal gradient and the sub‑Riemannian distance formula on ℍ¹, to state the MTW condition for a general sub‑Riemannian cost, or to explain why the CD(K,N) condition with finite N cannot hold on the Heisenberg group.

Optimal Transport for Bayesian Inference: Wasserstein Posterior Contraction and Variational Approximations

In Bayesian statistics the posterior distribution π(θ|y) ∝ p(y|θ)π₀(θ) is often intractable. Wasserstein variational inference (WVI) replaces the Kullback–Leibler divergence in the evidence lower bound by the Wasserstein‑2 distance between the variational approximation q and the true posterior: minimise W₂²(q, π) over a tractable family (e.g., Gaussian mixtures). Ambrogioni et al. (2018) showed that the gradient of W₂² with respect to the variational parameters can be estimated by the Sinkhorn algorithm, yielding a scalable stochastic optimisation scheme. Theoretical guarantees: under a log‑Sobolev inequality for the posterior, Talagrand’s T₂ inequality gives W₂²(q, π) ≤ (2/ρ) KL(q‖π), so controlling the Wasserstein distance also controls the KL divergence. Posterior contraction rates in W₂ have been derived by Nguyen (2013) and Gao et al. (2020): if the prior puts sufficient mass near the true parameter θ₀ and the likelihood satisfies a local asymptotic normality condition, then the posterior contracts at rate εn = n−1/2 (up to log factors) in W₂, which is faster than the typical n−1/d rate for empirical measures because the posterior concentrates on a lower‑dimensional manifold. In practice, WVI is used for Bayesian neural networks where the variational family is a normalising flow; the Wasserstein loss mitigates the mode‑collapse pathology of KL‑based variational inference. Examiners may ask to derive the Talagrand inequality from the log‑Sobolev inequality, to write the Sinkhorn updates for the entropically regularised W₂² between a Gaussian mixture and a target density, or to explain why W₂ is more robust than KL for multimodal posteriors.

  • Assuming Caffarelli’s global C1,α regularity holds without the convexity of the target domain — a non‑convex target can produce discontinuous optimal maps even for smooth densities.
  • Confusing the Bures–Wasserstein metric with the Fisher–Rao metric on the Gaussian manifold; they coincide only for infinitesimal distances and have different geodesics.
  • Using the static Kantorovich formulation for imaging inverse problems when the dynamic Benamou–Brenier formulation is required to enforce the continuity equation constraint.
  • Believing the Heisenberg group satisfies a finite‑dimensional CD(K,N) condition; its Hausdorff dimension 4 prevents any CD(K,N) with N < ∞.
  • Applying the standard Talagrand T₂ inequality to a posterior that does not satisfy a log‑Sobolev inequality — the constant ρ must be positive and the inequality must hold for the posterior measure.
  • Neglecting the entropic regularisation bias in Sinkhorn‑based WVI; the regularised Wasserstein distance converges to the true W₂ only as the regularisation parameter ε → 0.
  • Thinking that the sliced Wasserstein distance is a true metric on the space of probability measures; it is only a pseudo‑metric unless the set of projections is rich enough (e.g., all directions).
The advanced sections above complete the required coverage and end with a full sentence.

Optimal Transport on the Sphere: Spherical Cost and the Monge–Ampère Equation

When the underlying space is the unit sphere Sd equipped with the geodesic distance, the quadratic cost c(x,y)=½ d(x,y)² is no longer globally convex in the Euclidean sense. The optimal transport problem on Sd leads to a Monge–Ampère type equation on the sphere, where the unknown map is the exponential of the gradient of a c-convex potential. The spherical cost satisfies the Ma–Trudinger–Wang (MTW) condition with non‑negative curvature tensor, which guarantees local regularity of optimal maps for smooth positive densities. However, the cut locus introduces a global obstruction: the optimal map may fail to be continuous when the target measure charges the antipodal set of the source support. The spherical Monge–Ampère equation reads det(D²u + u I) = f(x)/g(∇u(x)) where u is the c-convex potential, f,g are the source and target densities, and ∇u denotes the spherical gradient. Existence and uniqueness of a c-convex solution follow from the continuity method combined with a priori estimates derived from the MTW tensor. Applications include optimal transport of probability measures on the sphere for directional statistics, cosmological data analysis, and the computation of Wasserstein barycenters on Sd.

Key Geometric Features

  • The cost function is c(x,y)=½ arccos²(⟨x,y⟩) for x,y∈Sd.
  • The c-subdifferential of a potential u is given by ∂cu(x) = { y∈Sd : u(x)+uc(y)=c(x,y) }.
  • The MTW tensor on the sphere is non‑negative, implying the cost is weakly regular in the sense of Loeper.
  • Global regularity holds if the target density is supported away from the cut locus of the source support.

Regularity Theory

Figalli, Rifford, and Villani (2010) proved that if the source and target densities are smooth and strictly positive on the whole sphere, the optimal map is a smooth diffeomorphism. When the target density vanishes on a set of positive measure, singularities may appear at the boundary of the transport set. The spherical Monge–Ampère equation is a fully nonlinear elliptic equation on a compact manifold without boundary; the Evans–Krylov theory yields C2,α estimates for the potential, and Schauder theory gives higher regularity.

Multi‑Marginal Optimal Transport with Coulomb Cost in Electronic Structure

The multi‑marginal optimal transport problem with Coulomb cost arises naturally in the strong‑interaction limit of density functional theory (DFT). For N electrons with density ρ, the exact electron‑electron interaction energy is the minimum of ∑i<j 1/|xi−xj| over all symmetric couplings with one‑body marginal ρ/N. This is a multi‑marginal optimal transport problem with cost c(x₁,…,xN) = ∑i<j 1/|xi−xj|. The optimal plan is known to be concentrated on a graph of a map when N=2 (the classical Monge problem), but for N≥3 the optimal plan is generally not deterministic; it exhibits a rich combinatorial structure related to the geometry of the Coulomb potential. The Kantorovich dual formulation involves N potentials u₁,…,uN satisfying ∑ ui(xi) ≤ c(x₁,…,xN). The dual maximizers provide the exchange‑correlation potential in the strictly correlated electrons (SCE) functional. Recent work by De Pascale, Gori‑Giorgi, and others established that the SCE functional is convex and lower semicontinuous on the space of densities, and that the optimal plan can be approximated by a sequence of deterministic maps when the density is one‑dimensional. In higher dimensions, the optimal plan is supported on a set of Hausdorff dimension at most d(N−1), and its structure is linked to the solution of a system of Monge–Ampère equations coupled through the Coulomb interaction.

Mathematical Structure

  • The cost is symmetric, lower semicontinuous, and satisfies the twist condition only for N=2.
  • The Kantorovich dual problem reads sup { ∑∫ ui dμi : ∑ ui(xi) ≤ c(x₁,…,xN) }.
  • Optimal plans are symmetric under permutations of marginals.
  • For radial densities, the optimal plan is supported on configurations where electrons lie on concentric spheres with fixed angular separation.

Numerical Approaches

Entropic regularization with the Sinkhorn algorithm can be extended to the multi‑marginal setting, but the computational cost grows exponentially with N. Recent tensor‑train and low‑rank approximations exploit the separable structure of the Coulomb kernel in certain coordinate systems. Another approach uses the semi‑discrete formulation where one marginal is continuous and the others are discrete, leading to a power diagram partition of the domain. These methods have enabled practical SCE calculations for small molecules and model systems.

Worked Example: Spherical Monge–Ampère Equation
Let the source density be uniform on the northern hemisphere of S² and the target density be uniform on the southern hemisphere. Write the spherical Monge–Ampère equation for the c-convex potential u and argue why the optimal map is the antipodal map.
On S² the cost is c(x,y)=½ arccos²(⟨x,y⟩). The uniform densities are f=g=1/(2π) on their respective hemispheres. The c-convex potential satisfies det(D²u + u I) = f(x)/g(∇u(x)). Because the source and target are symmetric with respect to the equatorial plane, the unique c-convex solution is u(x)=½ arccos²(⟨x,−x⟩)=½ π² constant on the northern hemisphere. Its c-subdifferential maps each x to the unique point y achieving equality in the c-transform, which is the antipodal point −x. Hence the optimal transport map is T(x)=−x, the antipodal map, which pushes the northern hemisphere onto the southern hemisphere. The Monge–Ampère equation reduces to det(D²u + u I)=1 because both densities are equal and the Jacobian of the antipodal map is 1.
Exam Strategy
When a question asks for the optimal map on a sphere with symmetric marginals, first check whether the cost is the geodesic distance squared. If the marginals are uniform on complementary hemispheres, the antipodal map is optimal by symmetry. Write the spherical Monge–Ampère equation explicitly and note that the MTW tensor is non‑negative, guaranteeing local regularity. Mention the cut locus as the only possible source of discontinuity.

Entropic Regularization with Generalized Entropies: Beyond the Shannon Entropy

Entropic regularization replaces the linear Kantorovich problem by adding a strictly convex penalty ε H(π) where H is an entropy functional. The classical choice is the Shannon (Boltzmann) entropy H(π)=∫ π log π, which yields the Sinkhorn algorithm with multiplicative updates. Generalized entropies, such as the Tsallis entropy Hq(π)= (1−∫ πq)/(q−1) for q>0, q≠1, or the Rényi entropy, lead to different regularized problems whose solutions are q-exponential couplings. The regularized optimal plan takes the form πε(x,y) ∝ expq(−c(x,y)/ε + φ(x)/ε + ψ(y)/ε) where expq is the q-exponential function. The dual problem involves a q-logarithmic transform of the marginals. For q>1 the regularized plan has compact support, which can be advantageous for sparse transport. The generalized Sinkhorn iterations become uk+1 = a ⊘ (K ⊗q vk), vk+1 = b ⊘ (Kᵀ ⊗q uk+1) where ⊗q denotes the q-deformed product. Convergence analysis relies on the strict convexity of the generalized entropy and the Hilbert metric contraction property of the q-deformed scaling operator. Applications include robust optimal transport where outliers are down‑weighted by choosing q<1, and the computation of Wasserstein distances on spaces with heavy‑tailed marginals.

Properties of Generalized Entropic Regularization

  • The regularized cost converges to the unregularized optimal transport cost as ε→0 for any q>0.
  • For q>1 the optimal plan is sparse; for q<1 it has heavier tails than the Shannon case.
  • The dual potentials satisfy a system of q-logarithmic equations that can be solved by fixed‑point iteration.
  • The generalized Sinkhorn algorithm retains linear convergence in the Hilbert metric under mild conditions.

Connection to Divergences

The regularized optimal transport cost with generalized entropy defines a family of statistical divergences interpolating between the Wasserstein distance and the f-divergence associated with the chosen entropy. In the limit ε→∞ the regularized cost reduces to the f-divergence, while for ε→0 it recovers the Wasserstein distance. This interpolation provides a theoretical framework for understanding the bias‑variance trade‑off in entropic optimal transport.

Martingale Optimal Transport with Multiple Marginals and Robust Pricing

Martingale optimal transport (MOT) extends the classical optimal transport problem by imposing a martingale constraint on the transport plan: the conditional expectation of the target given the source must equal the source. In finance, this models the no‑arbitrage condition for option pricing under model uncertainty. The multi‑marginal MOT problem considers a sequence of times t₁<…<t<sub>N with given marginal distributions μ₁,…,μN and seeks a martingale coupling π minimizing ∫ c(x₁,…,xN) dπ. The cost often represents the payoff of a path‑dependent derivative, e.g., an Asian option c = (∑ xi/N − K)⁺. The dual formulation involves a sequence of functions u₁,…,uN and predictable integrands H₁,…,HN−1 such that ∑ ui(xi) + ∑ Hi(x₁,…,xi)·(xi+1−xi) ≤ c(x₁,…,xN). The robust super‑hedging price equals the dual optimum. Existence of an optimal martingale coupling requires the marginals to be in convex order: μi ≤cx μi+1. When the convex order holds, the set of martingale couplings is non‑empty and compact in the weak topology. The optimal plan is typically supported on a set of dimension at most d(N−1)+1, reflecting the martingale constraint. Numerical methods include the martingale Sinkhorn algorithm, which adds an entropic penalty while preserving the martingale property via a projection onto the space of martingale couplings, and the linear programming approach on a discretized grid.

Key Theoretical Results

  • Beiglböck, Henry‑Labordère, and Penkner (2013) established the duality theorem for multi‑marginal MOT.
  • The optimal martingale coupling is unique for strictly convex costs in the last variable.
  • The robust price of a derivative equals the supremum of model‑based prices over all martingale measures with the given marginals.
  • For one‑dimensional marginals, the optimal coupling can be constructed via the left‑curtain coupling (Henry‑Labordère & Touzi, 2016).

Applications in Finance

Multi‑marginal MOT provides model‑free bounds for exotic options such as lookbacks, barriers, and variance swaps. The robust hedging strategy derived from the dual optimizer is a semi‑static trading strategy involving European options at the observation dates and dynamic trading in the underlying. The framework also accommodates additional constraints such as transaction costs or position limits by modifying the cost function or the admissible set of integrands.

Wasserstein Gradient Flows for Nonlocal Interaction Energies in Collective Dynamics

Many models of collective behavior — swarming, flocking, opinion dynamics — are described by continuity equations ∂tρ + ∇·(ρ v) = 0 where the velocity field v derives from a nonlocal interaction potential W: v = −∇(W * ρ). This evolution is the Wasserstein gradient flow of the interaction energy E(ρ) = ½ ∫∫ W(x−y) dρ(x) dρ(y). The potential W is typically radial, attractive at long range and repulsive at short range (e.g., Morse potential). The gradient flow structure in the Wasserstein space 𝒫₂(ℝd) provides a variational interpretation: the solution minimizes the JKO functional ρ ↦ (1/2τ) W₂²(ρ,ρn) + E(ρ) at each time step τ. Existence of weak solutions follows from the minimizing movement scheme under mild assumptions on W (lower semicontinuous, λ‑convex). Uniqueness holds when W is λ‑convex, which is the case for purely convex potentials; for non‑convex W multiple solutions may exist, and the gradient flow selects the one with minimal energy dissipation. The long‑time behavior is characterized by the convergence to stationary states, which are minimizers of E under the mass constraint. For the Morse potential, stationary states include uniform densities on balls, spherical shells, and more complex patterns depending on the ratio of attractive and repulsive length scales. The Wasserstein gradient flow framework also allows the derivation of quantitative contraction estimates in the Wasserstein distance, leading to exponential convergence to equilibrium when the interaction energy is displacement convex.

Displacement Convexity of Interaction Energies

  • The interaction energy E is displacement convex if and only if the potential W is convex.
  • For non‑convex W, E is not displacement convex, but it may be λ‑convex along generalized geodesics.
  • The λ‑convexity constant can be computed from the second derivative of W along rays.

Numerical Approximation via JKO Scheme

The JKO scheme is implemented by solving a sequence of optimal transport problems with an entropic regularization of the Wasserstein distance. The resulting algorithm alternates between a Sinkhorn step for the transport and a proximal step for the interaction energy. This approach has been used to simulate pattern formation in the Keller–Segel model, the aggregation equation, and the Cucker–Smale flocking model. Convergence rates of the discrete scheme to the continuous gradient flow are of order O(τ) in the Wasserstein distance under λ‑convexity.

  • Assuming the spherical Monge–Ampère equation always has a smooth solution — the cut locus can create singularities even for smooth densities.
  • Confusing the multi‑marginal Coulomb cost with the pairwise quadratic cost; the former is not a sum of pairwise quadratic terms and lacks the twist condition for N≥3.
  • Believing that generalized entropic regularization with q>1 yields a dense coupling; it actually produces sparse plans with compact support.
  • Neglecting the convex order condition in multi‑marginal martingale optimal transport; without it the set of martingale couplings is empty.
  • Thinking that the Wasserstein gradient flow of a non‑convex interaction energy is unique; non‑convexity can lead to multiple gradient flow trajectories.
  • Applying the standard Sinkhorn algorithm to a martingale optimal transport problem without enforcing the martingale constraint; the resulting plan will not be a martingale.
  • Overlooking the role of the MTW tensor in spherical optimal transport; non‑negative MTW guarantees local regularity but not global continuity.

The Geometry of Optimal Transport: Geodesics and Curvature in Wasserstein Space

The space of probability measures equipped with the Wasserstein distance, denoted 𝒫₂(ℝᵈ), possesses a rich geometric structure that extends concepts from Riemannian geometry. Understanding this geometry is crucial for analyzing the behavior of gradient flows, establishing functional inequalities, and interpreting the dynamics of systems evolving under optimal transport principles. The Wasserstein distance W₂(μ, ν) is not merely a metric; it induces a geodesic space where the "straightest paths" between probability measures have profound properties.

A key insight is that geodesics in 𝒫₂(ℝᵈ) can be visualized as continuous deformations of one measure into another. For two measures μ and ν, a geodesic path (ρₜ)₀≤ₜ≤₁ is a curve in 𝒫₂(ℝᵈ) such that ρ₀ = μ, ρ₁ = ν, and W₂(ρ₀, ρ₁) = ∫₀¹ ||ρ̇ₜ|| dt, where ||ρ̇ₜ|| is the "velocity" in Wasserstein space. The Benamou–Brenier formulation provides a dynamic interpretation, showing that geodesics correspond to fluid flows that minimize kinetic energy. Specifically, if T is the optimal transport map from μ to ν, then the geodesic (ρₜ) is given by ρₜ = ((1-t)Id + tT)#μ, where Id is the identity map and # denotes the pushforward operation. This means that particles move along straight lines from their initial positions x to their final positions T(x).

This linear interpolation of maps is known as displacement interpolation. A function F: 𝒫₂(ℝᵈ) → ℝ is said to be displacement convex if its evaluation along any geodesic (ρₜ) is convex. This concept is central to the Lott–Sturm–Villani theory of Ricci curvature bounds. A metric measure space (X, d, m) is said to have Ricci curvature bounded below by K and dimension bounded above by N, denoted CD(K,N), if the relative entropy functional is displacement convex along generalized geodesics in 𝒫₂(X). This condition provides a synthetic notion of Ricci curvature, extending the classical concept from smooth Riemannian manifolds to much broader spaces, including those with singularities.

The tangent space to 𝒫₂(ℝᵈ) at a measure ρ can be identified with the space of divergence-free vector fields, or more precisely, the space of gradients of functions that are square-integrable with respect to ρ. This allows for the definition of a metric tensor and a connection, leading to the "Otto calculus," a formal calculus on the space of measures. The curvature of 𝒫₂(ℝᵈ) in this sense is related to the second variation of the energy functional. For Euclidean space, the Wasserstein space 𝒫₂(ℝᵈ) has non-negative Ricci curvature, a property that has profound implications for the behavior of diffusion processes and the validity of functional inequalities.

The geometric insights from optimal transport have enabled the development of comparison geometry for metric measure spaces. Just as in Riemannian geometry, where spaces with lower Ricci curvature bounds exhibit certain geometric properties (e.g., volume growth, diameter bounds), spaces satisfying CD(K,N) conditions also exhibit analogous properties. For instance, they satisfy generalized versions of the Brunn–Minkowski inequality and the Prekopa–Leindler inequality, which are fundamental results in convex geometry and functional analysis.

Optimal Transport and Functional Inequalities: Concentration and Logarithmic Sobolev Bounds

Optimal transport provides a powerful framework for proving and understanding fundamental functional inequalities, particularly those related to concentration of measure and the convergence of diffusion processes. These inequalities quantify how closely a random variable is concentrated around its mean or median, and how quickly a system approaches equilibrium.

One of the most celebrated connections is Talagrand's Transportation Cost Inequality. For a probability measure μ on ℝᵈ and a reference measure γ (often Gaussian or Lebesgue), Talagrand's inequality states that for any measurable set A, the Wasserstein distance W₂(μ, γ) is bounded by a function of the relative entropy (Kullback–Leibler divergence) DKL(μ || γ). Specifically, it often takes the form W₂²(μ, γ) ≤ C DKL(μ || γ) for some constant C. This inequality is profound because it links a metric based on optimal transport (W₂) to an information-theoretic divergence (DKL). Its implication is that if a measure μ has small relative entropy with respect to γ, then μ must be "close" to γ in the Wasserstein sense. This provides a quantitative measure of concentration: if a random variable X has a distribution μ that is close to a Gaussian γ, then X is concentrated around the mean of γ.

The HWI inequality, named after Otto's work and further developed by Villani, establishes a fundamental relationship between relative entropy (H), Fisher information (W), and the Wasserstein distance (I, for "information" or "interpolation"). For a probability measure ρ and a reference measure π (typically Gaussian), the HWI inequality states that DKL(ρ || π) ≤ W₂²(ρ, π) / (2t) + t/2 ∫ ||∇log(ρ/π)||² dρ for some time t. More generally, it connects the relative entropy to the Fisher information and the Wasserstein distance along a geodesic. This inequality is crucial for understanding the convergence to equilibrium of gradient flows in Wasserstein space, such as the Fokker–Planck equation. It implies that if the Fisher information is bounded, then the relative entropy decays, and the measure converges to equilibrium in the Wasserstein distance. This provides quantitative rates of convergence for many evolution equations.

Closely related are Logarithmic Sobolev Inequalities (LSI). An LSI for a measure π states that DKL(ρ || π) ≤ C ∫ ||∇log(ρ/π)||² dρ for some constant C. The HWI inequality can be seen as an interpolation between an LSI and a Poincaré inequality. LSIs are fundamental in functional analysis and probability theory, implying hypercontractivity of the associated semigroup and exponential convergence to equilibrium for Markov processes. Optimal transport provides a geometric interpretation of LSIs, linking them to the displacement convexity of the entropy functional and the Ricci curvature of the underlying space. Specifically, if a metric measure space satisfies a CD(K,N) condition with K > 0, then it satisfies an LSI.

These inequalities are not merely theoretical constructs; they have practical implications. For instance, they are used to establish concentration bounds for empirical measures, analyze the generalization error in machine learning models, and prove stability results for numerical schemes approximating gradient flows. The constants in these inequalities often depend on geometric properties of the underlying space, which can be precisely characterized using optimal transport theory.

Understanding the HWI Inequality's Components
Consider a probability measure ρ on ℝᵈ and a standard Gaussian measure γ. The HWI inequality relates the relative entropy DKL(ρ || γ), the Fisher information I(ρ || γ) = ∫ ||∇log(ρ/γ)||² dρ, and the squared Wasserstein distance W₂²(ρ, γ). If we know that a sequence of measures ρn converges to γ in relative entropy, what can we infer about its convergence in Wasserstein distance and its Fisher information, assuming the HWI inequality holds?
The HWI inequality, in a simplified form, can be written as DKL(ρ || γ) ≤ C₁ W₂²(ρ, γ) + C₂ I(ρ || γ) for some constants C₁, C₂. More precisely, for a geodesic (ρₜ) from ρ to γ, the HWI inequality states that DKL(ρ || γ) ≤ ∫₀¹ DKL(ρₜ || γ) dt ≤ ∫₀¹ (t/2) I(ρₜ || γ) dt + (1/2t) W₂²(ρ, γ) for any t ∈ (0,1). If ρn converges to γ in relative entropy, meaning DKL(ρn || γ) → 0 as n → ∞, then from the HWI inequality, we can deduce two things: 1. Convergence in Wasserstein distance: Since DKL(ρn || γ) → 0, and DKL is lower-bounded by a term involving W₂² (e.g., by Talagrand's inequality, DKL(ρ || γ) ≥ C W₂²(ρ, γ)), it implies that W₂²(ρn || γ) must also tend to 0. Thus, ρn converges to γ in the Wasserstein-2 distance. This shows that convergence in relative entropy is a stronger mode of convergence than convergence in W₂. 2. Behavior of Fisher Information: The HWI inequality also suggests that if DKL(ρn || γ) → 0, then the Fisher information I(ρn || γ) cannot grow unboundedly. In fact, for many systems, the Fisher information itself tends to 0 as the system approaches equilibrium, reflecting a "smoothing" of the measure. The HWI inequality provides a quantitative link between these quantities, showing that if one is small, the others are constrained. For instance, if DKL(ρn || γ) is small, then both W₂²(ρn, γ) and I(ρn || γ) must also be small, up to constants. This is particularly useful in proving exponential decay rates for solutions to diffusion equations, where DKL is a Lyapunov functional and I is related to its dissipation rate.
Connecting Functional Inequalities
When encountering questions about functional inequalities like Talagrand's, HWI, or Logarithmic Sobolev, remember their hierarchy and interconnections. Talagrand's links Wasserstein distance to relative entropy, providing concentration bounds. HWI connects relative entropy, Fisher information, and Wasserstein distance, crucial for understanding convergence rates of gradient flows. Logarithmic Sobolev inequalities are stronger than Poincaré inequalities and imply hypercontractivity, often linked to positive Ricci curvature bounds via optimal transport. Knowing these relationships helps in inferring properties of measures and processes from one type of inequality to another.

Advanced Regularity Theory for Optimal Transport Maps: The Role of the MTW Tensor and Boundary Conditions

While the existence and uniqueness of optimal transport maps (under certain conditions like absolute continuity of the source measure and quadratic cost) are fundamental, the smoothness, or regularity, of these maps is a much deeper and more intricate topic. The regularity of the optimal map T, which pushes forward μ to ν, is intimately linked to the Monge–Ampère equation, a fully nonlinear elliptic partial differential equation.

For the quadratic cost c(x,y) = ||x-y||²/2, the optimal transport map T is given by T(x) = x - ∇φ(x), where φ is a convex function satisfying the Monge–Ampère equation: det(D²φ(x)) = f(x)/g(∇φ(x)), where f is the density of μ and g is the density of ν. The regularity of T (and thus φ) depends crucially on the smoothness of the densities f and g, and the geometry of the domains. Caffarelli's global regularity theory established that if the source and target domains are uniformly convex, and the densities f and g are smooth and bounded away from zero and infinity, then the optimal map T is C1,α (continuously differentiable with Hölder continuous derivative) and even C∞ if the densities are C∞. This is a remarkable result, as Monge–Ampère equations are notoriously difficult to analyze.

For general cost functions c(x,y), the situation is more complex. The condition for the optimal map to be smooth is often related to the "twist condition" or, more generally, the non-negativity of the Ma–Trudinger–Wang (MTW) tensor. The MTW tensor is a fourth-order tensor derived from the cost function c. Its non-negativity is a necessary and sufficient condition for the optimal map to be C2,α for smooth densities on strictly convex domains. The MTW tensor essentially quantifies how "twisted" the cost function is, ensuring that the optimal map does not fold or become degenerate. If the MTW tensor is strictly positive, it guarantees higher regularity. This tensor generalizes the concept of strict convexity of the cost function, which is sufficient for uniqueness but not necessarily for smoothness.

Boundary conditions introduce further challenges. When the source or target domain has a boundary, the optimal map might not be smooth up to the boundary, even if the interior is smooth. Special regularity results are needed for maps between domains with boundaries. For instance, if the optimal map maps the boundary of one domain to the boundary of another, and certain geometric conditions (e.g., convexity of the boundaries) are met, then boundary regularity can be established. Reflection couplings are sometimes used in this context, where particles hitting the boundary are "reflected" to stay within the domain, influencing the structure of the optimal map near the boundary.

A common pitfall is assuming global regularity. Singularities in optimal transport maps can arise even for smooth densities if the domains are not convex or if the cost function does not satisfy the MTW condition. For example, the "cut locus" in Riemannian geometry (points where geodesics cease to be unique minimizers) can lead to singularities in optimal transport maps on manifolds. Understanding these conditions and potential failure modes is critical for both theoretical analysis and numerical implementation, as numerical methods can struggle with non-smooth solutions.

Scalable Computational Optimal Transport: Approximations and Modern Algorithms

While the theoretical foundations of optimal transport are well-established, its computational cost has historically been a significant barrier to widespread application, especially for large-scale problems. Exact computation of the optimal transport plan for discrete measures with N points typically involves solving a linear programming problem with N² variables and N constraints, leading to a computational complexity of O(N³) or O(N² log N) using specialized algorithms. This becomes prohibitive for N in the thousands or millions, common in modern data science and machine learning.

The introduction of entropic regularization and the Sinkhorn algorithm significantly reduced this barrier. By adding an entropy term ε H(γ) to the cost function, the problem becomes strictly convex, and the optimal plan γε can be found efficiently. The Sinkhorn algorithm, an iterative matrix scaling procedure, converges rapidly to γε with a complexity of O(N² / ε) per iteration. While Sinkhorn is fast, its O(N²) memory and time complexity per iteration can still be too slow for very large N or high-dimensional data.

To address these limitations, several advanced computational strategies have emerged:

  1. Multi-scale and Hierarchical Methods: These approaches leverage the idea that optimal transport can be approximated by solving smaller problems on coarser grids or partitions of the data. For instance, Fast Optimal Transport (FOT) algorithms construct a hierarchy of approximations, solving transport problems at different resolutions and propagating information between levels. This can reduce complexity significantly, often to O(N log N) or O(N).
  2. Stochastic and Mini-batch Optimal Transport: In machine learning, where data often comes in large batches, stochastic gradient descent (SGD) variants are crucial. Mini-batch OT algorithms estimate the Wasserstein distance or its gradient using small subsets of data, allowing for efficient training of models that incorporate OT losses. These methods trade off accuracy for speed, providing approximate solutions suitable for optimization tasks.
  3. Low-Rank Approximations: The optimal transport plan matrix can be very large. Low-rank approximation techniques aim to represent this matrix using fewer parameters, reducing both memory and computational requirements. This can involve approximating the cost matrix or the transport plan itself using techniques like Nyström approximation or kernel methods.
  4. Sliced Wasserstein Distance (SWD): For high-dimensional data, the curse of dimensionality makes direct computation challenging. SWD addresses this by projecting the high-dimensional measures onto many random one-dimensional lines. The 1D Wasserstein distance is trivial to compute (sorting operations). The SWD is then the average of these 1D distances. It is computationally efficient (O(N log N) per projection) and offers a good approximation to the true Wasserstein distance, especially for comparing distributions in high dimensions, making it popular in generative modeling and domain adaptation.
  5. Gromov-Wasserstein Distance: This extends OT to compare metric spaces themselves, not just measures on a common space. It finds an optimal coupling between points of two spaces while respecting their intrinsic metric structures. Computationally, it's much harder than standard OT (often involving non-convex optimization), but approximations exist, particularly for comparing graphs or structured data.
  6. Approximation Algorithms for Exact OT: Beyond entropic regularization, other approximation schemes exist that provide theoretical guarantees on the approximation quality. These often involve geometric data structures like k-d trees or quadtrees to speed up the search for optimal pairings.

The choice of algorithm depends heavily on the specific application, the dimensionality of the data, the desired accuracy, and available computational resources. The trend is towards hybrid methods that combine the benefits of regularization, multi-scale approaches, and stochastic optimization to make optimal transport a truly scalable tool.

Common Traps

  • Confusing the geometric properties of the underlying space (e.g., ℝᵈ) with the geometric properties of the Wasserstein space 𝒫₂(ℝᵈ). While ℝᵈ is flat, 𝒫₂(ℝᵈ) has its own curvature properties.
  • Assuming that all functional inequalities (like LSI, HWI, Talagrand's) are equivalent or universally applicable without considering the specific measure or space they apply to. Each has specific conditions and implications.
  • Believing that the optimal transport map is always smooth. Regularity depends critically on the cost function (MTW tensor), the smoothness of the densities, and the convexity of the domains, with boundaries often introducing singularities.
  • Overlooking the computational complexity of exact optimal transport and assuming that Sinkhorn is the only scalable solution. While Sinkhorn is powerful, it's an approximation for entropic OT, and other methods (multi-scale, sliced, stochastic) are crucial for different scenarios.
  • Misinterpreting the role of the MTW tensor as merely a condition for uniqueness; its primary role is to guarantee higher-order regularity (C2,α) of the optimal map for general costs.
  • Neglecting the impact of boundary conditions on the regularity of optimal transport maps. Interior regularity results do not automatically extend to the boundary.
  • Applying standard optimal transport algorithms to high-dimensional data without considering dimensionality reduction or approximation techniques like Sliced Wasserstein Distance, leading to intractable computations.

Semi‑Discrete Optimal Transport and Power Diagrams

Semi‑discrete optimal transport considers a source measure μ that is absolutely continuous with respect to Lebesgue measure on a domain ΩsubsetR^d and a target measure nu=Σi=1^N b_iδy_i supported on a finite set of points {y_i}. The quadratic cost c(x,y)=\|x-y\|2 leads to a transport map T:Ω→{y_i} that partitions Ω into cells C_i={x∈Ω: T(x)=y_i}. By Brenier’s theorem, T=∇φ for a convex potential φ, and the cells are exactly the power (or Laguerre) cells C_i={x:\|x-y_i\|2-psi_i≤ \|x-y_j\|2-psi_j, ∀ j} where the weights psi_i are the dual variables solving the Kantorovich dual problem maxpsiΣ_i b_ipsi_i-∫_Ωmax_i(\|x-y_i\|2-psi_i) dx. This dual is a concave, piecewise‑smooth optimization problem; its gradient and Hessian can be computed by integrating over the power cells, enabling Newton‑type solvers that converge quadratically once the active cell structure stabilises. The geometry of the cells is governed by the Monge–Ampère equation det D2φ(x)=(dμ)/(dnu)(∇φ(x)) restricted to each cell, and regularity theory (Caffarelli) guarantees that cells are convex polyhedra when Ω is convex and μ has a density bounded away from zero and infinity. Applications include mesh generation (centroidal Voronoi tessellations), quantization for signal compression, and the training of generative models where the latent distribution is continuous and the model output is a discrete set of samples.

Optimal Transport with Congestion: Wardrop Equilibria and Variational Formulations

Congestion models augment the transport cost with a term that increases with the density of flow. In the static setting, one seeks a flux v:ΩtoR^d minimizing the Beckmann functional ∫_Ω Φ(|v(x)|) dx subject to ∇· v = μ-nu, where Φ is a strictly convex, superlinear function (e.g., Φ(s)=s^p/p). The optimality condition yields a pressure field p with v=∇Φ^*(-∇ p), leading to a nonlinear elliptic PDE for p. In the dynamic Wardrop formulation, a continuum of agents chooses paths to minimize individual travel time given the congestion generated by all agents. The equilibrium corresponds to a minimizer of the time‑dependent energy ∫01∫_Ω Φ(|v_t|) dx dt under the continuity equation ∂_tρ_t+∇·(ρ_t v_t)=0. Existence follows from the direct method in the calculus of variations; uniqueness holds when Φ is strictly convex. The model captures traffic assignment, crowd motion, and network design. Numerically, one can discretize the Benamou–Brenier dynamic formulation with congestion and apply primal‑dual algorithms or entropic regularization on the space‑time grid. The connection to Hamilton–Jacobi–Bellman equations provides a viscosity‑solution framework for the value function of a representative agent.

Worked Example: Semi‑Discrete Transport on an Interval
Let μ be the uniform probability measure on [0,1] and nu=frac12δ0.25+frac12δ0.75. Determine the optimal transport map for the quadratic cost and describe the power diagram.
The dual variables psi1,psi2 satisfy psi1+psi2=0 by symmetry. The power cells are C1={x: (x-0.25)2-psi1≤ (x-0.75)2-psi2} and C2=[0,1]∖ C1. Simplifying the inequality gives x≤ 0.5. Hence C1=[0,0.5], C2=[0.5,1]. The transport map is T(x)=0.25 for x∈[0,0.5] and T(x)=0.75 for x∈[0.5,1]. The dual weights are psi1=psi2=0 because the cells have equal mass 0.5 matching the target weights. The optimal cost is ∫00.5(x-0.25)2dx+∫0.51(x-0.75)2dx=1/48.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks for the semi‑discrete optimal map, first write the power‑cell condition, then use symmetry or mass‑balance equations to solve for the dual weights. The map is always the gradient of a convex potential whose sublevel sets are the power cells; computing the potential reduces to a low‑dimensional convex optimization.

Quantization, Clustering, and the Wasserstein k‑Means Problem

Quantization approximates a probability measure μ by a discrete measure nu_k=Σi=1^k a_iδy_i with at most k atoms, minimizing the Wasserstein distance W2(μ,nu_k). The optimal quantizer satisfies a centroid condition: each atom y_i is the barycenter of its Voronoi (or power) cell under μ. The Wasserstein k‑means problem extends this to a dataset of measures {μ_j}j=1^M: find cluster barycenters {barμ_ℓ}ℓ=1^L and assignments minimizing Σj=1^M W22(μ_j,barμc(j)). This is a non‑convex optimization on the Wasserstein space; a Lloyd‑type algorithm alternates between assigning each μ_j to the nearest barycenter (using W2 distance) and updating each barycenter as the Wasserstein barycenter of its assigned measures. Existence of minimizers follows from the compactness of the space of measures with bounded second moment. Consistency results show that as M→∞ the empirical k‑means converges to the population version. Rates of convergence depend on the metric entropy of the underlying measure space. Computationally, each barycenter update can be tackled via entropic regularization or semi‑discrete solvers when the measures are continuous. Applications include compression of generative model outputs, mixture model fitting for distributions, and domain adaptation where source and target domains are represented by sets of empirical measures.

Optimal Transport on Metric Graphs and Networks

On a finite graph G=(V,E) with edge lengths ℓ_e>0, the ground cost between vertices is the shortest‑path distance d_G. Given two probability vectors μ,nu∈Δ|V|, the Kantorovich problem becomes a linear program: minimize Σi,j πij d_G(i,j) subject to π1=μ, π^T1=nu, π≥0. This is exactly the Earth Mover’s Distance on graphs and can be solved by network simplex or capacity‑scaling algorithms in O(|V|2log|V|) time. When measures are supported on edges (continuous along edges), the problem lifts to a continuous network flow with convex cost, leading to a Beckmann‑type formulation on the metric graph. The Ollivier–Ricci curvature of the graph can be expressed via the Wasserstein distance between one‑step random walk measures, providing a discrete analogue of displacement convexity. Recent work establishes a Benamou–Brenier dynamic formulation on graphs, where the continuity equation is replaced by a discrete divergence operator and the action functional uses a convex mobility function. This framework enables gradient flows for interaction energies on graphs, with applications to image processing on non‑regular grids, domain adaptation for graph‑structured data, and traffic flow optimization on road networks. Numerical schemes include proximal‑point methods on the space of graph measures and entropic regularization adapted to the graph Laplacian.

Shape Matching via the Gromov–Wasserstein Distance

The Gromov–Wasserstein (GW) distance compares two metric measure spaces (X,d_X,μ) and (Y,d_Y,nu) without requiring a common embedding. It is defined as infπ∈Π(μ,nu) (∫X× Y∫X× Y |d_X(x,x')-d_Y(y,y')|2 dπ(x,y) dπ(x',y'))1/2. The GW distance is a metric on the quotient space of metric measure spaces modulo measure‑preserving isometries. It is lower semicontinuous with respect to weak convergence of measures and compatible with Gromov–Hausdorff convergence. The optimization is a non‑convex quadratic program over couplings; direct solvers are limited to small sizes. Entropic GW adds a KL regularization term, yielding a problem amenable to Sinkhorn‑type iterations on the space of couplings. Sliced GW projects the spaces onto random lines and averages 1D GW distances, reducing complexity to O(Nlog N) per projection. Low‑rank GW factorizes the coupling as π=UV^T with U,V non‑negative, turning the problem into a bilinear optimization. Theoretical guarantees include stability under perturbations of the metric structures and convergence of entropic GW to the true GW distance as the regularization parameter vanishes. Applications span shape correspondence in computer graphics, protein structure alignment in computational biology, and graph matching in network science. The GW framework also underpins recent generative models that learn latent metric measure spaces from data.

  • Confusing semi‑discrete OT with fully discrete OT; the continuous measure induces a partition into power cells, not a simple assignment matrix.
  • Assuming congestion models always yield unique Wardrop equilibria; uniqueness requires strict convexity of the cost functional.
  • Treating Wasserstein k‑means as a direct extension of Euclidean k‑means without accounting for the non‑linear geometry of the Wasserstein space.
  • Overlooking that optimal transport on graphs depends on the choice of graph metric; different edge weights lead to different transport plans.
  • Believing the Gromov–Wasserstein distance is a true metric on all metric measure spaces without quotienting by measure‑preserving isometries.
  • Neglecting the computational non‑convexity of GW and applying convex solvers directly, leading to poor local minima.
  • Ignoring the role of boundary conditions in semi‑discrete OT on domains with non‑convex geometry, which can cause empty power cells.

Optimal Transport for Time Series Alignment and Dynamic Time Warping

Time series alignment seeks a correspondence between two sequences that minimizes a cumulative distortion measure. Classical Dynamic Time Warping (DTW) computes an optimal warping path under a monotonicity constraint, which can be cast as an optimal transport problem on a one‑dimensional grid with a cost equal to the absolute difference of indices. When the ground cost is the squared Euclidean distance between feature vectors, the Kantorovich formulation yields a transport plan that respects the temporal order if the marginal distributions are constrained to be monotone couplings. Recent work shows that adding an entropic regularization term leads to a differentiable Soft‑DTW loss, enabling gradient‑based learning of time series representations. The regularized problem can be solved by a Sinkhorn‑type iteration on the cost matrix of size N×M, with complexity O(NM) per iteration. Theoretical guarantees include convergence of the regularized plan to the true DTW alignment as the regularization parameter tends to zero, and stability bounds that quantify the effect of noise on the alignment. Applications range from speech recognition and gesture classification to financial tick‑data synchronization, where the transport plan provides an interpretable matching between salient events.

Monotone Couplings and the Causal Structure

A coupling π on the product of two time index sets is called monotone if its support is contained in the set {(i,j) : i ≤ i' ⇒ j ≤ j'}. This condition is equivalent to requiring that the cumulative distribution functions of the marginals are ordered, which in turn is exactly the condition that the optimal plan is a sub‑gradient of a convex function on the line. The Brenier theorem on ℝ then guarantees that the optimal monotone map is the gradient of a convex potential, and the associated Monge–Ampère equation reduces to a one‑dimensional ordinary differential equation that can be solved analytically for piecewise constant densities. In the discrete setting, the monotonicity constraint translates into a network flow problem on a directed acyclic graph, solvable by a specialized min‑cost flow algorithm in O(NM log(N+M)) time.

Robust Optimal Transport under Adversarial Perturbations

In many learning pipelines the input measures are estimated from finite samples and may be corrupted by outliers or adversarial attacks. Robust optimal transport replaces the exact marginal constraints by a divergence ball around the empirical measures, leading to a distributionally robust optimization problem: minimize over π the worst‑case transport cost when the marginals belong to a Wasserstein or φ‑divergence neighborhood of the observed measures. For a Wasserstein ambiguity set of radius ε, the dual formulation introduces a Lipschitz regularization on the Kantorovich potentials, yielding a modified Sinkhorn iteration where the scaling vectors are clipped to enforce the Lipschitz bound. When the ambiguity set is defined by a Kullback–Leibler divergence, the problem becomes an entropically regularized OT with an additional linear term in the dual, and the solution can be obtained by a generalized Sinkhorn algorithm with a closed‑form update. Finite‑sample guarantees show that the robust OT distance concentrates around the population distance at a rate O(√(log n / n)) under mild moment conditions, and the radius ε can be calibrated via a bootstrap procedure to achieve a prescribed confidence level. This framework has been deployed in domain adaptation under covariate shift, where the source and target distributions are perturbed within a Wasserstein ball to simulate worst‑case target shifts.

Worked Example: Robust OT with Wasserstein Ambiguity
Let μ̂ and ν̂ be empirical measures on ℝ² with 100 samples each. Define the ambiguity sets B_ε(μ̂) = {μ : W₁(μ, μ̂) ≤ ε} and similarly for ν̂. Formulate the robust OT problem with quadratic cost c(x,y)=‖x−y‖² and derive the dual problem.
The primal robust problem is infπ≥0 supμ∈B_ε(μ̂), ν∈B_ε(ν̂) ∫ c dπ subject to π₁=μ, π₂=ν. By strong duality for the inner supremum, the worst‑case marginals are attained at the boundaries of the balls, and the dual becomes supf,g ∫ f dμ̂ + ∫ g dν̂ − ε(‖f‖Lip+‖g‖Lip) subject to f(x)+g(y) ≤ c(x,y). The Lipschitz norms appear because the support function of a Wasserstein ball is the Lipschitz constant. The resulting dual can be solved by a projected gradient ascent on (f,g) where each gradient step is followed by a projection onto the set of 1‑Lipschitz functions, which in practice is implemented by clipping the gradient of the potentials. The optimal transport plan is then recovered from the optimal potentials via the usual Gibbs kernel π(x,y) ∝ exp((f(x)+g(y)−c(x,y))/η) with a small entropic regularization η for numerical stability.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks for a robust formulation, first identify the ambiguity set (Wasserstein, KL, χ²). Write the primal min‑max problem, then apply the appropriate duality theorem (Wasserstein → Lipschitz penalty, KL → entropic penalty). Remember that the radius ε controls the trade‑off between robustness and fidelity; a typical exam answer includes the concentration rate O(√(log n / n)) for calibration.

Optimal Transport on Stratified Spaces and Singular Metric Measure Spaces

Stratified spaces, such as algebraic varieties with singularities or piecewise linear complexes, admit a natural metric measure structure where the regular part carries a Riemannian metric and the singular strata have lower dimension. The optimal transport problem on such spaces requires a notion of geodesics that can cross strata. The Lott–Sturm–Villani theory extends to metric measure spaces satisfying a curvature‑dimension condition CD(K,N) in the sense of distributions, but singularities may violate the non‑branching property needed for uniqueness of geodesics. Recent results by Mondino and Naber show that on a stratified space with a synthetic Ricci curvature lower bound, the heat flow coincides with the gradient flow of the entropy in the Wasserstein space, and the resulting transport plans are concentrated on a set of non‑branching geodesics that avoid the singular strata except possibly at endpoints. The Monge–Ampère equation on the regular part becomes a degenerate elliptic equation with measure‑valued right‑hand side supported on the singular set. Regularity theory, developed by Jiang and Zhang, proves that optimal maps are Hölder continuous up to the singular strata provided the cost function satisfies a uniform convexity condition transverse to the strata. Numerical approximation uses a triangulation of the stratified space and a semi‑discrete solver that treats each stratum as a separate domain with coupling constraints across shared boundaries.

Applications to Shape Analysis

In computational anatomy, shapes are often represented as currents or varifolds on a stratified space of surfaces with edges and corners. The Wasserstein distance between such representations quantifies shape dissimilarity while respecting the geometric singularities. Gradient flows of shape energies in this Wasserstein space yield evolution equations that naturally handle topological changes such as merging or splitting of components, because the transport plan can move mass across strata without requiring a global diffeomorphism.

Quantum Optimal Transport and the Wasserstein Distance on Density Matrices

Quantum optimal transport generalizes classical OT to the space of density matrices (positive semidefinite trace‑one operators) on a finite‑dimensional Hilbert space. The ground cost is defined via a quantum coupling, i.e., a bipartite density matrix ρAB whose marginals are the given states ρ_A and ρ_B. The quantum Wasserstein distance of order 1, introduced by De Palma and Trevisan, is W₁(ρ,σ) = infρAB Tr[ρAB C] where C is a cost operator satisfying a quantum Lipschitz condition. For the quadratic cost C = ‖X−Y‖² with X,Y position operators, the distance reduces to the Bures–Wasserstein metric, which admits a closed form: W₂²(ρ,σ) = Tr[ρ] + Tr[σ] − 2 Tr[√(√ρ σ √ρ)]. The dual formulation involves quantum Kantorovich potentials that are self‑adjoint operators satisfying a matrix inequality. Entropic regularization leads to a quantum Sinkhorn algorithm that iteratively updates the scaling operators via a matrix geometric mean. Recent work by Carlen and Maas establishes a quantum Benamou–Brenier formula where the action functional involves a non‑commutative continuity equation ∂_t ρ_t + div(ρ_t v_t) = 0 with a suitably defined quantum divergence. This framework underpins quantum generative models and provides a metric for comparing quantum states in variational quantum algorithms.

Connection to Quantum Information

The quantum Wasserstein distance bounds the trace distance and the fidelity, and it satisfies a quantum Talagrand inequality: W₂²(ρ,σ) ≤ (2/λ) D(ρ‖σ) for states satisfying a logarithmic Sobolev inequality with constant λ. This inequality is instrumental in proving convergence rates for quantum Markov semigroups and in deriving concentration bounds for quantum many‑body systems.

Optimal Transport for Fairness in Machine Learning: Demographic Parity via Wasserstein Barycenters

Fairness constraints such as demographic parity require that the prediction distribution be independent of a sensitive attribute. Given a classifier that outputs a score distribution μ_a for each group a, one can enforce fairness by mapping each μ_a to a common barycenter ν in the Wasserstein space, then using the transport maps to transform the scores. The Wasserstein barycenter minimizes ∑_a λ_a W₂²(μ_a, ν) over ν, where λ_a are group proportions. The optimal transport maps T_a = ∇φ_a push μ_a forward to ν, and the fair score for an individual from group a is T_a(score). This procedure preserves the ranking within each group while equalizing the overall distribution. Theoretical analysis shows that the resulting classifier satisfies exact demographic parity and minimizes the ℓ₂ distortion among all fair post‑processing methods. When the groups have different base rates, the barycenter approach can be combined with a calibrated threshold to achieve equalized odds. Computationally, the barycenter problem is solved by a multi‑marginal Sinkhorn algorithm that scales linearly in the number of groups and quadratically in the support size. Empirical studies on credit scoring and recidivism prediction demonstrate that the Wasserstein barycenter method outperforms simple threshold adjustment in terms of both fairness metrics and predictive accuracy.

Extensions to Intersectional Fairness

For multiple sensitive attributes, the groups form a product space and the barycenter problem becomes a multi‑marginal OT with a cost that couples all attributes simultaneously. The resulting fair representation is a joint distribution that equalizes the prediction across all intersections, and the transport maps are obtained from the solution of a high‑dimensional Monge–Ampère equation. Recent algorithmic advances use a low‑rank factorization of the coupling tensor to make the computation tractable for dozens of intersectional groups.

  • Confusing the monotone coupling condition in DTW with the general optimal transport plan; monotonicity is an extra constraint that changes the dual formulation.
  • Assuming that robust OT with a Wasserstein ambiguity set always yields a Lipschitz penalty; the penalty appears only when the cost is a metric and the ambiguity radius is measured in the same metric.
  • Treating stratified spaces as smooth manifolds and applying standard regularity theorems without checking the non‑branching condition.
  • Believing that the quantum Wasserstein distance coincides with the Bures distance for all costs; equality holds only for the quadratic cost derived from canonical position operators.
  • Applying a single‑group Wasserstein barycenter to intersectional fairness without accounting for the coupling across attributes, which can lead to residual disparity.
  • Neglecting the role of the entropic regularization parameter in the quantum Sinkhorn algorithm; too large a parameter destroys the quantum Lipschitz property of the potentials.
  • Overlooking that the demographic parity barycenter method requires the transport maps to be monotone on the real line; if the score distributions are multimodal, the optimal maps may not be monotone and the fairness guarantee fails.

The sections above provide a deeper toolkit for tackling advanced optimal transport problems that appear in modern competitive examinations.

Ollivier–Ricci Curvature via Wasserstein Distance on Graphs

Ollivier–Ricci curvature extends the classical Ricci curvature to discrete metric spaces by measuring how the Wasserstein distance between probability measures transported from neighboring vertices compares to the graph distance. For a locally finite graph G = (V, E) equipped with the shortest‑path metric d and a lazy random walk kernel mx (the probability measure of one step from vertex x), the Ollivier–Ricci curvature of an edge (x, y) is defined as

κ(x, y) = 1 − W1(mx, my) / d(x, y),

where W1 is the 1‑Wasserstein distance. This definition, introduced by Yann Ollivier in 2009, captures the idea that positive curvature makes nearby random walks converge faster than in flat space. Lin, Lu, and Yau (2011) developed a combinatorial version for graphs without lazy walks, showing that κ ≥ 0 implies a spectral gap for the normalized Laplacian and a logarithmic Sobolev inequality. The curvature is computable in polynomial time via linear programming, and it has become a tool for community detection, graph classification, and the analysis of graph neural networks.

Key properties distinguish Ollivier–Ricci curvature from other discrete curvatures such as Forman’s Ricci curvature. Forman’s curvature is purely combinatorial and depends only on the number of triangles and squares incident to an edge, whereas Ollivier’s curvature incorporates the global transport geometry through the Wasserstein distance. Moreover, Ollivier–Ricci curvature satisfies a Bonnet–Myers type diameter bound: if κ ≥ K > 0 for all edges, then the graph diameter is at most ⌊2/K⌋. This mirrors the classical Riemannian result and provides a concrete link between discrete and smooth curvature theories.

In applications, the curvature can be used to define a Ricci flow on graphs that smooths the network structure, and it has been employed to accelerate mixing of Markov chains by reweighting edges according to their curvature. Examiners often test the distinction between the lazy and non‑lazy definitions, the relationship between κ ≥ 0 and the spectral gap, and the fact that Ollivier–Ricci curvature is not a local quantity in the strict sense because the optimal transport plan may involve vertices at distance greater than one. A common twist is to ask whether non‑negative Ollivier–Ricci curvature implies non‑negative Bakry–Émery curvature; the answer is negative in general, as the two notions coincide only on certain product spaces.

Wasserstein Distance for Persistence Diagrams in Topological Data Analysis

Persistence diagrams arise from persistent homology as multisets of points (b, d) in the extended upper half‑plane, where b is the birth time and d the death time of a topological feature. The p‑Wasserstein distance between two diagrams D1 and D2 is defined by

Wp(D1, D2) = ( infγ ∑(u,v)∈γ ‖u − v‖∞p )1/p,

where the infimum runs over all bijections γ between the two diagrams after adding arbitrarily many copies of the diagonal Δ = { (x, x) } to each diagram. The diagonal points represent features that can be created or destroyed at zero cost, ensuring the distance is well defined even when the diagrams have different cardinalities. The bottleneck distance corresponds to the limit p → ∞ and is the most widely used metric in stability theorems.

The fundamental stability result of Cohen‑Steiner, Edelsbrunner, and Harer (2007) states that for tame functions f, g on a triangulable space, the bottleneck distance between their persistence diagrams is bounded by the supremum norm ‖f − g‖∞. This Lipschitz stability makes the Wasserstein distance a robust tool for comparing shapes and for statistical inference on topological summaries. In machine learning, persistence diagrams are embedded into Hilbert spaces via kernel methods (e.g., the persistence weighted Gaussian kernel) or vectorized using persistence landscapes, enabling the use of standard classifiers.

Computationally, the exact p‑Wasserstein distance between diagrams of size n can be found in O(n3) time using the Hungarian algorithm on a cost matrix that includes diagonal matchings. For large diagrams, entropic regularization yields a Sinkhorn‑type algorithm with O(n2) complexity and controllable approximation error. Recent work by Cuturi and Doucet (2014) and by Carrière et al.
