(2017) has adapted the Sinkhorn iteration to the diagonal‑augmented setting, preserving the geometric interpretation of the transport plan. Examiners frequently ask for the definition of the diagonal augmentation, the difference between bottleneck and Wasserstein distances, and the statement of the stability theorem. A typical trap is to assume that the optimal bijection never matches a point to the diagonal when the diagrams have equal cardinality; in fact, diagonal matchings can reduce the cost even when a perfect off‑diagonal matching exists.

Worked Example: Computing 1‑Wasserstein Distance Between Two Diagrams
Let D₁ = {(0, 2), (1, 3)} and D₂ = {(0.5, 2.5), (1.5, 3.5)}. Compute W₁(D₁, D₂) using the L∞ ground metric and allowing matches to the diagonal Δ = {(x, x)}.
We construct the cost matrix between the four off‑diagonal points and the diagonal copies. For each point u = (b, d), its distance to the diagonal is (d − b)/2 under L∞. For D₁: (0,2) → diag cost = 1; (1,3) → diag cost = 1. For D₂: (0.5,2.5) → diag cost = 1; (1.5,3.5) → diag cost = 1. Off‑diagonal pairwise L∞ distances: ‖(0,2)−(0.5,2.5)‖∞ = 0.5; ‖(0,2)−(1.5,3.5)‖∞ = 1.5; ‖(1,3)−(0.5,2.5)‖∞ = 0.5; ‖(1,3)−(1.5,3.5)‖∞ = 0.5. The optimal bijection matches (0,2)(0.5,2.5) (cost 0.5) and (1,3)(1.5,3.5) (cost 0.5). Total cost = 1.0. No diagonal matches are used. Hence W₁(D₁, D₂) = 1.0.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks for the Wasserstein distance between persistence diagrams, first write down the diagonal augmentation rule explicitly. Then compute the cost matrix including diagonal entries. If the diagrams are small, solve the assignment problem by inspection; if large, mention the Hungarian algorithm or entropic regularization. Always state the stability theorem (bottleneck distance ≤ ‖f−g‖∞) when asked about robustness.

The Hellinger–Kantorovich Metric and Riemannian Structure of Unbalanced Optimal Transport

Classical optimal transport preserves total mass, but many applications — such as image registration with varying illumination, cell growth models, and resource allocation with creation/destruction — require a framework that allows mass to change. The Hellinger–Kantorovich (HK) distance, also known as the Wasserstein–Fisher–Rao metric, provides a geometrically natural extension. For two non‑negative Radon measures μ, ν on a Polish space X, the HK distance is defined via the dynamic Benamou–Brenier formulation

HK²(μ, ν) = inf ∫₀¹ ∫X ( |vt|² + ½ |rt|² ) dρt dt,

where the infimum runs over curves (ρt) of measures and vector fields vt, scalar fields rt satisfying the continuity equation with reaction ∂tρt + div(ρtvt) = ρtrt, with endpoints ρ₀ = μ, ρ₁ = ν. The term |v|² accounts for transport, while |r|² penalizes mass creation/annihilation. This formulation, developed by Liero, Mielke, and Savaré (2016) and independently by Chizat, Peyré, Schmitzer, and Vialard (2018), endows the cone of non‑negative measures with a formal Riemannian structure: the tangent space at ρ consists of pairs (v, r) modulo the kernel of the continuity operator, and the metric tensor is ⟨(v, r), (v', r')⟩ρ = ∫ (v·v' + ½ r r') dρ.

Geodesics in this space are given by the McCann interpolation with an additional exponential scaling of densities, and the space has non‑negative curvature in the sense of Alexandrov when the base space X is a Riemannian manifold with non‑negative sectional curvature. The HK distance metrizes the weak convergence of measures plus convergence of total masses, and it satisfies a Talagrand‑type inequality HK²(μ, ν) ≤ 2 Ent(μ|ν) when the reference measure satisfies a logarithmic Sobolev inequality. In imaging, the HK metric is used for unbalanced registration where the total intensity differs between template and target; the reaction term naturally models appearance/disappearance of features. Examiners often test the distinction between the HK distance and the standard Wasserstein distance (the latter is recovered when total masses are equal and the reaction term is forced to zero), the form of the continuity equation with reaction, and the fact that the HK distance is not a true distance on the space of probability measures because it does not vanish only for equal measures — it vanishes only when both the transport and reaction costs are zero, which requires μ = ν as measures.

Optimal Transport for the Keller–Segel Chemotaxis Model: Gradient Flow Structure and Blow‑Up Analysis

The Keller–Segel system describes the collective motion of cells attracted by a self‑secreted chemical signal. In its parabolic‑elliptic form on ℝ²,

∂tρ = Δρ − ∇·(ρ ∇c), −Δc = ρ,

the cell density ρ evolves as the Wasserstein gradient flow of the free energy functional

ℰ(ρ) = ∫ ρ log ρ dx − ½ ∫ ρ c dx, where c = (−Δ)⁻¹ρ.

This gradient flow structure was identified by Blanchet, Carrillo, and Masmoudi (2008) and later refined by Carrillo, DiFrancesco, and Figalli (2010). The first term is the Boltzmann entropy, which is displacement convex in the sense of McCann; the second term is the interaction energy with the Newtonian kernel, which is displacement concave. The competition between these two effects leads to a critical mass phenomenon: if the total mass M = ∫ ρ dx is less than 8π, the free energy is bounded below and global weak solutions exist; if M > 8π, solutions blow up in finite time, concentrating mass into a Dirac delta. The threshold 8π is sharp and corresponds to the best constant in the logarithmic Hardy–Littlewood–Sobolev inequality.

The JKO minimizing movement scheme provides a constructive proof of existence and a numerical method: given a time step τ, define ρk+1 as the minimizer of

ρ ↦ (1/2τ) W₂²(ρ, ρk) + ℰ(ρ).

Because ℰ is not displacement convex, the standard convergence theory for JKO does not apply directly; however, the scheme can be shown to converge to a weak solution using the energy dissipation inequality and compactness arguments. Numerically, the scheme is implemented via entropic regularization of the Wasserstein term, leading to a Sinkhorn‑type iteration that handles the non‑convex interaction energy through a fixed‑point update of the potential c. Examiners frequently ask for the expression of the free energy, the critical mass 8π, the reason why displacement convexity fails (the interaction term is concave), and the interpretation of the blow‑up as a concentration of mass. A common trap is to confuse the parabolic‑elliptic Keller–Segel model with the porous medium equation ∂tρ = Δρm, which is the gradient flow of the Rényi entropy and is globally well‑posed for all masses. Another twist is to ask whether the JKO scheme for Keller–Segel preserves the total mass exactly; the answer is yes, because the Wasserstein distance is defined on the space of probability measures with fixed mass, but the continuous model allows mass concentration without loss of total mass.

  • Confusing Ollivier–Ricci curvature with Forman curvature; they coincide only on very special graphs.
  • Assuming the bottleneck distance equals the 1‑Wasserstein distance for persistence diagrams; they are different metrics with different stability constants.
  • Thinking the Hellinger–Kantorovich distance reduces to the Wasserstein distance whenever the total masses are equal; the reaction term can still be active if the optimal plan uses creation/annihilation to lower transport cost.
  • Believing the Keller–Segel free energy is displacement convex for all masses; it is convex only below the critical mass 8π.
  • Assuming the JKO scheme for unbalanced transport uses the standard Wasserstein distance; it must use the Hellinger–Kantorovich distance to allow mass variation.
  • Overlooking that diagonal points in persistence diagrams have zero persistence and can be matched at zero cost only in the bottleneck distance, not in the p‑Wasserstein distance for finite p.
  • Treating the Ollivier–Ricci curvature as a purely local quantity; the optimal transport plan may involve vertices at distance greater than one, making it a non‑local curvature notion.

The Heat Flow as the Gradient Flow of the Entropy in Wasserstein Space and the Bakry–Émery Theory

The heat equation ∂ₜρ = Δρ on ℝⁿ can be interpreted as the steepest descent of the Boltzmann entropy ℰ(ρ) = ∫ ρ log ρ dx with respect to the quadratic Wasserstein distance W₂. This identification, due to Jordan, Kinderlehrer, and Otto (1998), provides a variational formulation: for a time step τ > 0, the JKO scheme defines ρₖ₊₁ as the minimizer of ρ ↦ (1/2τ) W₂²(ρ, ρₖ) + ℰ(ρ). As τ → 0, the piecewise constant interpolation converges to the heat flow. The Bakry–Émery Γ₂ calculus gives a synthetic Ricci curvature lower bound: if the underlying space satisfies the curvature‑dimension condition CD(K, ∞), then the entropy is K‑displacement convex, which yields the exponential decay of the relative entropy along the heat flow, Ent(ρₜ|γ) ≤ e⁻²ᴷᵗ Ent(ρ₀|γ), where γ is the Gaussian invariant measure. On a Riemannian manifold, CD(K, ∞) is equivalent to Ric ≥ K. The Bakry–Émery condition also implies the logarithmic Sobolev inequality Ent(ρ|γ) ≤ (1/2K) I(ρ|γ), where I is the Fisher information. This inequality is equivalent to the hypercontractivity of the heat semigroup and to the Talagrand transportation cost inequality W₂²(ρ, γ) ≤ (2/K) Ent(ρ|γ). The gradient flow structure extends to the porous medium equation ∂ₜρ = Δρᵐ (m > 1) as the gradient flow of the Rényi entropy ℰₘ(ρ) = (1/(m−1)) ∫ ρᵐ dx, and to the Fokker–Planck equation with a confining potential V as the gradient flow of the free energy ℰ(ρ) = ∫ ρ log ρ dx + ∫ V ρ dx. The JKO scheme provides a constructive proof of existence, uniqueness, and regularity for these flows, and it underlies many numerical methods based on entropic regularization.

The Fokker–Planck Equation as a Wasserstein Gradient Flow and the Logarithmic Sobolev Inequality

The Fokker–Planck equation ∂ₜρ = ∇·(ρ ∇V) + Δρ describes the evolution of the law of the overdamped Langevin diffusion dXₜ = −∇V(Xₜ) dt + √2 dWₜ. Its stationary distribution is the Gibbs measure γ = Z⁻¹ e⁻ⱽ dx. Otto (2001) showed that this equation is the Wasserstein gradient flow of the free energy functional ℱ(ρ) = ∫ ρ log ρ dx + ∫ V ρ dx = Ent(ρ|γ) + log Z. The dissipation of ℱ along the flow is exactly the Fisher information I(ρ|γ) = ∫ |∇ log(ρ/γ)|² ρ dx. If the potential V is uniformly convex with ∇²V ≥ K > 0, then the Bakry–Émery criterion gives CD(K, ∞), which implies the logarithmic Sobolev inequality Ent(ρ|γ) ≤ (1/2K) I(ρ|γ). Integrating this differential inequality yields the exponential convergence Ent(ρₜ|γ) ≤ e⁻²ᴷᵗ Ent(ρ₀|γ). The same curvature bound also yields the Talagrand inequality W₂²(ρ, γ) ≤ (2/K) Ent(ρ|γ) and the HWI inequality Ent(ρ|γ) ≤ W₂(ρ, γ) √I(ρ|γ) − (K/2) W₂²(ρ, γ). These functional inequalities are equivalent to the displacement convexity of the entropy and to the contractivity of the heat semigroup in W₂. The JKO scheme for the Fokker–Planck equation reads ρₖ₊₁ = argmin ρ { (1/2τ) W₂²(ρ, ρₖ) + ℱ(ρ) }. When V is quadratic, the minimizer can be computed explicitly via a Gaussian convolution; for general V, entropic regularization leads to a Sinkhorn‑type iteration that converges to the JKO step. The gradient flow structure also provides a natural framework for the study of metastability: the Eyring–Kramers law for the spectral gap of the generator can be derived from the geometry of the Wasserstein space and the landscape of ℱ.

Worked Example: Exponential Convergence in Relative Entropy
Let γ be the standard Gaussian measure on ℝⁿ and let ρₜ solve the Fokker–Planck equation ∂ₜρ = Δρ + ∇·(ρ x) with initial density ρ₀. Show that Ent(ρₜ|γ) ≤ e⁻²ᵗ Ent(ρ₀|γ).
The equation is the gradient flow of ℱ(ρ) = Ent(ρ|γ) with respect to W₂. The potential V(x) = |x|²/2 satisfies ∇²V = I ≥ 1, so the Bakry–Émery curvature is K = 1. The CD(1, ∞) condition implies the logarithmic Sobolev inequality Ent(ρ|γ) ≤ (1/2) I(ρ|γ). Along the flow, d/dt Ent(ρₜ|γ) = −I(ρₜ|γ) ≤ −2 Ent(ρₜ|γ). Grönwall's lemma gives Ent(ρₜ|γ) ≤ e⁻²ᵗ Ent(ρ₀|γ).
Exam Strategy: Recognizing Gradient Flow Structures
When a PDE is presented as ∂ₜρ = ∇·(ρ ∇(δℰ/δρ)), identify the energy ℰ and the metric. For quadratic cost, the metric is W₂. Check if the energy is displacement convex (CD(K, ∞)). If K > 0, exponential convergence in entropy and W₂ follows. Memorize the three equivalent inequalities: logarithmic Sobolev, Talagrand, and HWI. Examiners often ask to derive one from another using the gradient flow dissipation identity.

McKean–Vlasov Equations and the Propagation of Chaos in Wasserstein Space

McKean–Vlasov equations describe the mean‑field limit of interacting particle systems. The typical form is ∂ₜρₜ = ∇·(ρₜ ∇(V + W * ρₜ)) + Δρₜ, where V is a confining potential and W is an interaction potential. The law ρₜ of a typical particle solves this nonlinear Fokker–Planck equation, which is the Wasserstein gradient flow of the free energy ℰ(ρ) = ∫ ρ log ρ dx + ∫ V ρ dx + ½ ∫∫ W(x−y) ρ(x) ρ(y) dx dy. The interaction term is displacement convex if W is convex; otherwise it may be concave, leading to phase transitions. The propagation of chaos result (Sznitman, 1991) states that the empirical measure of N independent particles driven by the same McKean–Vlasov dynamics converges in law to ρₜ as N → ∞, with a rate of order 1/√N in W₂ under suitable regularity. The proof uses the coupling method: construct N independent copies of the nonlinear process and compare them with the N‑particle system. The Wasserstein distance between the empirical measure and the limit satisfies a differential inequality that yields the convergence rate. For singular interactions such as the Coulomb potential in dimension d ≥ 3, the well‑posedness theory requires the concept of renormalized solutions and the use of the modulated energy method (Serfaty, 2017). The JKO scheme for McKean–Vlasov equations involves minimizing (1/2τ) W₂²(ρ, ρₖ) + ℰ(ρ). When W is convex, the scheme is convex and converges to the unique gradient flow. For non‑convex W, the scheme may select a local minimizer; the long‑time behavior can exhibit multiple stationary states and hysteresis. The gradient flow structure also provides a natural framework for the study of phase transitions: the free energy landscape in Wasserstein space can have multiple wells, and the dynamics can be analyzed via the Eyring–Kramers law for the transition rates between metastable states.

Caffarelli's Contraction Theorem and the Gaussian Isoperimetric Inequality

Caffarelli's contraction theorem (2000) states that if μ = e⁻ⱽ dx is a log‑concave probability measure on ℝⁿ with ∇²V ≥ I (i.e., V is 1‑convex), then the Brenier map T pushing the standard Gaussian γ forward to μ is 1‑Lipschitz: |T(x) − T(y)| ≤ |x − y| for all x, y. Equivalently, the optimal transport map for the quadratic cost from γ to μ contracts distances. This result has profound consequences for functional inequalities. It implies the Gaussian isoperimetric inequality: among all sets of given Gaussian measure, half‑spaces minimize the Gaussian surface measure. The proof uses the fact that the Brenier map is the gradient of a convex function φ solving the Monge–Ampère equation det D²φ = e⁻ⱽ⁽∇φ⁾ / e⁻|ˣ|²/². The contraction property follows from the maximum principle applied to the matrix D²φ. Caffarelli's theorem also yields the Brascamp–Lieb inequality Var_μ(f) ≤ ∫ |∇f|² dμ for any smooth f, and the Poincaré inequality with constant 1. Moreover, it implies the log‑Sobolev inequality for μ with constant 1, and the Talagrand inequality W₂²(ν, μ) ≤ 2 Ent(ν|μ) for any ν ≪ μ. These inequalities are sharp and are attained by half‑spaces or linear functions. The contraction theorem extends to the case where μ is a perturbation of the Gaussian by a convex potential: if V = |x|²/2 + Ψ with Ψ convex, then the optimal map from γ to μ is still 1‑Lipschitz. This provides a robust method to transfer functional inequalities from the Gaussian to a large class of log‑concave measures. The theorem also plays a central role in the proof of the Gaussian correlation inequality (Royen, 2014) via the Ehrhard inequality, which is a consequence of the contraction property of the heat flow on the Ornstein–Uhlenbeck semigroup.

Martingale Optimal Transport in Continuous Time and the Stochastic Control Formulation

Martingale optimal transport (MOT) seeks a martingale coupling between two given marginal distributions μ₀, μ₁ that minimizes the expected cost E[c(X₀, X₁)]. In continuous time, the problem is formulated as a stochastic control problem: find a martingale diffusion dXₜ = σₜ dWₜ with X₀ ∼ μ₀, X₁ ∼ μ₁ that minimizes E[∫₀¹ L(t, Xₜ, σₜ) dt + c(X₀, X₁)]. The dual formulation involves a pair of functions (φ, ψ) and a process Zₜ such that φ(x) + ψ(y) + ∫₀¹ Zₜ·dXₜ ≤ c(x, y) for all martingales X. The dual value equals the primal value under appropriate conditions (Beiglböck, Henry‑Labordère, Penkner, 2013). The continuous‑time MOT is closely related to the Skorokhod embedding problem: given a Brownian motion B and a target distribution μ, find a stopping time τ such that B_τ ∼ μ. The optimal embedding minimizes E[c(B₀, B_τ)] and can be characterized by the Root barrier or the Rost barrier. In the multi‑marginal setting, one seeks a martingale (Xₜ)ₜ∈[0,1] with prescribed marginals at times t₁ < … < tₙ. The dual problem involves a family of functions and a martingale integrand. The continuous‑time formulation allows the use of stochastic calculus tools such as Itô's formula and the martingale representation theorem. It also connects to robust finance: the super‑replication price of an exotic option under model uncertainty equals the MOT value. The numerical solution of continuous‑time MOT can be approached via the discretization of the stochastic control problem, leading to a sequence of semi‑discrete optimal transport problems that can be solved by linear programming or by the Sinkhorn algorithm with a martingale constraint. Recent work (Backhoff‑Veraguas, Beiglböck, Lin, Zalashko, 2020) established the existence of optimal martingale transports for general cost functions and the stability of the value with respect to the marginals in the Wasserstein topology.

  • Confusing the heat flow gradient flow with the porous medium equation gradient flow; the former uses Boltzmann entropy, the latter uses Rényi entropy.
  • Assuming the logarithmic Sobolev inequality holds for any convex potential; it requires uniform convexity (∇²V ≥ K > 0) or a Bakry–Émery CD(K, ∞) condition.
  • Thinking propagation of chaos holds for singular interactions without additional regularity; the Coulomb case needs renormalized solutions and modulated energy.
  • Believing Caffarelli's contraction theorem applies to any log‑concave measure; it requires the target measure to be more log‑concave than the Gaussian (∇²V ≥ I).
  • Treating martingale optimal transport as a standard optimal transport problem; the martingale constraint makes the dual formulation involve a stochastic integral and the primal problem a stochastic control problem.
  • Assuming the continuous‑time MOT value equals the discrete‑time MOT value for the same marginals; the continuous‑time problem allows more flexibility and typically yields a lower cost.
  • Overlooking that the JKO scheme for McKean–Vlasov with non‑convex interaction may converge to a local minimizer, not the global gradient flow.

Optimal Transport on Infinite‑Dimensional Spaces: Wiener Space and Gaussian Measures

Classical optimal transport theory is built on finite‑dimensional Euclidean spaces, but many stochastic models live on infinite‑dimensional path spaces such as the Wiener space C[0,1] equipped with the Wiener measure γ. The Monge problem on Wiener space asks for a map T pushing γ forward to a target measure μ while minimizing the quadratic cost ∫‖x−T(x)‖²H dγ(x), where ‖·‖H is the Cameron‑Martin norm. When μ is absolutely continuous with respect to γ and has a log‑concave density, the Brenier theorem extends: there exists a unique optimal map T = ∇Φ where Φ is a convex functional on the Cameron‑Martin space H. The Monge–Ampère equation becomes a determinant identity on the Malliavin covariance matrix, and its solvability relies on the infinite‑dimensional version of Caffarelli’s contraction theorem proved by Feyel and Üstünel (2004). In particular, if μ = e−Vγ with V convex on H, the optimal map is 1‑Lipschitz in the Cameron‑Martin norm. This contraction yields Gaussian isoperimetric inequalities on Wiener space and implies Talagrand’s transportation‑cost inequality W₂²(ν,γ) ≤ 2 Ent(ν‖γ) for all ν ≪ γ. The same structure appears for abstract Gaussian measures on separable Banach spaces: the optimal transport between two centered Gaussian measures N(0,C₀) and N(0,C₁) is linear, given by T = C₁1/2C₀−1/2, and the Wasserstein distance has the closed form W₂² = Tr(C₀ + C₁ − 2(C₀1/2C₁C₀1/2)1/2). These formulas are the backbone of Bayesian inverse problems where the prior and posterior are Gaussian on function spaces.

Dynamic Optimal Transport with State Constraints and Obstacle Problems

The Benamou–Brenier dynamic formulation expresses the squared Wasserstein distance as the minimal action ∫₀¹∫ |vₜ|² dρₜ dt over curves (ρₜ,vₜ) solving the continuity equation ∂ₜρₜ + ∇·(ρₜvₜ)=0. When the density ρₜ is required to stay inside a convex domain Ω ⊂ ℝⁿ (state constraint) or to avoid a moving obstacle, the problem becomes a constrained optimal control problem in the space of measures. The first‑order optimality conditions lead to a Hamilton–Jacobi–Bellman equation with a state‑constraint boundary condition: the value function u(t,x) satisfies ∂ₜu + ½|∇u|² = 0 in (0,1)×Ω with Neumann condition ∂nu=0 on ∂Ω. The associated optimal velocity is vₜ = ∇u(t,·). If the constraint is an obstacle K(t) that the mass cannot enter, the continuity equation is complemented by a reflection measure on the moving boundary, yielding a Skorokhod‑type problem for the characteristic flow. Existence of minimizers follows from the direct method in the space of measures with the narrow topology, while uniqueness of the velocity field holds under a displacement convexity assumption on the constraint set. Numerically, the problem can be discretized by a staggered grid scheme that enforces the constraint via a projection step, or by a penalized formulation adding an indicator functional of the admissible set to the action. Recent work by Figalli and Gigli (2010) shows that the constrained dynamic problem is equivalent to a static optimal transport problem with a modified cost that incorporates the geodesic distance inside the admissible region.

Worked Example: Constrained Dynamic Transport on a Convex Domain
Let Ω = {x ∈ ℝ² : |x| ≤ 1} be the unit disk. Consider the dynamic optimal transport problem with quadratic cost, initial density ρ₀ = uniform on Ω, final density ρ₁ = uniform on Ω, and the state constraint ρₜ supported in Ω for all t ∈ [0,1]. Show that the constant velocity field vₜ ≡ 0 is optimal and compute the minimal action.
Because ρ₀ = ρ₁, the static optimal transport map is the identity, giving W₂²(ρ₀,ρ₁)=0. The Benamou–Brenier formulation minimizes ∫₀¹∫_Ω |vₜ|² ρₜ dx dt subject to ∂ₜρₜ+∇·(ρₜ vₜ)=0, ρ₀=ρ₁=uniform. Choosing vₜ≡0 and ρₜ≡uniform satisfies the continuity equation (∂ₜρₜ=0, ∇·(ρₜ vₜ)=0) and respects the state constraint. The action evaluates to ∫₀¹∫_Ω 0 dx dt = 0. By non‑negativity of the integrand, no other admissible pair can achieve a lower value, so the minimal action is 0 and the constant zero velocity is optimal.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a dynamic OT problem includes a state constraint, first check whether the static optimal plan already satisfies the constraint. If the initial and final measures are identical and supported inside the admissible set, the zero‑velocity solution is always feasible and optimal because the action functional is non‑negative. This shortcut avoids solving a Hamilton–Jacobi equation in many exam questions.

Wasserstein Gradient Flows for Nonlocal Interaction Energies with Singular Kernels

Gradient flows in the Wasserstein space (𝒫₂(ℝⁿ),W₂) generate a large class of evolution equations of the form ∂ₜρ = ∇·(ρ ∇(δℰ/δρ)), where ℰ(ρ) = ∫ V dρ + ½∬ W(x−y) dρ(x)dρ(y) is an interaction energy. When the interaction kernel W is singular at the origin — for instance the Riesz kernel W(x)=|x|−s with 0<s<n, or the Newtonian potential W(x)=−log|x| in two dimensions — the energy is not displacement convex in the classical sense. Nevertheless, the JKO minimizing movement scheme ρ<sub>k+1 ∈ argminρ { W₂²(ρ,ρk)/(2τ) + ℰ(ρ) } is well‑posed for sufficiently small time step τ because the singularity is integrable against the quadratic transport cost. The limit τ→0 yields a weak solution of the aggregation equation ∂ₜρ = ∇·(ρ ∇W*ρ). For the Keller–Segel chemotaxis model in two dimensions, W is the Newtonian potential and the energy ℰ(ρ)=∫ ρ log ρ dx − ½∬ log|x−y| dρ(x)dρ(y) is λ‑displacement convex only for subcritical mass M<8π. Above the critical mass, the JKO scheme still produces a curve of measures but the limit exhibits finite‑time blow‑up, reflecting the loss of convexity. Recent results by Carrillo, DiFrancesco, and Figalli (2019) establish a refined λ‑convexity along generalized geodesics that captures the singular interaction and yields quantitative convergence rates for the scheme. Numerically, the singular kernel is regularized at scale ε and the JKO step is solved via a semi‑discrete optimal transport formulation using power diagrams, with ε→0 after the time discretization.

Optimal Transport for Uncertainty Quantification in PDE‑Constrained Optimization

In Bayesian inverse problems governed by a PDE, the posterior measure μy on the parameter space 𝒳 is often approximated by a Gaussian or by a pushforward of a reference measure via an optimal transport map. The Wasserstein distance provides a natural metric to quantify the discrepancy between the true posterior and its approximation. If the forward map G:𝒳→𝒴 is Fréchet differentiable and the prior μ₀ is Gaussian, the posterior is log‑concave under a quadratic likelihood, and the Brenier map T pushing μ₀ to μy solves a Monge–Ampère equation with a Hessian that involves the Gauss‑Newton approximation of the data‑misfit Hessian. The squared Wasserstein distance W₂²(μy,ν) between the posterior and a Gaussian approximation ν = N(m,Σ) can be bounded by the Kullback–Leibler divergence via Talagrand’s inequality W₂² ≤ 2 KL, which holds because the prior satisfies a log‑Sobolev inequality. This bound is used to design adaptive transport‑based samplers: one constructs a sequence of maps Tk that progressively reduce the KL divergence, each step being a solution of a regularized Monge–Ampère equation. In high‑dimensional settings, the map is restricted to a low‑dimensional active subspace identified by the dominant eigenvectors of the prior‑preconditioned Hessian, leading to a reduced‑order transport problem that retains the most informative directions for uncertainty propagation. The approach has been applied to subsurface flow, where the permeability field is a log‑Gaussian random field, and to cardiac electrophysiology, where the conductivity tensor is inferred from voltage measurements.

Optimal Transport for Robust Statistics and Outlier Detection

Classical robust statistics replaces the empirical measure by a weighted version that down‑weights outliers. Optimal transport offers a geometric alternative: given a contaminated sample measure μ̂ = (1−ε)μ + ε νout, one seeks a clean measure μ* close to μ̂ in Wasserstein distance while enforcing structural constraints such as moment bounds or support restrictions. The projection of μ̂ onto a convex set 𝒞 ⊂ 𝒫₂(ℝⁿ) in the Wasserstein metric, μ* = argminμ∈𝒞 W₂(μ,μ̂), yields a robust estimator that automatically removes mass located far from the bulk. When 𝒞 is the set of measures with a given mean and covariance, the projection has a closed form: the optimal map is an affine transformation that matches the first two moments, and the Wasserstein distance reduces to the Bures–Wasserstein distance between Gaussian approximations. For general 𝒞, the projection can be computed via a primal‑dual algorithm that alternates between a Wasserstein proximal step (a JKO step with indicator of 𝒞) and a gradient step on the dual potential. This framework extends to multivariate functional data by working on the space of square‑integrable functions with the L²‑Wasserstein metric. Theoretical guarantees show that the projected estimator achieves a breakdown point of ½ and attains the minimax rate of convergence for Huber’s contamination model. In practice, the method is implemented by discretizing the measures on a grid and solving the resulting linear program with a network simplex, or by using entropic regularization and the Sinkhorn algorithm for large‑scale problems.

  • Assuming the Brenier map on Wiener space is always linear; it is linear only for Gaussian‑to‑Gaussian transport, not for general log‑concave targets.
  • Ignoring the Neumann boundary condition in the Hamilton–Jacobi equation for state‑constrained dynamic OT; the optimal velocity must be tangent to the boundary.
  • Treating singular interaction kernels as if they were displacement convex for all masses; the Keller–Segel model loses λ‑convexity above the critical mass 8π.
  • Applying Talagrand’s inequality W₂² ≤ 2 KL without verifying a log‑Sobolev inequality for the reference measure; the Gaussian prior satisfies it, but a heavy‑tailed prior may not.
  • Believing the Wasserstein projection onto a moment‑constrained set always yields a Gaussian; the projection is Gaussian only when the constraint set is exactly the set of measures with prescribed mean and covariance.
  • Confusing the Bures–Wasserstein distance on Gaussian measures with the standard Wasserstein distance on the same space; they coincide only for centered Gaussians with commuting covariances.
  • Overlooking that entropic regularization of the robust projection introduces a bias that does not vanish as the regularization parameter tends to zero unless the constraint set is a singleton.

Optimal Transport for Uncertainty Quantification: Transport Maps and Polynomial Chaos Expansions

Uncertainty quantification (UQ) seeks to propagate input uncertainties through a computational model to obtain statistics of the output. When the input is a random field with a known prior distribution μ₀ and the model defines a pushforward map G, the output distribution is ν = G♯μ₀. In high dimensions, direct sampling of ν is prohibitive. Optimal transport provides a deterministic alternative: construct a transport map T that pushes a simple reference measure (typically a standard Gaussian γ) onto the target ν, i.e. T♯γ = ν. The map T is the gradient of a convex potential φ solving the Monge–Ampère equation det D²φ(x) = γ(x)/ν(∇φ(x)). In practice φ is approximated in a finite‑dimensional basis, often using polynomial chaos expansions (PCE) of the form φ(x) = Σₐ cₐ Ψₐ(x) where Ψₐ are multivariate Hermite polynomials orthogonal w.r.t. γ. The coefficients cₐ are determined by minimizing a loss functional derived from the Kantorovich dual, such as the expected squared error 𝔼[|∇φ(X) − Y|²] with X∼γ, Y∼ν. This yields a convex optimization problem because the objective is convex in the coefficients when the cost is quadratic. The resulting map T = ∇φ is then used for fast sampling, sensitivity analysis, and Bayesian inference: the posterior density π(θ|y) ∝ exp(−½‖G(θ)−y‖²) π₀(θ) can be represented as the pushforward of the prior by a transport map that solves a regularized Monge–Ampère equation where the right‑hand side involves the likelihood. The approach has been applied to subsurface flow with log‑Gaussian permeability fields, where the Karhunen–Loève expansion reduces the prior to a finite set of Gaussian coefficients, and to cardiac electrophysiology where the conductivity tensor is inferred from voltage measurements. Theoretical guarantees rely on the displacement convexity of the Kullback–Leibler divergence along Wasserstein geodesics, which ensures that the optimization landscape has no spurious local minima when the target is log‑concave.

The Monge–Ampère Equation on Riemannian Manifolds with Boundary: Regularity and Neumann Conditions

On a compact Riemannian manifold (M,g) with smooth boundary ∂M, the optimal transport problem with quadratic cost c(x,y)=½d²(x,y) leads to a Monge–Ampère type equation for the Kantorovich potential φ. The optimal map is T(x)=expₓ(∇φ(x)) where exp is the Riemannian exponential map. The potential satisfies det(D²φ + D²ₓc(x,T(x))) = μ(x)/ν(T(x)) det(DT(x)). When ∂M is non‑empty, the transport map must satisfy a Neumann‑type boundary condition: the optimal velocity field v(x)=∇φ(x) is tangent to the boundary, i.e. ⟨v(x), n(x)⟩ = 0 for x∈∂M, where n is the outward unit normal. This condition arises because mass cannot leave the domain; it is equivalent to requiring that the pushforward of μ by T stays supported in M. Regularity theory for this boundary value problem was developed by Figalli, Kim, and McCann (2011) and later refined by Chen, Liu, and Wang. They proved that if μ and ν are bounded away from zero and infinity, and the cost satisfies the Ma–Trudinger–Wang (MTW) condition (A3w) together with a boundary convexity condition (the domain is c‑convex with respect to the target), then the potential φ is C¹,α up to the boundary and the transport map is a homeomorphism. The MTW tensor on a Riemannian manifold is given by 𝔖₍ᵢⱼₖₗ₎ = −⅔ D⁴c(·,·) evaluated on the diagonal; for the squared distance cost it reduces to the sectional curvature tensor. Positive sectional curvature implies the MTW condition holds, while negative curvature may violate it, leading to possible singularities. The Neumann condition is essential for the well‑posedness of the associated Hamilton–Jacobi equation ∂ₜu + ½|∇u|² = 0 with state constraints; the viscosity solution satisfies ∂u/∂n = 0 on ∂M. Numerical methods based on semi‑discrete approximations (power diagrams) or entropic regularization must enforce this tangency condition to avoid boundary layers that corrupt the transport plan.

Worked Example: Neumann Condition for Quadratic Cost on a Convex Domain
Let M = [0,1]² ⊂ ℝ² with Euclidean metric, μ = ν = uniform probability on M. Show that the optimal transport map for quadratic cost is the identity and verify the Neumann boundary condition.
For identical source and target measures on a convex domain, the optimal map is T(x)=x. The Kantorovich potential can be taken as φ(x)=½|x|², so ∇φ(x)=x. On the boundary ∂M, the outward normal n is (±1,0) on vertical edges and (0,±1) on horizontal edges. The inner product ⟨∇φ(x), n⟩ = ⟨x, n⟩ equals the coordinate normal to the edge. Since x lies on the edge, that coordinate is either 0 or 1. For the left edge x₁=0, n=(−1,0) gives ⟨x,n⟩=0; for the right edge x₁=1, n=(1,0) gives ⟨x,n⟩=1, which is not zero. However, the optimal map must keep mass inside M; the identity map does not move mass across the boundary, so the velocity field v(x)=∇φ(x)−x = 0. The correct Neumann condition for the dynamic formulation is that the Eulerian velocity vₜ(x) satisfies vₜ·n=0. In the static formulation, the condition is that the transport map T maps ∂M into ∂M, which holds for T(x)=x. Thus the Neumann condition is satisfied in the sense that no mass flux crosses the boundary.
Exam Strategy
When a question asks for the boundary condition in optimal transport on a manifold with boundary, recall that the static Monge problem requires T(∂M)⊂∂M, while the dynamic Benamou–Brenier formulation imposes v·n=0 on ∂M. Distinguish between the two; examiners often test whether you confuse the Neumann condition for the potential (∂φ/∂n=0) with the tangency of the velocity field.

Mean‑Field Control and the Master Equation in Wasserstein Space

Mean‑field control problems involve a representative agent optimizing a cost functional that depends on the distribution of the whole population. The value function U(t,μ) is defined on the Wasserstein space 𝒫₂(ℝᵈ) and satisfies the master equation, a first‑order PDE on the space of measures: ∂ₜU + ∫ ℝᵈ H(x, ∂ₘU(t,μ)(x)) μ(dx) + ∫ ℝᵈ ∂ₘU(t,μ)(x)·b(x,μ) μ(dx) = 0, where H is the Hamiltonian, b the drift, and ∂ₘU denotes the Lions derivative (the Wasserstein gradient). This equation was introduced by Lions in his Collège de France lectures and rigorously analyzed by Cardaliaguet, Delarue, Lasry, and Lions. The master equation is the infinite‑population limit of the Nash system for N‑player stochastic differential games; its solution provides the optimal feedback control α*(t,x,μ) = −∂ₚH(x, ∂ₘU(t,μ)(x)). The Wasserstein gradient flow structure appears when the Hamiltonian is quadratic in the momentum: H(x,p)=½|p|²+V(x). Then the master equation reduces to a Hamilton–Jacobi–Bellman equation on 𝒫₂, and the optimal control drives the population distribution along a gradient flow of the energy ℰ(μ)=∫ V dμ + Ent(μ) where Ent is the Boltzmann entropy. The well‑posedness of the master equation requires displacement convexity of the Hamiltonian and a monotonicity condition (Lasry–Lions monotonicity) on the coupling term. Recent work by Gangbo, Święch, and others extends the theory to non‑quadratic Hamiltonians and to state‑constrained domains, where the Neumann boundary condition on the master equation corresponds to reflecting the population at the boundary. Numerical approximation uses particle methods: a system of N coupled ODEs approximates the McKean–Vlasov dynamics, and the value function is approximated by a neural network trained to satisfy the master equation residual. Applications include crowd motion modeling, where agents avoid congestion, and financial mathematics, where a central bank optimizes monetary policy affecting a continuum of banks.

Information Geometry and the Fisher–Rao Metric on the Wasserstein Manifold

The space of probability densities on a manifold M carries two natural Riemannian structures: the Wasserstein metric (Otto calculus) and the Fisher–Rao metric from information geometry. The Fisher–Rao metric at a density ρ is defined by g_FR(ρ)(σ₁,σ₂) = ∫ (σ₁/ρ)(σ₂/ρ) ρ dx for tangent vectors σ₁,σ₂ with zero mean. Otto showed that the Wasserstein metric can be written as g_W(ρ)(∇φ₁,∇φ₂) = ∫ ∇φ₁·∇φ₂ ρ dx where φᵢ solve the continuity equation ∂ₜρ + ∇·(ρ∇φ)=0. The two metrics are related by the fact that the Fisher–Rao metric is the pullback of the Wasserstein metric under the square‑root map ρ ↦ 2√ρ, which embeds the probability simplex into the L² sphere. This embedding turns the Fisher–Rao geodesics into great circles on the sphere, while Wasserstein geodesics correspond to displacement interpolations. The interplay is crucial for gradient flows: the heat flow is the gradient flow of the entropy with respect to the Wasserstein metric, but it is also the gradient flow of the Fisher information with respect to the Fisher–Rao metric. The Bakry–Émery Γ₂ calculus provides a unified framework: the curvature‑dimension condition CD(K,N) can be expressed either in terms of displacement convexity of the entropy (Wasserstein) or in terms of the Γ₂ operator (Fisher–Rao). On a Riemannian manifold with Ricci curvature bounded below by K, both formulations are equivalent. The Fisher–Rao metric also appears in the unbalanced optimal transport (Hellinger–Kantorovich) where the cost includes a penalty for mass creation/annihilation; the resulting geodesic equations combine Wasserstein transport with Fisher–Rao scaling. In statistical applications, the Fisher–Rao metric yields the Cramér–Rao bound, while the Wasserstein metric yields Talagrand’s inequality W₂² ≤ 2 KL. The two inequalities are linked by the HWI inequality: W₂(μ,ν) ≤ √(2 KL(μ|ν)) + (1/2)√(I(μ|ν)) where I is the Fisher information. This inequality interpolates between the two geometries and is a cornerstone of modern functional analysis.

Entropic Regularization of the Boltzmann Equation and Hydrodynamic Limits via Optimal Transport

The Boltzmann equation ∂ₜf + v·∇ₓf = Q(f,f) describes the evolution of a rarefied gas. The collision operator Q drives the distribution f(t,x,v) towards a local Maxwellian M[ρ,u,T] with density ρ, velocity u, and temperature T. Entropic regularization of optimal transport provides a variational formulation of the hydrodynamic limit. Consider the relative entropy H(f|M) = ∫ f log(f/M) dv. The Boltzmann H‑theorem states d/dt H(f|M) ≤ 0. In the diffusive scaling (Knudsen number ε→0), the solution fε converges to a solution of the incompressible Navier–Stokes equations. This limit can be captured by a time‑discrete variational scheme: given fⁿ, define fⁿ⁺¹ as the minimizer of (1/2τ) W₂,ε²(fⁿ, f) + H(f|M) where W₂,ε is the entropically regularized Wasserstein distance with regularization parameter ε. As ε→0, W₂,ε converges to the standard Wasserstein distance, and the scheme becomes the JKO (Jordan–Kinderlehrer–Otto) scheme for the heat equation in velocity space. The hydrodynamic limit emerges when the scaling τ = ε² is chosen; the resulting macroscopic equations are the compressible Euler equations with a pressure law derived from the Maxwellian. Rigorous convergence was proved by Dolbeault, Nazaret, and Savaré (2009) for the linear Boltzmann equation and by Erbar and Maas (2012) for the nonlinear case using the gradient flow structure on the space of probability measures with the Fisher–Rao metric. The entropic regularization parameter ε plays the role of the Knudsen number; the Sinkhorn algorithm computes the regularized transport plan efficiently, enabling numerical simulation of the Boltzmann equation in high dimensions. Recent work by Carlier, Duval, and Peyré extends this to the quantum Boltzmann equation, where the state space is the set of density matrices and the cost is the quantum relative entropy. The optimal transport perspective unifies kinetic theory, fluid dynamics, and information geometry, and provides a systematic way to derive macroscopic models from microscopic dynamics.

  • Assuming the Brenier map on a manifold with boundary is always the gradient of a globally convex potential; the potential is only c‑convex and may fail to be C² up to the boundary if the MTW condition is violated.
  • Confusing the Neumann condition for the static Monge problem (T(∂M)⊂∂M) with the dynamic condition v·n=0; they are equivalent only when the transport map is induced by a velocity field tangent to the boundary.
  • Believing the master equation always has a classical solution; it may require viscosity solutions when the Hamiltonian is not displacement convex.
  • Treating the Fisher–Rao metric as a Riemannian metric on the whole Wasserstein space; it is only defined on the subset of densities with full support and is not compatible with the Wasserstein geodesics except via the square‑root embedding.
  • Overlooking that entropic regularization of the Boltzmann equation introduces a bias in the collision operator that vanishes only when the regularization parameter scales correctly with the Knudsen number.
  • Thinking the HWI inequality holds for any reference measure; it requires the reference to satisfy a log‑Sobolev inequality, which fails for heavy‑tailed distributions.
  • Neglecting the boundary convexity condition in the Monge–Ampère regularity theory; without c‑convexity of the domain the transport map can develop singularities even if the MTW tensor is positive.

Optimal Transport for Large Deviation Principles and Rate Functions

The theory of large deviations provides exponential asymptotics for probabilities of rare events. When a sequence of random probability measures satisfies a large deviation principle (LDP) on the space of probability measures equipped with the Wasserstein topology, the rate function often takes the form of a relative entropy or a kinetic energy functional. Sanov’s theorem states that the empirical measure of i.i.d. samples satisfies an LDP with rate function given by the Kullback–Leibler divergence relative to the true law. In the Wasserstein setting, the contraction principle yields LDPs for functionals such as the empirical Wasserstein distance. The rate function for the empirical Wasserstein distance between the empirical measure and the true measure is the squared Wasserstein distance itself, reflecting the fact that the Wasserstein metric metrizes weak convergence plus convergence of first moments. For Markov processes, the Donsker–Varadhan rate function can be expressed as a supremum over smooth test functions of an integral involving the generator; this variational representation is closely related to the Benamou–Brenier dynamic formulation of optimal transport. In particular, the action functional ∫₀¹ ∫ |vₜ|² dμₜ dt appears both as the kinetic energy in the dynamic formulation and as the rate function for the empirical measure of a diffusion process. The connection extends to the Freidlin–Wentzell theory for small‑noise diffusions, where the rate function is the minimum of the action over all absolutely continuous paths connecting two points; the minimizer is precisely the optimal transport geodesic in Wasserstein space when the diffusion coefficient is the identity. These links allow one to transfer regularity results from optimal transport (e.g., displacement convexity of the entropy) to large deviation estimates, and conversely to use large deviation techniques to prove concentration inequalities for empirical Wasserstein distances.

Recent work by Léonard, Mikami, and others has shown that the Schrödinger bridge problem — an entropically regularized optimal transport problem — arises naturally as the zero‑noise limit of a system of interacting Brownian particles conditioned on their empirical measure at terminal time. The rate function for the conditioned process is the relative entropy with respect to the Wiener measure, and its minimizer solves a static Schrödinger system that is equivalent to the entropic optimal transport plan. This perspective unifies optimal transport, stochastic control, and large deviations, and provides a probabilistic interpretation of the Sinkhorn algorithm as an iterative proportional fitting procedure that computes the most likely coupling under an entropy constraint.

Optimal Transport in Uncertainty Quantification and Bayesian Inverse Problems

In Bayesian inverse problems one seeks to infer an unknown parameter field u from noisy observations y = G(u) + η, where G is a forward operator and η is noise. The posterior distribution π(u|y) ∝ exp(−Φ(u;y)) π₀(u) combines a prior π₀ with a likelihood potential Φ. When the prior is a Gaussian measure on a function space, the posterior is typically non‑Gaussian and high‑dimensional. Optimal transport offers a deterministic alternative to Markov chain Monte Carlo: one constructs a transport map T that pushes the prior forward to the posterior, i.e., T♯π₀ = π. The map is obtained by solving a Monge problem with cost c(u,v) = ½‖u−v‖²H where H is the Cameron–Martin space of the prior. The Brenier theorem guarantees that the optimal map is the gradient of a convex potential φ, and the Monge–Ampère equation det(D²φ) = dπ₀/dπ ∘ ∇φ characterises φ. In practice, one approximates φ using a finite‑dimensional parameterisation (e.g., polynomial chaos, normalising flows) and minimises the Kullback–Leibler divergence between the pushforward and the target, which is equivalent to a variational formulation of optimal transport. The Wasserstein distance between the approximate and exact posterior provides a rigorous error metric that is stronger than total variation and respects the geometry of the parameter space. Moreover, the displacement convexity of the KL functional along Wasserstein geodesics yields convergence guarantees for gradient‑based optimisation of the transport map. For nonlinear forward models, the map can be built iteratively via a sequence of linearised transport problems, each corresponding to a Gauss–Newton step in the Wasserstein metric. This approach has been applied to subsurface flow, seismic imaging, and climate model calibration, where the dimension of u can exceed 10⁶ and the forward model is expensive. The transport map also enables fast sampling and uncertainty propagation: once T is known, independent draws from the prior are pushed forward to produce independent posterior samples at negligible cost.

Worked Example: Constructing a Gaussian‑to‑Gaussian Transport Map
Let π₀ = N(0, I_d) and π = N(m, Σ) with Σ positive definite. Find the optimal transport map T pushing π₀ to π for quadratic cost.
For Gaussian measures the Brenier map is affine: T(x) = m + A x where A is the unique symmetric positive definite square root of Σ, i.e., A = Σ1/2. Indeed, if X ∼ N(0, I_d) then m + Σ1/2 X ∼ N(m, Σ). The convex potential is φ(x) = ½ xᵀ Σ1/2 x + mᵀ x, whose gradient is ∇φ(x) = Σ1/2 x + m = T(x). The Monge–Ampère equation reduces to det(Σ1/2) = (det Σ)1/2 = dπ/dπ₀ ∘ T, which holds because the density ratio is exp(−½‖T(x)−m‖²Σ-1 + ½‖x‖²) and the Jacobian of T is det(Σ1/2). This closed‑form map is the basis for many Gaussian‑approximate transport methods in high dimensions.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks for the optimal map between two Gaussians, write down the affine form immediately and identify the square‑root of the target covariance. Do not attempt to solve a Monge–Ampère PDE; the Gaussian case is the only one with an explicit solution and appears frequently in both theory and application sections.

Optimal Transport for Mean‑Field Limits of Interacting Particle Systems

Consider a system of N particles with positions X_i(t) ∈ ℝ^d evolving according to the stochastic differential equation dX_i = −(1/N) ∑j≠i ∇W(X_i − X_j) dt + √2 dB_i, where W is a symmetric interaction potential and B_i are independent Brownian motions. The empirical measure μ^N_t = (1/N) ∑ δX_i(t) converges as N → ∞ to a deterministic density ρ_t solving the McKean–Vlasov equation ∂_t ρ = ∇·(ρ ∇(W * ρ)) + Δρ. This limit can be viewed as the gradient flow of the free energy functional F(ρ) = ∫ ρ log ρ + ½ ∫∫ W(x−y) ρ(x) ρ(y) dx dy in the Wasserstein space. The displacement convexity of F along Wasserstein geodesics, which holds when W is convex, guarantees uniqueness of the limit and exponential convergence to equilibrium. The JKO scheme provides a variational time discretisation: given ρ^n, define ρn+1 as the minimiser of (1/2τ) W₂²(ρ, ρ^n) + F(ρ). The Euler–Lagrange equation of this minimisation recovers the McKean–Vlasov equation in the limit τ → 0. For non‑convex W, the free energy may have multiple local minima, leading to phase transitions and metastability; the Wasserstein geometry captures the energy barriers between metastable states via the geodesic distance. Recent results by Carrillo, McCann, and Villani extend this framework to singular kernels such as the Newtonian potential (W(x) = |x|2−d) arising in gravitational and Coulomb interactions. In that case, the interaction energy is displacement convex only after adding a suitable confinement potential, and the mean‑field limit yields the Keller–Segel or Vlasov–Poisson equations. The optimal transport perspective also yields quantitative propagation‑of‑chaos estimates: the expected Wasserstein distance between μ^N_t and ρ_t is of order N-1/2 for smooth potentials, and the rate can be sharpened using the HWI inequality and the logarithmic Sobolev inequality satisfied by the invariant measure.

Optimal Transport on Manifolds with Conical Singularities

When the underlying space is a metric measure space with conical singularities — for example, a two‑dimensional cone with angle α < 2π — the standard theory of optimal transport on smooth Riemannian manifolds must be adapted. The cost function is still the squared geodesic distance, but geodesics may hit the singular tip and bifurcate. The Ma–Trudinger–Wang (MTW) tensor, which controls regularity of optimal maps, can be computed explicitly for cones and shows that the cost satisfies the MTW condition only when α ≥ π. For α < π, the MTW tensor fails to be non‑negative, and optimal maps can develop discontinuities even for smooth densities. Nevertheless, the existence and uniqueness of optimal transport plans hold under mild conditions (e.g., absolute continuity of the source measure) by the general theory on geodesic metric spaces. The Brenier theorem generalises: the optimal plan is induced by a map T = exp_x(∇φ) where φ is c‑concave and the exponential map is defined almost everywhere. Regularity results by Figalli and others show that if the source and target densities are bounded away from zero and infinity on domains that are c‑convex with respect to the cost, then the optimal map is Hölder continuous up to the singular set, with an exponent depending on the cone angle. The displacement convexity of the entropy functional on such spaces is characterised by the curvature‑dimension condition CD(K,N) in the sense of Lott–Sturm–Villani; cones satisfy CD(0,N) for N = 2/α, reflecting the synthetic Ricci curvature bound. This synthetic curvature bound enables the use of functional inequalities (Poincaré, log‑Sobolev) on singular spaces, which in turn give convergence rates for the heat flow viewed as a Wasserstein gradient flow. Numerically, semi‑discrete optimal transport on cones can be solved via power diagrams adapted to the conical metric, and the resulting transport maps are used in mesh generation and surface registration for shapes with conical singularities.

Optimal Transport for Stochastic Control and Hamilton–Jacobi–Bellman Equations

Stochastic optimal control problems of the form inf_u 𝔼[∫₀^T (½|u_t|² + V(X_t)) dt + g(X_T)] with dynamics dX_t = u_t dt + dW_t can be reformulated as an optimal transport problem on path space. The control u enters linearly in the drift, and the cost is quadratic in u, which matches the Benamou–Brenier dynamic formulation where the velocity field v_t = u_t and the action is ∫ ½|v_t|² dμ_t dt. The value function of the control problem satisfies the Hamilton–Jacobi–Bellman (HJB) equation ∂_t u + ½|∇u|² − Δu + V = 0 with terminal condition u(T,x) = g(x). The Hopf–Cole transform w = exp(−u) linearises the HJB equation into the backward heat equation ∂_t w = Δw − V w, whose solution can be represented by the Feynman–Kac formula. This linearisation is equivalent to the Schrödinger bridge problem: the optimal controlled process is the most likely path of a diffusion conditioned on its terminal distribution, and the optimal control is u_t = ∇ log w(t,X_t). The connection extends to mean‑field control, where the cost depends on the law of the state; the resulting master equation is a PDE on the Wasserstein space whose characteristics are the optimal transport geodesics. In the deterministic limit (vanishing noise), the HJB equation reduces to the classical Hamilton–Jacobi equation, and the optimal trajectories are the projections of the Hamiltonian flow, which coincide with the displacement interpolants of the optimal transport problem with cost given by the action integral. This duality provides a powerful numerical tool: the HJB equation can be solved by computing the optimal transport map between the initial and terminal distributions via the JKO scheme, and the control is recovered from the displacement interpolation. Applications include robotic path planning, portfolio optimisation with transaction costs, and crowd motion modelling where the Hamilton–Jacobi equation captures the eikonal cost of moving through a density‑dependent environment.

Common Traps for Advanced Topics

  • Assuming the large deviation rate function for empirical Wasserstein distances is always the squared Wasserstein distance; this holds only when the underlying measures have finite second moments and the topology is the weak‑plus‑moment topology.
  • Treating the Gaussian‑to‑Gaussian transport map as a generic template for all Bayesian inverse problems; the affine structure is special to Gaussian priors and likelihoods, and non‑Gaussian posteriors require iterative or parametric approximations.
  • Believing that displacement convexity of the free energy holds for any interaction potential; it requires convexity of the potential or a suitable confinement, otherwise the mean‑field limit may exhibit multiple equilibria and the gradient flow structure breaks down.
  • Ignoring the cone angle condition α ≥ π when applying MTW regularity theory on conical manifolds; for sharper cones the optimal map can be discontinuous even with smooth densities.
  • Confusing the Hopf–Cole transform for the stochastic HJB equation with the deterministic Hopf–Lax formula; the former linearises to a backward heat equation with a potential, the latter yields a variational representation without diffusion.
  • Overlooking that the Schrödinger bridge problem is an entropic regularisation of optimal transport, not a pure optimal transport problem; the regularisation parameter must scale correctly with the noise level to recover the correct stochastic control limit.
  • Assuming the Wasserstein gradient flow of the entropy on a singular space always satisfies a log‑Sobolev inequality; the synthetic curvature‑dimension condition CD(K,N) may hold with K = 0 but N finite, which yields only a Poincaré inequality, not a log‑Sobolev inequality.

Optimal Transport for Domain Adaptation in Deep Learning

Domain adaptation seeks to transfer a model trained on a source distribution to a target distribution with limited or no labels. Optimal transport provides a principled way to align the two distributions by learning a coupling that minimizes the Wasserstein distance between the source feature representation and the target feature representation. The coupling can be used to transport source samples toward the target domain, yielding a transported dataset on which a standard classifier is trained. This approach avoids adversarial training instabilities and offers a convex objective when the cost is the squared Euclidean distance.

In practice the exact optimal transport problem is replaced by an entropically regularized version solved with the Sinkhorn algorithm, which yields a differentiable transport plan suitable for back‑propagation. Mini‑batch approximations further reduce the quadratic complexity to linear in the batch size, enabling integration into stochastic gradient descent pipelines. Theoretical work shows that the generalization error on the target domain can be bounded by the Wasserstein distance between the source and target marginals plus a term depending on the hypothesis class complexity, providing a clear statistical justification for the method.

Recent extensions incorporate class‑conditional transport by adding a label‑aware cost term, ensuring that samples from the same class are matched across domains. This conditional OT formulation leads to improved performance on benchmark datasets such as Office‑31 and VisDA, and it can be combined with deep feature extractors trained end‑to‑end. The overall framework remains compatible with standard deep learning libraries because the Sinkhorn iterations are fully differentiable and can be executed on GPU.

Wasserstein Gradient Flows in Reinforcement Learning

Reinforcement learning objectives often involve maximizing an expected return while maintaining sufficient exploration. Entropy‑regularized RL can be interpreted as a gradient flow of the free energy functional in the Wasserstein space of probability measures over state‑action trajectories. The free energy consists of the negative expected return plus a temperature‑scaled entropy term, and its Wasserstein gradient flow yields a continuous‑time dynamics that coincides with the soft Bellman optimality equations.

Discretizing this flow with the Jordan–Kinderlehrer–Otto (JKO) scheme produces an implicit update rule: each iteration solves a proximal problem that minimizes the sum of the free energy and a squared Wasserstein distance to the previous policy distribution. This proximal step can be approximated by a Sinkhorn‑regularized optimal transport problem between the current policy and a reference distribution, leading to practical algorithms such as Wasserstein Policy Gradient and Wasserstein Actor‑Critic. Convergence analyses rely on the displacement convexity of the entropy and on the Lipschitz continuity of the reward function, guaranteeing that the iterates converge to a stationary point of the regularized objective.

The Wasserstein perspective also clarifies the role of exploration: the entropy term induces a diffusion in the policy space that prevents premature collapse to deterministic policies. By adjusting the temperature parameter one can trade off exploitation and exploration in a geometrically meaningful way, and the resulting policy updates are invariant to reparameterizations of the action space, a property not shared by standard Euclidean policy gradient methods.

Worked Example: Proximal Policy Update via Sinkhorn
Consider a discrete action space with three actions. The current policy distribution is π = (0.6, 0.3, 0.1). The reward vector is r = (1.0, 0.5, 0.0). The temperature is τ = 0.2 and the proximal step size is η = 0.1. Using the squared Euclidean cost between actions (cost matrix C with Cij = (i−j)²), compute one JKO proximal update approximated by a single Sinkhorn iteration with regularization ε = 0.05.
The free energy gradient step adds τ⁻¹ r to the log‑policy: log π' = log π + η τ⁻¹ r = log(0.6,0.3,0.1) + 0.1/0.2 * (1.0,0.5,0.0) = (−0.511, −1.204, −2.303) + (0.5, 0.25, 0) = (−0.011, −0.954, −2.303). Exponentiating and normalizing gives the unregularized target distribution μ = (0.49, 0.30, 0.21). The Sinkhorn scaling vectors u, v are initialized to ones. One iteration updates u_i = μ_i / Σ_j Kij v_j with K = exp(−C/ε). For ε = 0.05 the kernel is sharply peaked, so after one iteration the transport plan is close to the identity coupling. The resulting updated policy π⁺ ≈ (0.48, 0.31, 0.21). This demonstrates how the proximal step moves probability mass toward higher‑reward actions while respecting the Wasserstein geometry.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks to connect RL with optimal transport, identify the free energy functional, write its Wasserstein gradient flow, and then describe the JKO discretization. Remember that the entropy term provides displacement convexity, which is the key to convergence proofs. Mention the Sinkhorn approximation as the practical bridge to deep RL implementations.

Optimal Transport for Causal Inference and Counterfactual Estimation

Causal inference requires estimating the distribution of potential outcomes under interventions. Optimal transport offers a non‑parametric way to construct counterfactual distributions by learning a transport map that pushes the observed factual distribution toward a target interventional distribution while preserving the structural relationships encoded in a causal graph. The map is obtained by solving a constrained optimal transport problem where the cost penalizes deviations from the causal mechanisms, for example by adding a term that enforces invariance of the conditional distribution of the outcome given the parents.

Under the assumption of unconfoundedness and overlap, the optimal transport plan between the treated and control covariate distributions yields balancing weights that achieve exact covariate balance in the Wasserstein sense. These weights can be used in inverse‑probability‑weighting estimators or as importance sampling proposals for Monte‑Carlo estimation of average treatment effects. Theoretical results show that the Wasserstein distance between the weighted control distribution and the treated distribution provides a finite‑sample bound on the bias of the treatment effect estimator.

Algorithmic implementations combine the Sinkhorn algorithm with a causal regularization term, leading to a differentiable objective that can be optimized jointly with representation learning networks. This enables end‑to‑end learning of representations that are both predictive and causally invariant, a property validated on benchmark causal inference tasks such as the IHDP and ACIC datasets.

Optimal Transport for Anomaly Detection in High‑Dimensional Data

Anomaly detection can be framed as measuring the discrepancy between a test sample and a reference distribution of normal data. The Wasserstein distance between the empirical distribution of a mini‑batch and a learned normal model provides a geometrically meaningful anomaly score that is sensitive to both location and shape differences. Because the exact Wasserstein distance scales poorly with dimension, sliced Wasserstein distances are employed: the high‑dimensional distributions are projected onto a set of random one‑dimensional directions, the one‑dimensional Wasserstein distances are computed analytically, and the results are averaged.

The sliced Wasserstein anomaly score admits a concentration inequality: under the null hypothesis that the test batch is drawn from the normal distribution, the score concentrates around its expectation with sub‑Gaussian tails. This permits the construction of thresholding rules with controlled false positive rates without resorting to density estimation. In practice, a small number of projections (e.g., 50–100) yields a good trade‑off between statistical power and computational cost, and the projections can be learned to maximize detection power for specific anomaly types.

Applications include network intrusion detection, where the normal traffic distribution is modeled by a Gaussian mixture and the sliced Wasserstein score flags deviations in real time, and medical imaging, where the method detects out‑of‑distribution scans without requiring labeled anomalies. The approach is also compatible with streaming data because the reference distribution can be updated incrementally using online Sinkhorn barycenter updates.

Optimal Transport for Generative Diffusion Models

Diffusion models generate data by reversing a stochastic differential equation that gradually adds noise to the data distribution. This forward process is a gradient flow of the Kullback–Leibler divergence in the Wasserstein space, and the reverse process corresponds to the time‑reversed diffusion, which can be expressed as a Wasserstein gradient flow of the same functional with a drift given by the score function. The training objective, score matching, is equivalent to minimizing the Fisher divergence, which is the squared Wasserstein norm of the gradient of the log‑density.

The connection to optimal transport is made explicit through the Schrödinger bridge problem: the most likely path of a diffusion conditioned on its endpoint distribution is an entropically regularized optimal transport plan between the initial and terminal distributions. The regularization parameter equals the diffusion coefficient, and the optimal drift is the gradient of the log‑density of the bridge. This viewpoint yields new training algorithms that directly optimize the Schrödinger bridge objective using Sinkhorn iterations, avoiding the need for score networks and providing exact likelihood evaluation.

Recent work shows that the Wasserstein gradient flow perspective also explains the empirical success of classifier‑free guidance: the guidance term adds a potential to the free energy functional, and the resulting guided diffusion is the gradient flow of the perturbed free energy. This geometric interpretation suggests principled ways to anneal the guidance strength and to design adaptive step‑size schedules that respect the underlying Wasserstein geometry.

  • Assuming that any entropic regularization yields a convex objective; the regularized OT problem is convex only when the cost is convex and the entropy is the Shannon entropy, other entropies may break convexity.
  • Confusing the sliced Wasserstein distance with the true Wasserstein distance; sliced versions provide lower bounds but can miss high‑dimensional structure that is orthogonal to all projection directions.
  • Treating the Schrödinger bridge as a pure optimal transport problem; it is an entropic regularization whose solution converges to the OT plan only as the diffusion coefficient tends to zero.
  • Ignoring the overlap assumption in causal OT; without sufficient overlap the transport map may extrapolate beyond the support of the data, leading to unreliable counterfactuals.
  • Believing that the JKO scheme for RL converges globally for non‑convex rewards; convergence guarantees typically require displacement convexity of the free energy, which fails for general reward landscapes.
  • Using a fixed number of random projections for sliced Wasserstein anomaly detection without verifying concentration; the required number of projections grows with the intrinsic dimension of the data manifold.
  • Overlooking that the Wasserstein gradient flow of the KL divergence in diffusion models assumes a smooth density; singularities in the data distribution can cause the score to be undefined and the flow to break down.

Optimal Transport for Mean Field Control and Stochastic Games

Mean field control studies the limit of large populations of interacting agents where each agent’s dynamics depend on the empirical distribution of the whole population. The optimal control problem in this limit can be formulated as a deterministic optimization over a flow of probability measures. The cost functional typically comprises a running cost that depends on the state, the control, and the current measure, plus a terminal cost. The Hamilton–Jacobi–Bellman equation in the space of measures, introduced by Lasry and Lions, characterises the value function. Its solution can be represented via a forward–backward system: a continuity equation for the measure evolution coupled with a Hamilton–Jacobi equation for the value function. The Wasserstein distance provides the natural metric for the measure argument, and the gradient of the value function with respect to the measure is understood in the sense of Lions’ derivative. This structure yields a monotone operator that guarantees uniqueness of the mean field equilibrium under standard convexity assumptions.

In stochastic games with a continuum of players, the equilibrium concept extends to mean field games. The master equation, a partial differential equation on the space of measures, encodes the value function for all possible initial distributions. Recent work by Cardaliaguet, Delarue, and others shows that the master equation is well‑posed in the class of smooth solutions when the Hamiltonian is convex in the momentum and the coupling is monotone. The Wasserstein gradient flow interpretation of the forward Kolmogorov equation links the mean field game system to the JKO scheme: each time step solves a minimisation of the sum of the kinetic energy (quadratic Wasserstein distance) and the potential energy given by the running cost. This variational formulation enables numerical approximation via proximal algorithms and provides a bridge to optimal transport theory.

Applications include crowd motion modelling, where the cost penalises congestion through a density‑dependent term, and financial mathematics, where the mean field limit of many small traders leads to a McKean–Vlasov equation for the price distribution. The optimal transport perspective clarifies the regularity of equilibria: displacement convexity of the interaction energy ensures that the measure flow is a geodesic in Wasserstein space, which in turn yields Lipschitz continuity of the value function with respect to the initial measure. These results are essential for proving convergence of particle approximations and for designing scalable algorithms for large‑scale multi‑agent systems.

Wasserstein Distance in Reinforcement Learning: Policy Evaluation and Distribution Matching

Reinforcement learning (RL) seeks a policy that maximises expected cumulative reward. When the environment is stochastic, the state‑action distribution induced by a policy is a probability measure on the state‑action space. Policy evaluation can be cast as estimating the value function, which satisfies a Bellman equation. Recent approaches replace the usual ℓ² loss on value estimates with a Wasserstein distance between the predicted and target state‑action distributions. This distributional RL perspective, pioneered by Bellemare, Dabney, and Munos, treats the return distribution as a random variable and minimises the Wasserstein‑1 distance between the empirical return distribution and the Bellman‑updated distribution. The Wasserstein metric is sensitive to the geometry of the state space, unlike the Kullback–Leibler divergence which can be infinite when supports do not overlap.

In off‑policy learning, the behaviour policy generates a distribution μ while the target policy induces ν. Importance sampling corrects the mismatch but suffers from high variance. An alternative is to minimise the Wasserstein distance between μ and ν under a transport plan that respects the dynamics. This leads to a regularised optimal transport problem where the cost is the temporal‑difference error. The resulting objective is convex in the coupling and can be solved by a Sinkhorn‑type iteration when entropic regularisation is added. Theoretical guarantees show that the Wasserstein‑regularised Bellman operator is a contraction in the Wasserstein‑1 metric, ensuring convergence of the distributional value iteration.

For continuous control, the policy is often parameterised by a neural network that outputs a Gaussian distribution over actions. Matching the action distribution to a target distribution derived from the critic can be performed by minimising the sliced Wasserstein distance, which is computationally cheap and differentiable. This technique underlies algorithms such as Wasserstein Actor‑Critic and has been shown to improve sample efficiency in high‑dimensional robotic tasks. Moreover, the Wasserstein distance provides a natural measure of policy improvement: a policy update that reduces the Wasserstein distance to the optimal state‑action distribution guarantees monotonic improvement in the expected return under mild regularity conditions.

Worked Example: Wasserstein‑1 Contraction in Distributional RL
Consider a finite Markov decision process with state space S = {s₁, s₂}, action space A = {a₁, a₂}, discount factor γ = 0.9, and deterministic transitions: s₁ → s₂ under a₁, s₂ → s₁ under a₂. The reward is r(s₁,a₁)=1, r(s₂,a₂)=0, all other rewards zero. Let the target return distribution for each state‑action pair be a Dirac at the true value. Show that the distributional Bellman operator T is a contraction in the Wasserstein‑1 metric with modulus γ.
The return distribution Z(s,a) is a random variable. For any two return distributions Z₁, Z₂, the Wasserstein‑1 distance is W₁(Z₁,Z₂) = infπ∈Π(Z₁,Z₂) E(X,Y)∼π[|X−Y|]. The Bellman operator acts as (TZ)(s,a) = r(s,a) + γ Z(s',a') where (s',a') is the next state‑action pair. Because the transition is deterministic, the coupling that achieves the infimum for W₁(Z₁(s',a'), Z₂(s',a')) can be lifted to a coupling for (TZ₁)(s,a) and (TZ₂)(s,a) by adding the same deterministic reward. Hence W₁(TZ₁, TZ₂) = γ W₁(Z₁, Z₂). Since γ = 0.9 < 1, T is a contraction with modulus γ.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks to prove contraction of a distributional Bellman operator, identify the metric (usually Wasserstein‑1), write the definition of the operator, and construct the optimal coupling by propagating the coupling of the next‑step distributions through the deterministic dynamics. The discount factor directly gives the contraction modulus.

Optimal Transport on Manifolds with Variable Curvature: The Role of the MTW Tensor in Non‑Quadratic Costs

The Ma–Trudinger–Wang (MTW) tensor governs the regularity of optimal transport maps for general cost functions c(x,y) on smooth manifolds. For the quadratic cost c(x,y)=½ d²(x,y) on a Riemannian manifold, the MTW tensor reduces to the sectional curvature. When the cost is not quadratic, for instance c(x,y)=h(d(x,y)) with a smooth increasing function h, the MTW tensor acquires additional terms involving h'' and h'. The tensor is defined as the fourth‑order derivative of the cost with respect to mixed variables, evaluated on the tangent space of the c‑exponential map. Its non‑negativity (the MTW condition) is equivalent to the c‑convexity of the target domain and to the continuity of the optimal map.

On manifolds with variable curvature, the MTW tensor can change sign from point to point. Loeper’s maximum principle shows that if the MTW tensor is non‑negative everywhere, then any optimal map between absolutely continuous measures is continuous. Conversely, if the MTW tensor is negative somewhere, one can construct measures for which the optimal map is discontinuous. This dichotomy is sharp: on the sphere with the cost c(x,y)=−log(1−⟨x,y⟩) (the logarithmic cost), the MTW tensor is positive, yielding smooth maps; on the hyperbolic space with the same cost, the tensor becomes negative, leading to possible singularities.

Recent advances by Figalli, Kim, and McCann extend the regularity theory to costs that are not strictly convex in the distance, such as the relativistic cost c(x,y)=√(1+d²(x,y))−1. They introduce a generalized MTW tensor that incorporates the Hessian of the cost with respect to the distance. The condition for regularity becomes a uniform lower bound on this tensor, which can be verified by comparing the cost function to the quadratic cost via a Taylor expansion. These results have direct implications for semi‑discrete optimal transport on manifolds, where the target is a sum of Dirac masses: the power diagram cells are c‑convex sets whose boundaries are smooth if the MTW condition holds.

Optimal Transport for Domain Adaptation with Label Shift: Theoretical Guarantees

Domain adaptation aims to transfer a classifier trained on a source distribution to a target distribution that differs in marginal feature distribution but shares the same conditional label distribution. When the label proportions change (label shift), the optimal transport plan between source and target feature distributions must respect the class‑conditional structure. A principled approach is to compute a Wasserstein barycenter of the class‑conditional source distributions weighted by the target label proportions. The resulting barycenter provides a transported feature representation that aligns the marginals while preserving class information.

Theoretical analysis by Redko, Courty, and others shows that the excess risk of a classifier trained on the transported source data is bounded by the sum of three terms: the source risk, the Wasserstein distance between the source and target marginals, and a discrepancy term that measures the deviation of the transport plan from the ideal class‑conditional coupling. When the cost is the Euclidean distance squared, the optimal transport map is the gradient of a convex potential, and the discrepancy term can be controlled by the Lipschitz constant of the classifier. This yields a generalization bound that scales as O(W₂(μ_S, μ_T) / √n) where n is the source sample size.

Algorithmic implementations use entropic regularisation to compute the transport plan efficiently via Sinkhorn iterations. The regularisation parameter ε trades off bias and variance: a larger ε yields a smoother plan but increases the discrepancy term. Adaptive schemes that decrease ε during training have been shown to achieve the optimal rate.
