Empirically, this method outperforms importance‑weighting and adversarial domain adaptation on benchmark datasets such as Office‑31 and VisDA, especially when the label shift is severe.

Optimal Transport for Uncertainty Quantification: Pushforward Measures and Polynomial Chaos

Uncertainty quantification (UQ) seeks to propagate input uncertainties through a computational model to obtain the distribution of outputs. Let the input random vector X have distribution μ on ℝᵈ and the model be a measurable map f: ℝᵈ → ℝ. The output distribution is the pushforward ν = f♯μ. When f is expensive to evaluate, one approximates the pushforward by constructing a transport map T that pushes a simple reference measure (e.g., standard Gaussian) to μ, and then composes with f. The composition f∘T pushes the reference measure directly to ν, enabling cheap sampling.

Polynomial chaos expansions (PCE) represent the transport map T as a finite sum of orthogonal polynomials with respect to the reference measure. The coefficients are determined by solving a regularised optimal transport problem where the cost is the squared Euclidean distance and the regularisation enforces sparsity in the polynomial basis. This yields a sparse PCE that captures the most important directions of uncertainty. The error in the output distribution measured in Wasserstein‑1 distance is bounded by the product of the Lipschitz constant of f and the Wasserstein‑1 error of the transport map approximation.

For high‑dimensional inputs, the curse of dimensionality is mitigated by using a low‑rank tensor‑train representation of the transport map. The resulting algorithm, known as transport‑based polynomial chaos, has been applied to uncertainty quantification in computational fluid dynamics and Bayesian inverse problems. Theoretical guarantees show that if the transport map belongs to a Sobolev space of mixed regularity, the tensor‑train approximation achieves algebraic convergence rates independent of the nominal dimension. This makes optimal transport a powerful tool for scalable UQ in complex physical models.

  • Confusing the MTW tensor with sectional curvature for non‑quadratic costs; the MTW tensor contains extra terms that can change sign even on constant‑curvature manifolds.
  • Assuming that entropic regularisation always yields a convex objective in domain adaptation; the regularised problem is convex only when the cost is convex and the entropy is Shannon entropy.
  • Treating the Wasserstein distance between return distributions as a simple ℓ¹ norm on histograms; the true Wasserstein‑1 metric requires optimal coupling, not bin‑wise differences.
  • Neglecting the label‑shift discrepancy term in generalization bounds; ignoring it leads to overly optimistic risk estimates when class proportions differ significantly.
  • Using a fixed polynomial degree for the transport map in high‑dimensional UQ without verifying sparsity; the required degree grows with the effective dimension of the input uncertainty.
  • Believing that the pushforward of a Gaussian by a polynomial chaos map remains Gaussian; the output distribution is generally non‑Gaussian and its moments must be estimated from samples.
  • These traps highlight the subtle pitfalls in advanced optimal transport applications.

Common Noise in Mean Field Games: A Wasserstein Perspective

Mean field games (MFG) with common noise arise when all agents are subject to a shared stochastic environment, for example a macroeconomic shock or a common market factor. In the limit of infinitely many agents, the empirical measure of the states becomes a stochastic process taking values in the Wasserstein space P₂(ℝᵈ). The value function of a representative agent is no longer deterministic; it depends on the whole path of the common noise and can be viewed as a random field on P₂(ℝᵈ). The master equation, which characterises the value function, turns into a stochastic partial differential equation on the Wasserstein space. Its differential involves the Lions derivative (or Wasserstein derivative) of the value function with respect to the measure argument, and the common noise appears as a Stratonovich term driven by a cylindrical Brownian motion on the space of measures.

The probabilistic representation of the solution uses the conditional law of the state given the common noise. This conditional law evolves as a measure‑valued diffusion whose drift is the optimal feedback control and whose diffusion coefficient is the common noise intensity. The fixed‑point argument that proves existence and uniqueness of a solution works on the space of flows of measures equipped with a suitable Wasserstein‑type metric. Carmona and Delarue (2018) and Cardaliaguet (2019) established well‑posedness under monotonicity conditions on the coupling cost. The propagation of chaos result states that the empirical measure of the N‑particle system converges in Wasserstein distance to the conditional law, with a rate of order N⁻¹ᐟ² in dimensions d ≤ 2 and a logarithmic correction in higher dimensions.

From a numerical viewpoint, the presence of common noise means that any particle‑based algorithm must sample the common noise path and propagate an ensemble of conditional laws. A stochastic version of the JKO scheme can be derived by discretising the master equation in time and solving a sequence of optimal transport problems with a random cost. The resulting scheme preserves the gradient‑flow structure at the level of conditional laws and provides a convergent approximation of the MFG solution. This perspective links the theory of stochastic gradient flows in Wasserstein space with the analysis of large‑scale stochastic games.

Key references include the monographs by Carmona and Delarue Probabilistic Theory of Mean Field Games (2018) and the lecture notes by Cardaliaguet Notes on Mean Field Games (2019). The stochastic master equation is also treated in the work of Gangbo, Święch, and others on stochastic Hamilton‑Jacobi equations in Wasserstein space.

Wasserstein Gradient Flows for the Vlasov–Poisson Equation

The Vlasov–Poisson equation describes the evolution of a collisionless plasma in phase space ℝᵈˣ × ℝᵈᵛ. The unknown is a probability density f(t,x,v) satisfying ∂ₜf + v·∇ₓf − ∇ₓΦ·∇ᵥf = 0, where the self‑consistent potential Φ solves −ΔΦ = ρ with ρ(t,x) = ∫ f(t,x,v) dv. The free energy functional ℰ(f) = ½∫|v|² f dx dv + ½∫∫ ρ(x) ρ(y) |x−y|²⁻ᵈ dx dy (for d ≥ 3) is the sum of kinetic energy and Coulomb interaction energy. Carrillo, McCann, and Villani (2003) proved that the Vlasov–Poisson equation is the gradient flow of ℰ in the Wasserstein space P₂(ℝ²ᵈ) equipped with the quadratic cost c((x,v),(y,w)) = |x−y|² + |v−w|².

The interaction energy is displacement convex for the Coulomb potential when d ≥ 3, a fact that follows from the non‑negativity of the Ma–Trudinger–Wang tensor associated with the cost c(z,w) = |z−w|²⁻ᵈ on ℝᵈ. This convexity guarantees uniqueness of the gradient flow and provides a variational formulation via the JKO scheme: given a time step τ > 0, the discrete solution fⁿ⁺¹ minimises ℰ(f) + (2τ)⁻¹ W₂²(f, fⁿ) over f ∈ P₂(ℝ²ᵈ). The scheme converges as τ → 0 to the unique weak solution of the Vlasov–Poisson system.

Particle approximations (particle‑in‑cell methods) can be analysed through the lens of propagation of chaos in Wasserstein distance. Hauray and Jabin (2007) showed that the empirical measure of N particles interacting via the Coulomb force converges to the Vlasov–Poisson solution with a rate of order N⁻¹ᐟ² in Wasserstein‑1 distance, provided the initial data are sufficiently regular. The quantitative estimate relies on the displacement convexity of the interaction energy and on a modulated energy functional that controls the difference between the particle system and the mean‑field limit.

The MTW tensor for the Coulomb cost plays a crucial role in the regularity theory of the optimal transport maps that appear in the JKO scheme. For the quadratic cost on phase space the MTW tensor vanishes, but the interaction potential introduces a non‑local term whose convexity is governed by the sign of the MTW tensor of the Coulomb kernel. The non‑negativity of this tensor for d ≥ 3 ensures that the optimal transport maps are smooth and that the discrete solutions remain regular, which is essential for the convergence proof of the variational scheme.

Worked Example: Geodesic in Fisher–Rao Geometry
Compute the Fisher–Rao geodesic between two Gaussian densities on ℝ with means μ₁, μ₂ and common variance σ².
Let p₀(x) = (2πσ²)⁻¹ᐟ² exp(−(x−μ₁)²/(2σ²)) and p₁(x) = (2πσ²)⁻¹ᐟ² exp(−(x−μ₂)²/(2σ²)). Their square roots are √p₀(x) = (2πσ²)⁻¹ᐟ⁴ exp(−(x−μ₁)²/(4σ²)) and √p₁(x) = (2πσ²)⁻¹ᐟ⁴ exp(−(x−μ₂)²/(4σ²)). The Fisher–Rao geodesic is given by pₜ(x) = ((1−t)√p₀(x) + t√p₁(x))² / ∫((1−t)√p₀ + t√p₁)² dx. The numerator expands to (1−t)² p₀(x) + t² p₁(x) + 2t(1−t)√(p₀(x)p₁(x)). The cross term √(p₀p₁) is proportional to exp(−((x−μ₁)²+(x−μ₂)²)/(4σ²)) = exp(−(x−(μ₁+μ₂)/2)²/(2σ²) − (μ₁−μ₂)²/(8σ²)). After completing the square, the integral can be evaluated in closed form using the Gaussian integral formula. The normalisation constant is Zₜ = (1−t)² + t² + 2t(1−t) exp(−(μ₁−μ₂)²/(8σ²)). Hence the geodesic density is pₜ(x) = Zₜ⁻¹ [(1−t)² p₀(x) + t² p₁(x) + 2t(1−t) √(p₀(x)p₁(x))]. This density is not Gaussian unless μ₁ = μ₂; it is a mixture of two Gaussians and a scaled geometric mean. The Fisher–Rao distance between p₀ and p₁ is d_FR(p₀,p₁) = arccos(∫√(p₀p₁) dx) = arccos(exp(−(μ₁−μ₂)²/(8σ²))).
Exam-Day Strategy
Focus on the geometric distinctions: Wasserstein metrics move mass, Fisher–Rao only reshapes densities. Memorise the key theorems — Carrillo‑McCann‑Villani for Vlasov‑Poisson displacement convexity, Carmona‑Delarue for MFG with common noise, and the H‑theorem gradient flow structure for Boltzmann. Be ready to write the JKO scheme for a given energy functional and to identify the correct cost function (quadratic on phase space for Vlasov‑Poisson, Fisher–Rao spherical cost for information geometry). Practise deriving the Fisher–Rao geodesic formula for simple families (Gaussians, exponentials) and computing the corresponding distance. In multiple‑choice questions, eliminate answers that confuse the Boltzmann metric with the Wasserstein metric or that assume Gaussianity is preserved under Fisher–Rao interpolation.

Optimal Transport on the Space of Probability Measures with the Fisher–Rao Metric

The Fisher–Rao metric (also called the Fisher information metric) endows the space of smooth probability densities on a manifold M with a Riemannian structure. For a density p, the tangent space consists of functions u with ∫ u = 0, and the metric is gₚ(u,v) = ∫ (u v)/p dvol. The square‑root map Φ(p) = √p embeds the density space isometrically into the positive orthant of the L² sphere of radius 2. Under this embedding, geodesics become great circles: the geodesic joining p₀ and p₁ is pₜ = ((1−t)√p₀ + t√p₁)² / ∫((1−t)√p₀ + t√p₁)². The induced distance is the Hellinger distance d_FR(p₀,p₁) = arccos(∫√(p₀p₁)). This geometry is fundamentally different from Wasserstein geometry: the Fisher–Rao metric does not transport mass, it only changes the shape of the density, and its geodesics preserve the support of the densities.

The relation between Fisher–Rao and Wasserstein metrics can be understood through the Benamou–Brenier formula. The Wasserstein distance arises from a kinetic energy ∫ |v|² ρ dx, while the Fisher–Rao distance arises from a “Fisher information” kinetic energy ∫ |∇ log ρ|² ρ dx. In the limit of a vanishing diffusion coefficient, the Wasserstein gradient flow of the entropy converges to the heat equation, whereas the Fisher–Rao gradient flow of the entropy yields the replicator equation ∂ₜρ = ρ (log ρ − ∫ ρ log ρ). The Hellinger–Kantorovich metric (also known as the Wasserstein–Fisher–Rao metric) interpolates between the two by allowing both transport and creation/annihilation of mass; its geodesics are solutions of a coupled system of continuity and reaction equations.

Applications of Fisher–Rao geometry are abundant in statistics and information geometry. The Cramér–Rao bound is a direct consequence of the Riemannian structure: the Fisher information matrix is the metric tensor in a parametric family. Bayesian updating can be viewed as a geodesic flow on the statistical manifold when the prior and likelihood belong to an exponential family. In machine learning, natural gradient descent follows the Fisher–Rao gradient of the loss, which is invariant under reparameterisation. The connection to optimal transport is exploited in the Hellinger–Kantorovich framework for unbalanced transport problems, where the cost functional combines a Wasserstein transport term with a Fisher–Rao reaction term.

Key references include the foundational paper by Čencov (1982) on statistical decision rules, the monograph by Amari and Nagaoka Methods of Information Geometry (2000), and the recent work by Liero, Mielke, and Savaré (2018) on the Hellinger–Kantorovich distance. The gradient flow of the entropy in Fisher–Rao geometry is treated in the paper by Ay, Jost, Lê, and Schwachhöfer (2017).

Optimal Transport for the Boltzmann Equation: Entropy Dissipation and Gradient Flow Structure

The Boltzmann equation ∂ₜf = Q(f,f) models the evolution of a rarefied gas. The collision operator Q(f,f) conserves mass, momentum, and energy, and satisfies the H‑theorem: the Boltzmann entropy H(f) = ∫ f log f dv dx is non‑increasing along solutions. Cercignani’s conjecture, proved for hard spheres by Villani (2003) and for a wide class of potentials by Mouhot (2006), states that the entropy production D(f) = −dH/dt is bounded below by a constant times the relative entropy H(f|M), where M is the Maxwellian equilibrium with the same conserved quantities. This inequality implies exponential convergence to equilibrium in relative entropy.

A gradient flow structure for the Boltzmann equation was discovered by Carlen and Gangbo (2003) and later refined by Villani. They introduced a metric on the space of probability measures, called the Boltzmann metric, defined via the Dirichlet form of the linearised collision operator L. For a tangent vector u = ∂ₜf, the squared norm is ‖u‖²Boltz = ⟨u, L⁻¹ u⟩L²(M⁻¹). With this metric, the Boltzmann equation becomes the gradient flow of the entropy: ∂ₜf = −∇Boltz H(f). The metric is not the Wasserstein metric; it encodes the microscopic collision mechanism and degenerates in the directions of the collision invariants (mass, momentum, energy).

The grazing collisions limit (Landau equation) provides a bridge to optimal transport. When the collision kernel concentrates on small deflections, the Boltzmann metric converges to a quadratic form that coincides with the Wasserstein metric weighted by the diffusion matrix of the Landau operator. In this limit, the Landau equation is the Wasserstein gradient flow of the entropy, a result due to Villani (2000) and later extended by Carrillo, McCann, and Villani (2003). Thus the Boltzmann gradient flow can be seen as a non‑local, non‑quadratic perturbation of the Wasserstein gradient flow.

Entropic regularisation of the Boltzmann equation leads to a family of approximate gradient flows that converge to the true Boltzmann flow as the regularisation parameter vanishes. This perspective has inspired numerical schemes based on the JKO minimisation with a Boltzmann‑type cost, which preserve the entropy dissipation property at the discrete level. The interplay between the Boltzmann metric, the Wasserstein metric, and the Fisher–Rao metric (which appears in the linearised entropy dissipation) illustrates the rich geometric structure underlying kinetic equations.

  • Confusing the Fisher–Rao metric with the Wasserstein metric; they induce different geodesics and the Fisher–Rao distance does not account for mass transport.
  • Assuming the Vlasov–Poisson gradient flow uses the quadratic cost on configuration space only; the correct phase space cost includes both position and velocity variables.
  • Overlooking the role of the common noise in the master equation; the value function becomes a random field on the Wasserstein space, not a deterministic function.
  • Treating the Boltzmann collision operator as a Wasserstein gradient; the natural metric is the Boltzmann metric derived from the linearised collision operator.
  • Believing that the Fisher–Rao geodesic between Gaussians remains Gaussian; the square‑root interpolation generally produces non‑Gaussian densities.
  • Neglecting the displacement convexity condition for the Coulomb interaction; it holds only for dimensions d ≥ 3 and fails in lower dimensions, affecting well‑posedness.
  • Confusing the Hellinger–Kantorovich metric with the Fisher–Rao metric; the former interpolates between Wasserstein and Fisher–Rao and allows mass variation.

These advanced topics illustrate the breadth of optimal transport beyond the quadratic cost and highlight the interplay between geometry, PDE, and probability.

The Optimal Transport Approach to the Semigeostrophic Equations: The Monge–Ampère Equation in Geophysical Fluid Dynamics

The semigeostrophic (SG) equations provide a simplified model for large-scale atmospheric and oceanic flows where the Coriolis force dominates inertia. In the formulation due to Cullen and Purser (1984), the SG system can be rewritten as a Hamiltonian system on the group of volume-preserving diffeomorphisms, and its solutions correspond to geodesics in the Wasserstein space of probability measures with a specific cost function. The key insight is that the potential vorticity q is transported by the geostrophic velocity field ug, and the invertibility principle relating q to the geostrophic streamfunction ψ takes the form of a Monge–Ampère equation. On the f-plane with constant Coriolis parameter f, the geostrophic coordinates X = x + ∇ψ/f define a map from physical space to geostrophic space. The conservation of potential vorticity q implies that the pushforward of the Lebesgue measure by this map equals the potential vorticity distribution: X# Leb = q dx. The geostrophic streamfunction ψ then solves the Monge–Ampère equation det(I + D²ψ/f) = q with the constraint that I + D²ψ/f is positive definite. This is precisely the optimal transport problem with quadratic cost c(x,y) = |x−y|²/2 where the target measure has density q. The Brenier theorem guarantees the existence and uniqueness of a convex potential ψ solving this equation when q is a probability density. The time evolution of the SG system corresponds to the displacement interpolation between the initial and final potential vorticity distributions in Wasserstein space. Cullen and Gangbo (2001) proved that weak solutions of the SG equations can be constructed as limits of discrete-time variational schemes analogous to the JKO scheme, where each time step minimizes a functional combining the Wasserstein distance and a potential energy term. The Hamiltonian structure of the SG equations is reflected in the fact that the geodesic equations in Wasserstein space coincide with the SG equations when the cost is quadratic and the potential energy is the integral of ψ q. This optimal transport perspective has led to new existence results for weak solutions with measure-valued potential vorticity, and it provides a geometric framework for understanding the formation of fronts and the regularity of solutions. The MTW tensor for the quadratic cost on the torus or on the sphere (for the spherical SG model) is nonnegative, which implies displacement convexity of the potential energy and stability of the variational scheme. Numerical methods based on this formulation, such as the semi-discrete optimal transport approach using power diagrams, have been developed by Benamou, Brenier, and others, providing structure-preserving discretizations that conserve potential vorticity exactly.

The Multi-Marginal Optimal Transport for the Hubbard Model: The Strong Correlation Limit and Density Functional Theory

In density functional theory (DFT), the exact ground-state energy of a many-electron system is expressed as a minimization over the electron density ρ of the sum of the kinetic energy, the electron-nucleus attraction, and the electron-electron repulsion energy. The latter is the most challenging term. The strictly correlated electrons (SCE) limit, introduced by Seidl, Gori-Giorgi, and Savin (2007), corresponds to the infinite correlation strength limit where the kinetic energy is neglected and the electrons are perfectly correlated to minimize the repulsion. For N electrons with density ρ, the SCE energy is given by the multi-marginal optimal transport problem with N marginals all equal to ρ/N and the Coulomb cost c(x₁,…,xN) = ∑i<j</sub> 1/|xi−xj|. The optimal transport plan π is a probability measure on (ℝ³)N with marginals ρ/N that minimizes the expected Coulomb repulsion. The Kantorovich duality for this problem yields N Kantorovich potentials u₁,…,uN satisfying ∑ ui(xi) ≤ c(x₁,…,xN) with equality on the support of π. By symmetry, the potentials are equal up to additive constants: ui = u + ci. The optimal plan is concentrated on a graph (x, T₂(x), …, TN(x)) where the maps Ti are the co-motion functions. These maps satisfy the cyclical monotonicity condition ∑i u(Ti(x)) = c(x, T₂(x), …, TN(x)) and push ρ/N forward to itself: Ti#(ρ/N) = ρ/N. For N=2, the co-motion function is the optimal transport map for the Coulomb cost, which is known to be the gradient of a convex function only in one dimension; in higher dimensions the cost does not satisfy the MTW condition and the map can be discontinuous. For N≥3, the structure is even more complex. The SCE energy provides a lower bound for the exact electron-electron interaction energy and serves as a reference for constructing exchange-correlation functionals. The interpolation between the weak-correlation (Kohn-Sham) and strong-correlation (SCE) limits is the basis of the adiabatic connection formula. Recent work by Colombo, Di Marino, and others has established the existence and regularity of co-motion functions for radial densities, showing that they are measure-preserving maps that partition space into cells. The multi-marginal formulation also connects to the Wasserstein barycenter problem: the SCE energy can be seen as the minimum of the sum of pairwise Wasserstein distances between the marginals. This perspective has inspired new numerical algorithms for DFT based on entropic regularization of the multi-marginal problem, where the Sinkhorn algorithm is extended to multiple marginals. The computational complexity scales exponentially with N, but low-rank tensor approximations and the symmetry of the problem reduce the cost significantly. The SCE limit has been used to develop accurate functionals for strongly correlated systems such as stretched bonds and Mott insulators, where standard DFT approximations fail.

Worked Problem: SCE Energy for a Two-Electron System
Consider a two-electron system in one dimension with density ρ(x) = (1/2)𝟙[−1,1](x). Compute the strictly correlated electrons (SCE) energy, i.e., the minimum of ∫∫ (1/|x−y|) dπ(x,y) over couplings π with marginals ρ/2.
The marginals are μ = ν = (1/4)𝟙[−1,1]. The optimal transport map T for the cost c(x,y)=1/|x−y| on ℝ with these marginals is the decreasing rearrangement. Since the cost is convex and decreasing in |x−y|, the optimal coupling is the antimonotone coupling: T(x) = −x. The SCE energy is ∫−11 (1/|x−(−x)|) (1/4) dx = (1/4) ∫−11 1/(2|x|) dx. This integral diverges logarithmically at x=0, reflecting the Coulomb singularity. In 1D, the Coulomb cost 1/|x−y| is not integrable against the product measure near the diagonal. The SCE energy is +∞ for this density. For a regularized cost or a density vanishing at the origin, the energy would be finite. The co-motion function is T(x)=−x, which preserves the symmetric density.
Exam Strategy: Multi-Marginal OT in DFT
When asked about the SCE limit, remember: (1) it is a multi-marginal OT problem with N equal marginals ρ/N and Coulomb cost; (2) the optimal plan is deterministic (Monge) and given by co-motion functions Ti; (3) the Kantorovich potentials are equal up to constants by symmetry; (4) for N=2 in 1D, the optimal map is the decreasing rearrangement; (5) the SCE energy is a lower bound for the exact interaction energy and provides the strong-correlation reference for the adiabatic connection. Do not confuse the SCE limit (infinite correlation, zero kinetic energy) with the Kohn-Sham system (non-interacting, exact kinetic energy).

The Fisher–Rao Geometry on the Space of Probability Measures: The Spherical Structure, the Hellinger Distance, and the Gradient Flow of the Entropy

The Fisher–Rao metric is a Riemannian metric on the space of probability densities that arises naturally from the Fisher information matrix in statistical estimation theory. For a parametric family p(x|θ), the Fisher–Rao metric is gij(θ) = ∫ (∂i log p)(∂j log p) p dx. On the infinite-dimensional manifold of all positive probability densities on a domain Ω, the Fisher–Rao metric at a density ρ is defined on tangent vectors u, v (with ∫ u = ∫ v = 0) by ⟨u, v⟩FR = ∫ (u v)/ρ dx. This metric has a remarkable geometric interpretation: the map Φ: ρ ↦ 2√ρ is an isometry from the space of probability densities equipped with the Fisher–Rao metric onto the positive orthant of the unit sphere in L²(Ω) with the standard L² metric. Indeed, if ρt is a curve of densities, then ∂t Φ(ρt) = (∂t ρt)/√ρt, and the L² norm squared of this derivative is ∫ (∂t ρt)²/ρt dx, which is the Fisher–Rao squared speed. Consequently, the Fisher–Rao geodesic between two densities ρ₀, ρ₁ is given by the spherical interpolation √ρt = (1−t)√ρ₀ + t√ρ₁ (normalized to unit L² norm), which corresponds to the Hellinger arc. The Hellinger distance H(ρ₀, ρ₁) = ‖√ρ₀ − √ρ₁‖L² is exactly the Fisher–Rao geodesic distance. This spherical geometry implies that the Fisher–Rao metric has constant positive sectional curvature, in stark contrast to the Wasserstein metric which has nonnegative Alexandrov curvature. The gradient flow of the Boltzmann entropy H(ρ) = ∫ ρ log ρ dx with respect to the Fisher–Rao metric is the replicator equation ∂t ρ = ρ (log ρ − ∫ ρ log ρ), which preserves the total mass and positivity but does not transport mass spatially. The Fisher–Rao gradient of a functional F(ρ) is ∇FR F = ρ (δF/δρ − ∫ ρ δF/δρ), where δF/δρ is the first variation. The Fisher–Rao metric is the natural metric for reaction processes (birth-death, mutation) where mass is created or destroyed locally, while the Wasserstein metric is natural for transport processes. The Hellinger–Kantorovich (HK) metric, also known as the Wasserstein–Fisher–Rao metric, interpolates between these two by allowing both transport and reaction. It is defined via a dynamic formulation with a continuity equation ∂t ρ + ∇·(ρ v) = ρ r where v is a velocity field and r is a reaction rate, and the action is ∫ (|v|² + α² r²) ρ dx dt. The HK metric reduces to the Wasserstein metric when α → ∞ (no reaction) and to the Fisher–Rao metric when the transport cost dominates. The gradient flow of the entropy in the HK metric yields the Fisher–KPP equation ∂t ρ = Δρ + ρ(1−ρ), combining diffusion (Wasserstein) and reaction (Fisher–Rao). The spherical structure of the Fisher–Rao metric also underlies the information geometry of exponential families, where the Fisher–Rao metric coincides with the Hessian of the log-partition function, and the e- and m-connections are dual with respect to this metric.

The Schrödinger Bridge Problem and Large Deviations: The Donsker–Varadhan Rate Function and the Sanov Theorem

The Schrödinger bridge problem (SBP) asks for the most likely evolution of a system of independent Brownian particles conditioned to have a given initial density ρ₀ and final density ρ₁ at time T. Formulated by Schrödinger in 1931, it seeks a probability measure P on the path space C([0,T]; ℝd) that minimizes the relative entropy H(P | P0) with respect to the Wiener measure P0 (Brownian motion with a given initial distribution) subject to the marginal constraints P0 = ρ₀, PT = ρ₁. The solution is a Markov process whose transition densities are given by p(t,x; s,y) = f(t,x) g(s,y) p0(t,x; s,y) where p0 is the Brownian transition kernel and f, g solve the Schrödinger system f(t,x) = ∫ p0(t,x; T,y) g(T,y) dy, g(s,y) = ∫ p0(0,x; s,y) f(0,x) dx with boundary conditions f(0,x)g(0,x) = ρ₀(x), f(T,x)g(T,x) = ρ₁(x). This is equivalent to the entropic optimal transport problem with cost c(x,y) = |x−y|²/(2T) and entropy regularization parameter ε = T. The large deviations perspective, developed by Donsker and Varadhan (1975) and later by Léonard (2010), identifies the SBP as the zero-noise limit of a large deviation principle for the empirical measure of N independent Brownian particles. The rate function for the empirical path measure is the relative entropy with respect to the Wiener measure, and the contraction principle yields the rate function for the marginal densities at times 0 and T. The Sanov theorem states that the empirical measure of N i.i.d. samples from a distribution μ satisfies a large deviation principle with rate function H(ν | μ). For the Schrödinger bridge, the joint empirical measure at times 0 and T satisfies an LDP with rate function H(π | π0) where π0 is the law of (X₀, XT) under the Wiener measure. The minimizer of this rate function subject to the marginal constraints is exactly the Schrödinger bridge. This connection reveals that the entropic regularization parameter ε in optimal transport corresponds to the temperature or noise level in the underlying stochastic system. The Donsker–Varadhan variational formula for the relative entropy, H(ν | μ) = supf {∫ f dν − log ∫ ef dμ}, is the dual formulation of the entropic OT problem. The Sinkhorn algorithm can be interpreted as a coordinate ascent on this dual problem. The large deviations approach also provides a rigorous foundation for the convergence of the entropic OT plan to the true OT plan as ε → 0: the rate function Γ-converges to the indicator function of the set of optimal transport plans. Moreover, the Schrödinger bridge at finite ε can be seen as the gradient flow of the entropy in the Wasserstein space with a modified metric, or equivalently as the solution of a stochastic control problem where the control cost is the relative entropy. This stochastic control formulation, due to Fleming and others, connects the SBP to the Hamilton–Jacobi–Bellman equation and the Hopf–Cole transform.

The Optimal Transport for the Vlasov–Poisson System: The Gradient Flow Structure in Phase Space and Landau Damping

The Vlasov–Poisson (VP) system describes the evolution of a collisionless plasma or a self-gravitating system in the mean-field limit. The distribution function f(t,x,v) on phase space ℝd × ℝd satisfies ∂t f + v·∇x f + E·∇v f = 0 with the self-consistent electric field E = −∇x φ and the Poisson equation −Δφ = ρ − 1 where ρ(t,x) = ∫ f(t,x,v) dv is the spatial density. The VP system conserves the total mass, momentum, energy, and the Boltzmann entropy H(f) = ∫ f log f dx dv. Unlike the Fokker–Planck equation, the VP system is not a gradient flow in the Wasserstein metric on the space of probability measures on phase space with the standard quadratic cost. However, a gradient flow structure can be recovered by considering a modified metric that accounts for the Hamiltonian nature of the dynamics. The key observation is that the VP system can be written as ∂t f = {f, H} where H = |v|²/2 + φ is the single-particle Hamiltonian and {·,·} is the Poisson bracket. This is a Hamiltonian system on the space of probability measures with the Lie–Poisson structure induced by the Poisson bracket. The Wasserstein gradient flow structure emerges in the grazing collisions limit of the Boltzmann equation, which leads to the Landau equation, and the VP system is the collisionless limit. For the VP system, the displacement convexity of the energy functional E(f) = ∫ (|v|²/2) f dx dv + (1/2) ∫ |∇φ|² dx along Wasserstein geodesics in phase space is related to the stability of stationary solutions. The Antonov criterion for stability of spherical stellar systems translates into a condition on the second variation of the energy-Casimir functional. The Landau damping phenomenon, proved by Mouhot and Villani (2011) for the VP system on the torus, shows that perturbations of a homogeneous equilibrium decay weakly in time due to phase mixing, despite the absence of dissipation. This can be understood geometrically as the filamentation of the distribution function in phase space, which increases the fine-scale structure while the coarse-grained density relaxes. The optimal transport perspective on Landau damping involves the study of the Wasserstein distance between the time-evolved distribution and the equilibrium. The work of Han-Kwan, Nguyen, and Rousset has shown that the Wasserstein distance between the solution and the equilibrium can grow at most polynomially in time, while the negative Sobolev norms decay exponentially. The gradient flow structure for the VP system with a penalized Poisson equation (the Vlasov–Poisson–Fokker–Planck system) has been studied by Carrillo, McCann, and Villani, who showed that it is a gradient flow of the free energy in the Wasserstein metric. For the pure VP system, the lack of a gradient flow structure in the standard Wasserstein metric is related to the fact that the Poisson bracket is not symmetric. However, the system can be embedded in a larger space where a gradient flow structure exists, using the concept of GENERIC (General Equation for Non-Equilibrium Reversible-Irreversible Coupling) or by considering the Wasserstein metric on the space of measures with a cost function adapted to the Hamiltonian flow.

  • Confusing the Fisher–Rao gradient flow (replicator equation) with the Wasserstein gradient flow (Fokker–Planck); the former preserves mass locally without transport, the latter transports mass spatially.
  • Assuming the Schrödinger bridge at finite ε is the same as the optimal transport plan; it converges to the OT plan only as ε → 0, and the rate of convergence depends on the regularity of the cost and the measures.
  • Believing the Vlasov–Poisson system is a Wasserstein gradient flow with the standard quadratic cost on phase space; it is a Hamiltonian system with a Lie–Poisson structure, and a gradient flow structure requires a modified metric or the addition of dissipation.
  • Overlooking that the SCE energy for N=2 in 1D with Coulomb cost 1/|x−y| is infinite for densities with overlapping support due to the non-integrability of the singularity.
  • Confusing the Hellinger–Kantorovich metric with the Fisher–Rao metric; the HK metric allows both transport and reaction, while Fisher–Rao only allows reaction.
  • Assuming the Monge–Ampère equation for the semigeostrophic system is the standard quadratic cost on ℝd; on the sphere or with variable Coriolis parameter, the cost function is modified and the MTW tensor must be checked for regularity.
  • Neglecting the role of the Donsker–Varadhan variational formula in connecting the Schrödinger bridge to the dual formulation of entropic OT; the Sinkhorn algorithm is a coordinate ascent on this dual, not a primal iteration.

Large Deviation Principles for Empirical Measures and the Rate Function

The theory of large deviations provides a precise asymptotic description of the probability that an empirical measure deviates from its expected limit. For a sequence of independent and identically distributed random variables X1,…,X_n with common law μ on a Polish space X, Sanov's theorem (1957) states that the empirical measures L_n = frac1nΣi=1^n δX_i satisfy a large deviation principle (LDP) on the space P(X) equipped with the weak topology, with speed n and good rate function given by the relative entropy H(nu|μ) = ∫ log(dnu)/(dμ) dnu if nu≪μ and +∞ otherwise. The Donsker–Varadhan variational formula (1975) characterises this rate function as the Legendre transform of the logarithmic moment generating functional, linking it directly to the dual formulation of entropic optimal transport. In the Schrödinger bridge problem, one considers the most likely evolution of a system of independent particles conditioned on marginal constraints at two times; the large deviation principle for the empirical path measure yields the entropic optimal transport problem as the zero‑noise limit. Léonard (2010) showed that the rate function for the Schrödinger bridge is the relative entropy with respect to the reversible diffusion measure, and that the minimiser converges to the classical Monge–Kantorovich optimal plan as the noise parameter varepsilon→0. This connection explains why the Sinkhorn algorithm, which solves the entropic regularised problem, can be interpreted as a coordinate ascent on the Donsker–Varadhan dual. The LDP framework also provides quantitative estimates: the probability that the empirical measure lies in a set A decays like exp(-ninfnu∈ A H(nu|μ)), a fact used in statistical inference to derive confidence regions for Wasserstein distances.

p‑Wasserstein Geometry for General Exponents and Displacement Convexity

For any exponent p≥1 the p-Wasserstein distance between two probability measures μ,nu on a metric space (X,d) is defined by W_p(μ,nu) = bigl(infπ∈Π(μ,nu)∫ d(x,y)^p dπ(x,y)bigr)1/p. The space P_p(X) of measures with finite p-th moment becomes a complete geodesic metric space; geodesics are given by displacement interpolations μ_t = ((1-t)π1 + tπ2)_#π where π is an optimal plan for the cost d^p. Otto's formal Riemannian calculus, which identifies the tangent space at μ with the closure of gradient vector fields in L2(μ), relies crucially on the quadratic cost p=2. For p≠2 the tangent cone is not a Hilbert space and the Benamou–Brenier dynamic formulation involves the L^p norm of the velocity field. McCann's displacement convexity theorem (1997) proves that the Boltzmann entropy H(μ)=∫ρlogρ is displacement convex along W2-geodesics when the underlying space has non‑negative Ricci curvature. Sturm (2006) and von Renesse–Sturm (2009) showed that for p>2 the entropy fails to be displacement convex even on the Euclidean line; a counterexample uses Gaussian measures whose W_p-geodesics are not given by linear interpolation of means and variances. Conversely, for 1≤ p<2 the entropy is displacement convex on spaces satisfying a suitable CD_p(K,N) condition introduced by Ohta (2009). These distinctions are essential when studying gradient flows of interaction energies in W_p spaces: the JKO scheme converges for p=2 under standard convexity assumptions, while for p≠2 one must verify a p-convexity condition tailored to the exponent.

Worked Example
Explain why the relative entropy functional is displacement convex in the 2‑Wasserstein space but fails to be displacement convex in the p‑Wasserstein space for p>2.
Displacement convexity of a functional F along a geodesic (μ_t)t∈[0,1] means F(μ_t)≤ (1-t)F(μ0)+tF(μ1). For the relative entropy H(μ)=∫ρlogρ on R^d, McCann (1997) proved that H is displacement convex along W2-geodesics because the quadratic cost yields a linear interpolation of the optimal map T(x)=x+∇φ(x) and the Jacobian determinant satisfies a Prékopa–Leindler type inequality. When p>2, the optimal transport map for the cost |x-y|^p is no longer the gradient of a convex function; the geodesics are not given by linear interpolation of maps. Sturm (2006) constructed a counterexample on R using two centred Gaussian measures μ0=N(0,σ02) and μ1=N(0,σ12). Their W_p-geodesic μ_t has variance σ_t2 that is not an affine function of t; the entropy H(μ_t)=frac12log(2π eσ_t2) becomes strictly larger than the linear interpolation of the endpoint entropies, violating displacement convexity. Hence the property is specific to p=2.
Exam Strategy
When asked about displacement convexity, first identify the exponent p; recall McCann's theorem holds only for p=2. For p≠2, cite the counterexample by Sturm (2006) using Gaussian measures on ℝ.

The Grazing Collisions Limit of the Boltzmann Equation and the Landau Equation

The Boltzmann equation describes the evolution of a dilute gas through binary collisions. In the grazing collisions regime, the deflection angle of each collision becomes very small while the collision frequency increases so that the total momentum transfer remains finite. Landau (1936) formally derived a limiting equation, now called the Landau equation, in which the collision operator is replaced by a diffusion operator in velocity space: ∂_t f + v·∇_x f = ∇_v·bigl(∫ a(v-v_*)(f_*∇_v f - f∇v_*f_*) dv_*bigr) with the matrix a(z)=|z|2 I - z⊗ z. Villani (1998) gave a rigorous proof of this limit using entropy dissipation estimates and compactness arguments. The Landau equation conserves mass, momentum, and energy, and satisfies an H‑theorem: the Boltzmann entropy H(f)=∫ flog f is non‑increasing. Carrillo, McCann, and Villani (2003) discovered that the spatially homogeneous Landau equation can be written as a gradient flow of the entropy with respect to a weighted Wasserstein metric where the cost function is the inverse of the diffusion matrix a. This gradient flow structure provides a powerful tool for proving existence, uniqueness, and long‑time convergence to equilibrium. The same limit connects to the Vlasov–Poisson system: when the grazing collisions parameter tends to zero while a mean‑field scaling is applied, one recovers the Vlasov–Poisson equation as the collisionless limit. The interplay between the Hamiltonian Vlasov dynamics and the dissipative Landau dynamics is a central theme in kinetic theory.

Hamilton–Jacobi–Bellman Equations, Viscosity Solutions, and the Benamou–Brenier Formulation

The Benamou–Brenier dynamic formulation expresses the squared 2‑Wasserstein distance as the minimal action inf(ρ_t,v_t)∫01∫ |v_t|2ρ_t dx dt subject to the continuity equation ∂_tρ_t+∇·(ρ_t v_t)=0. Introducing a Lagrange multiplier φ(t,x) for the constraint leads to the Hamilton–Jacobi–Bellman (HJB) equation ∂_tφ + frac12|∇φ|2 = 0 with terminal condition φ(1,x)=psi(x). The value function φ represents the optimal cost-to-go for a controlled diffusion whose drift is the velocity field. Crandall and Lions (1983) developed the theory of viscosity solutions to give a well‑posedness framework for such first‑order Hamilton–Jacobi equations, even when classical solutions cease to exist due to shock formation. The Hopf–Lax formula φ(t,x)=inf_ybigl(psi(y)+(|x-y|2)/(2(1-t))bigr) provides the unique viscosity solution and coincides with the Kantorovich potential in optimal transport. The duality between the continuity equation and the HJB equation is the infinite‑dimensional analogue of the Pontryagin maximum principle. This perspective extends to general costs c(x,y): the dynamic formulation involves a Lagrangian L(x,v) that is the Legendre transform of the Hamiltonian H(x,p)=sup_v(p· v-L(x,v)). The resulting HJB equation ∂_tφ+H(x,∇φ)=0 governs the evolution of the Kantorovich potential, and its viscosity solution selects the physically relevant optimal transport map.

Optimal Transport on Non‑Compact Manifolds: The Monge–Ampère Equation and Asymptotic Geometry

On a complete non‑compact Riemannian manifold (M,g) the optimal transport problem with cost c(x,y)=frac12 d_g(x,y)2 leads to a Monge–Ampère type equation det(D2φ + Hess_g(d2)/(2)) = (ρ0)/(ρ1(exp_x∇φ)) for the transport potential φ. Caffarelli's global regularity theory (1992) requires the target domain to be convex in Euclidean space; on a general manifold the analogous condition is that the exponential map of the target measure's support is convex with respect to the cost. Figalli, Kim, and McCann (2011) proved that if the manifold has non‑negative sectional curvature and the densities are bounded away from zero and infinity on their supports, then the optimal map is a global diffeomorphism. The Ma–Trudinger–Wang (MTW) tensor, which measures the cost's cross‑curvature, plays a decisive role: non‑negativity of the MTW tensor (the condition A3≥0) guarantees local regularity, while its failure can produce singular transport maps even for smooth densities. On asymptotically Euclidean or asymptotically hyperbolic manifolds, the behaviour of the transport map at infinity is governed by the asymptotic geometry of the cost function. For instance, on hyperbolic space the squared distance cost satisfies A3>0, yielding smooth optimal maps for all smooth positive densities. These results have applications in geometric analysis, such as proving functional inequalities (log‑Sobolev, Talagrand) on manifolds with curvature bounds via displacement convexity along Wasserstein geodesics.

  • Confusing the large deviation rate function for empirical measures with the relative entropy of the limiting measure; the rate function is the relative entropy with respect to the true law, not the entropy of the empirical measure itself.
  • Assuming displacement convexity of the entropy holds for all p‑Wasserstein spaces; it is a special feature of p=2 and fails for p>2 as shown by Sturm.
  • Believing the Landau equation is a gradient flow in the standard 2‑Wasserstein metric; it requires a weighted metric with cost given by the inverse diffusion matrix.
  • Treating viscosity solutions of the HJB equation as classical solutions; shocks correspond to the formation of transport rays and must be handled via the viscosity framework.
  • Overlooking the role of the MTW tensor on non‑compact manifolds; regularity can break down even with smooth densities if the cost's cross‑curvature is negative.
  • Neglecting the distinction between the Benamou–Brenier formulation for quadratic cost and its generalisation to arbitrary costs; the Lagrangian and Hamiltonian change accordingly.
  • Thinking that the grazing collisions limit yields the Vlasov–Poisson system directly; the Vlasov–Poisson system arises from a mean‑field scaling, not from the grazing collisions limit alone.

These advanced topics illustrate how optimal transport intertwines with large deviation theory, geometric analysis, kinetic equations, and stochastic control, providing a unified language for many modern research directions.

Mean Field Games with Common Noise and Optimal Transport

Mean field games (MFG) describe the limit of large populations of interacting agents where each agent solves a stochastic control problem influenced by the empirical distribution of all agents. When a common noise affects all agents simultaneously, the limiting object is a random measure flow rather than a deterministic one. Optimal transport provides the natural geometry to quantify distances between these random flows: the 2‑Wasserstein distance between conditional laws given the common noise path measures the discrepancy between two candidate solutions. The master equation, an infinite‑dimensional PDE on the space of probability measures, can be interpreted as a Hamilton–Jacobi equation in the Wasserstein space. Its viscosity solution theory, developed by Lions and Souganidis, relies on the displacement convexity of the Hamiltonian along Wasserstein geodesics. The propagation of chaos result shows that the empirical measure of the N‑particle system converges in law to the unique MFG solution, with a rate that can be expressed in terms of the Wasserstein distance between the empirical measure and the limiting conditional law. Numerically, the JKO scheme (Jordan–Kinderlehrer–Otto) can be adapted to the MFG setting by alternating a proximal step in the Wasserstein metric for the Fokker–Planck equation and a backward Hamilton–Jacobi step for the value function. This splitting preserves the gradient‑flow structure and yields convergent algorithms even in the presence of common noise. Recent work by Cardaliaguet, Delarue, Lasry, and Lions establishes well‑posedness of the master equation under monotonicity conditions that are precisely displacement convexity assumptions on the coupling cost. The optimal transport viewpoint also clarifies the relationship between MFG and McKean–Vlasov equations: the latter correspond to the case where the Hamiltonian is linear in the measure argument, so the master equation reduces to a linear Kolmogorov equation on the Wasserstein space.

Optimal Transport for Causal Inference and Counterfactual Estimation

Causal inference aims to estimate the effect of a treatment T on an outcome Y using observational data. The potential outcomes framework posits two distributions: the factual distribution of Y given T=1 and the counterfactual distribution of Y given T=0. Optimal transport enters by providing a principled way to map the observed treated population onto the control population, thereby constructing a synthetic counterfactual. The Monge problem with quadratic cost seeks a transport map ∇φ that pushes the distribution of covariates X in the treated group to that in the control group; under unconfoundedness and overlap, the pushforward of the treated outcome distribution via this map yields an estimator of the average treatment effect on the treated (ATT). When the covariate dimension is high, entropic regularization (Sinkhorn algorithm) yields a stochastic coupling that approximates the deterministic Monge map while remaining computationally tractable. The Kantorovich dual formulation gives a bound on the ATT: the difference in expected outcomes is bounded by the product of the Lipschitz constant of the outcome regression and the 1‑Wasserstein distance between the treated and control covariate distributions. This bound is sharp and leads to sensitivity analysis: if an unobserved confounder shifts the covariate distribution by at most ε in Wasserstein distance, the ATT can change by at most L·ε. Wasserstein barycenters generalize this idea to multiple treatment arms: the barycenter of the covariate distributions across arms represents a common target population, and transporting each arm to the barycenter yields a set of counterfactual distributions that are mutually comparable. Recent work by Johansson, Shalit, and Sontag shows that minimizing the Wasserstein distance between the treated and control representations learned by a neural network improves covariate balance and reduces bias in treatment effect estimation. The approach also extends to instrumental variable settings where the transport map is constrained to respect the exclusion restriction.

Examiner's trap
A question asks: "In a causal inference setting with high‑dimensional covariates, why might one prefer an entropically regularized optimal transport coupling over the exact Monge map for estimating the ATT?"
The exact Monge map requires solving a high‑dimensional Monge–Ampère equation, which is computationally prohibitive and numerically unstable when the covariate dimension exceeds a few tens. Entropic regularization replaces the deterministic transport plan with a Gibbs kernel that can be computed via the Sinkhorn algorithm in O(n²) time with GPU acceleration. The regularized coupling converges to the true Monge map as the regularization parameter ε → 0, and for finite ε it provides a biased but consistent estimator whose bias can be quantified using the dual formulation. Moreover, the stochastic coupling naturally yields confidence intervals via bootstrap on the transport plan, whereas the deterministic map does not. Therefore, the regularized approach trades a controllable bias for massive computational gains and statistical tractability.
Exam‑day strategy
When a question links optimal transport to causal inference, identify the three pillars: (1) the cost function (usually quadratic), (2) the regularization (entropy vs. none), and (3) the identification assumption (unconfoundedness, overlap). Write down the Kantorovich dual bound L·W₁ as a quick sanity check for sensitivity analysis. If the problem mentions high dimensions, immediately argue for Sinkhorn; if it asks for exact counterfactuals, mention the Monge map and the Monge–Ampère equation.

Optimal Transport in Differential Privacy and Robust Statistics

Differential privacy (DP) requires that the output distribution of a mechanism changes only slightly when a single data point is altered. The 2‑Wasserstein distance provides a natural metric for measuring this sensitivity when the mechanism adds Gaussian noise: the Gaussian mechanism with variance σ²I satisfies (ε,δ)‑DP with ε proportional to the 2‑Wasserstein distance between the output distributions on neighboring datasets. More generally, the optimal transport coupling between the two output distributions yields the tightest possible privacy guarantee for a given noise distribution, because the privacy loss random variable is exactly the log‑density ratio along the coupling. This insight leads to the "optimal transport privacy" framework where one designs the noise distribution by solving a Monge problem that minimizes the expected cost subject to a Wasserstein sensitivity constraint. In robust statistics, the Huber contamination model assumes that an adversary can replace a fraction α of the data with arbitrary outliers. The resulting ambiguity set is a Wasserstein ball of radius α around the empirical distribution. The robust estimator that minimizes the worst‑case risk over this ball is equivalent to a regularized empirical risk minimization where the regularizer is the optimal transport cost to the empirical measure. For linear regression with squared loss, the robust estimator coincides with the solution of a distributionally robust optimization problem that can be solved via a convex program involving the Sinkhorn divergence. Recent work by Blanchet, Kang, and Murthy shows that the Wasserstein distributionally robust estimator achieves the minimax optimal rate under heavy‑tailed distributions, and the optimal transport map provides a certificate of robustness by explicitly transporting the contaminated distribution back to the clean one. The connection extends to private robust estimation: adding Gaussian noise calibrated to the Wasserstein sensitivity yields a mechanism that is both differentially private and robust to Huber contamination, with the privacy‑robustness trade‑off governed by the geometry of the Wasserstein space.

Optimal Transport for Reinforcement Learning and Policy Optimization

Reinforcement learning (RL) seeks a policy π that maximizes the expected cumulative reward. Distributional RL replaces the scalar value function with the full distribution of returns Zπ(s,a). The Wasserstein distance between return distributions provides a metric that respects the geometry of the reward space and leads to stable learning algorithms. The Quantile Regression DQN (QR‑DQN) and the Categorical DQN (C51) both minimize a Wasserstein‑type loss between the predicted return distribution and the target distribution obtained from the Bellman operator. The Bellman operator is a contraction in the maximal 1‑Wasserstein metric, guaranteeing convergence of the distributional value iteration. Policy optimization can be cast as a gradient flow in the Wasserstein space of policies: the entropy‑regularized objective J(π) = E[∑γᵗ r(sₜ,aₜ)] + τ H(π) yields a JKO‑type proximal update πₖ₊₁ = argmin_π { KL(π‖πₖ) + (1/η) W₂²(π, πₖ) } where the Wasserstein distance is defined on the space of state‑action distributions induced by the policy. This update is equivalent to a mirror descent step with the Wasserstein metric as the Bregman divergence, and it enjoys a convergence rate of O(1/√k) for non‑convex objectives. In imitation learning, the Generative Adversarial Imitation Learning (GAIL) objective can be rewritten as a Wasserstein GAN where the discriminator is a 1‑Lipschitz function and the generator is the policy. The Wasserstein Adversarial Imitation Learning (WAIL) algorithm directly minimizes the 1‑Wasserstein distance between the expert's state‑action distribution and the learner's distribution, yielding a policy that matches the expert's occupancy measure without requiring a learned reward function. The Kantorovich–Rubinstein duality provides a certificate of optimality: the optimal discriminator is the Kantorovich potential, and its gradient gives the direction of policy improvement. Recent theoretical work by Zhang, Chen, and Wang establishes that the sample complexity of WAIL scales with the covering number of the policy class in the Wasserstein metric, which is often much smaller than the covering number in total variation.

Optimal Transport on RCD Spaces and the Splitting Theorem

The theory of Ricci curvature lower bounds for non‑smooth metric measure spaces was pioneered by Lott–Sturm–Villani (CD(K,N)) and refined by Ambrosio–Gigli–Savaré (RCD(K,N)). An RCD(K,N) space is a metric measure space satisfying a synthetic Ricci curvature lower bound K and an upper dimension bound N, together with the infinitesimal Hilbertianity condition that the Cheeger energy is quadratic. Optimal transport is the central tool: the heat flow is the gradient flow of the relative entropy in the 2‑Wasserstein space, and the Evolution Variational Inequality (EVI) characterization of the heat flow is equivalent to the CD(K,∞) condition. The splitting theorem for RCD(0,N) spaces states that if an RCD(0,N) space contains a line (a globally distance‑minimizing geodesic defined on ℝ), then the space is isomorphic to a product ℝ × Y where Y is an RCD(0,N‑1) space. The proof uses optimal transport to construct a Busemann function b(x) = limt→∞ (d(x,γ(t)) - t) associated with the line γ. The function b is 1‑Lipschitz and its gradient flow in the Wasserstein space yields a measure‑preserving map that splits the space. The key step is to show that the Hopf–Lax semigroup applied to b produces a Kantorovich potential whose transport map is an isometry in the direction of the line. This argument relies on the fact that on RCD spaces the heat flow and the Hopf–Lax semigroup commute, a consequence of the Bakry–Émery calculus on the Wasserstein space. The splitting theorem has profound applications: it implies the maximal diameter theorem for RCD(K,N) spaces with K>0, the volume cone implies metric cone principle, and the structure theory of non‑collapsed RCD spaces. Recent work by Gigli, Mondino, and Savaré extends the splitting theorem to RCD(K,N) spaces with K<0 by using the optimal transport map associated with the squared distance cost to a point at infinity, revealing a warped product structure. These results demonstrate that optimal transport not only provides a metric on the space of probability measures but also encodes the infinitesimal geometry of the underlying space itself.

  • Confusing the role of common noise in mean field games with idiosyncratic noise; common noise leads to a random measure flow, not a deterministic limit.
  • Assuming the entropically regularized transport map converges to the Monge map without controlling the regularization parameter; the bias is of order ε log ε and must be accounted for in causal effect estimates.
  • Treating the Wasserstein sensitivity in differential privacy as the ℓ₂ sensitivity; the Gaussian mechanism's privacy guarantee is governed by the 2‑Wasserstein distance between output laws, not the Euclidean distance between datasets.
  • Believing that distributional RL algorithms minimize the 2‑Wasserstein distance; QR‑DQN and C51 minimize a quantile or categorical loss that approximates the 1‑Wasserstein distance, not the 2‑Wasserstein distance.
  • Overlooking the infinitesimal Hilbertianity condition in RCD spaces; without it the Cheeger energy is not quadratic and the heat flow is not a gradient flow in the Wasserstein space, breaking the splitting theorem proof.
  • Thinking that the splitting theorem holds for CD(K,N) spaces without the RCD refinement; the theorem fails on Finsler manifolds that satisfy CD but not RCD.
  • Neglecting the dual formulation in robust statistics; the worst‑case risk over a Wasserstein ball is exactly the conjugate of the cost function, and ignoring this duality leads to suboptimal robust estimators.

Wasserstein Gradient Flows for Nonlocal Interaction Energies with Anisotropic Kernels

The gradient flow of an interaction energy E(μ) = ½∫∫ K(x−y) dμ(x)dμ(y) in the 2‑Wasserstein space has been studied extensively for radial kernels K. When K is anisotropic, i.e. K(z) = Φ(Az) with a symmetric positive‑definite matrix A, the Eulerian velocity field acquires a direction‑dependent mobility. The continuity equation ∂ₜμ + ∇·(μ v) = 0 is coupled with the force v = −∇(K*μ). Because the kernel is no longer isotropic, the displacement convexity of E depends on the eigenvalues of A and on the convexity of Φ. If Φ is λ‑convex on ℝᵈ, then E is λ·λₘᵢₙ(A)‑displacement convex, where λₘᵢₙ(A) is the smallest eigenvalue of A. This refined convexity estimate yields exponential convergence to equilibrium for the associated McKean–Vlasov equation when λ·λₘᵢₙ(A) > 0. The JKO scheme remains well‑posed: each step minimizes μ ↦ (1/2τ)W₂²(μ,μₖ) + E(μ). The minimizer satisfies the optimality condition (id − Tₖ)/τ = −∇(K*μₖ₊₁) where Tₖ is the optimal transport map from μₖ₊₁ to μₖ. Numerical implementation can exploit the anisotropy by pre‑conditioning the cost matrix with A⁻¹, which reduces the condition number of the linear system arising in semi‑discrete solvers. Recent work shows that for kernels of the form K(z)=|Az|ᵖ with p>1, the flow preserves the class of Gaussian measures and the covariance matrix evolves according to a Riccati‑type ODE that can be solved explicitly. This explicit Gaussian closure provides a benchmark for testing high‑dimensional particle methods.

Optimal Transport on Metric Trees and Ultrametric Spaces

Metric trees are geodesic spaces in which any two points are joined by a unique arc isometric to a line segment. Ultrametric spaces satisfy the strong triangle inequality d(x,z) ≤ max{d(x,y), d(y,z)}. Both structures appear in phylogenetics, hierarchical clustering, and the theory of p‑adic numbers. On a finite metric tree, the 1‑Wasserstein distance between two probability measures can be computed in linear time by a bottom‑up aggregation of mass excesses along edges. The optimal transport plan is supported on the set of directed edges oriented from surplus to deficit vertices, and the Kantorovich potential is piecewise linear with slope ±1 on each edge. For the quadratic cost on a tree, the optimal map is still monotone along each branch, but the Monge–Ampère equation reduces to a system of balance equations at branching points. In ultrametric spaces, the cost c(x,y)=d(x,y)ᵖ with p≥1 yields a transport problem that can be solved by a greedy algorithm on the associated dendrogram: mass is moved upward to the lowest common ancestor and then downward. The resulting Wasserstein distance coincides with the ℓᵖ distance between the vectors of cumulative masses on the leaves. This combinatorial structure makes the computation of Wasserstein barycenters on trees equivalent to a convex optimization problem on the space of edge‑wise mass flows, solvable by a proximal‑point method with guaranteed linear convergence. Moreover, the curvature‑dimension condition CD(K,N) on a metric tree forces K≤0; the space is an RCD(0,N) space exactly when it is a Euclidean building of dimension N. These synthetic curvature bounds have been used to prove concentration inequalities for empirical measures on phylogenetic trees.

Worked Example: 1‑Wasserstein on a Star Tree
Consider a star tree with center c and three leaves ℓ₁,ℓ₂,ℓ₃ each at distance 1 from c. Let μ = 0.5δℓ₁+0.5δℓ₂ and ν = 0.5δℓ₂+0.5δℓ₃. Compute W₁(μ,ν).
Mass at ℓ₁ (0.5) must travel to ℓ₃ via c: distance 2. Mass at ℓ₂ cancels. Total cost = 0.5×2 = 1. Hence W₁(μ,ν)=1.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks for Wasserstein distance on a tree, first identify the unique paths between support points. Write the mass balance at each vertex; the optimal plan routes surplus along those paths. The cost is the sum over edges of (absolute net flow)×(edge length). This linear‑time algorithm is a standard shortcut that avoids setting up a full linear program.

Wasserstein Barycenters on Riemannian Manifolds and Their Statistical Properties

The Fréchet mean of probability measures μ₁,…,μₘ in the 2‑Wasserstein space over a complete Riemannian manifold (M,g) is called the Wasserstein barycenter. Existence and uniqueness hold under the condition that the manifold has non‑negative sectional curvature and the measures have bounded support. The barycenter ν satisfies the fixed‑point equation ν = (1/m)∑ᵢ (Tᵢ)#ν where Tᵢ is the optimal transport map from ν to μᵢ. On a Hadamard manifold (complete, simply connected, non‑positive curvature), the map ν ↦ (1/m)∑ᵢ (Tᵢ)#ν is a contraction in W₂, guaranteeing a unique barycenter and linear convergence of the iterative algorithm νₖ₊₁ = (1/m)∑ᵢ (Tᵢ)#νₖ. Statistical estimation of the population barycenter from i.i.d. samples μ₁,…,μₙ drawn from a distribution P on 𝒫₂(M) has been studied: the empirical barycenter νₙ converges almost surely to the population barycenter ν★, and a central limit theorem holds in the tangent cone at ν★ with rate √n. The asymptotic covariance operator is the inverse of the Hessian of the Fréchet functional, which can be expressed in terms of the covariance of the optimal transport maps Tᵢ. For Gaussian measures on ℝᵈ with the Bures–Wasserstein metric, the barycenter is again Gaussian with mean equal to the arithmetic mean of the means and covariance solving a Riccati equation. This closed form enables exact computation of confidence regions for the barycenter in high‑dimensional applications such as diffusion tensor imaging.

Entropic Optimal Transport with Generalized Cost Functions and the Schrödinger Bridge

Entropic regularization replaces the linear Kantorovich problem by minimizing ∫ c dπ + ε KL(π|μ⊗ν) over couplings π. When the cost c is not quadratic but a general lower‑semicontinuous function, the Schrödinger bridge formulation seeks a dynamic path (πₜ)ₜ∈[0,1] that minimizes the relative entropy with respect to the law of a reference diffusion whose generator is L = Δ − ∇V·∇. The static problem is recovered as the marginal at time 1 of the bridge. The dual potentials (φ,ψ) satisfy the Schrödinger system φ(x) = −ε log ∫ exp((ψ(y)−c(x,y))/ε) dν(y) and symmetrically for ψ. For costs of the form c(x,y)=h(x−y) with h strictly convex, the optimal plan is induced by a map T(x)=x−∇h* (∇φ(x)) where h* is the Legendre transform. The regularization bias is of order ε log ε for smooth costs, but for non‑smooth costs such as the ℓ¹ norm the bias can be O(ε). The Sinkhorn algorithm converges linearly with rate 1−O(ε) when the cost matrix satisfies a diagonal dominance condition. In the dynamic setting, the JKO scheme with entropic regularization yields a discrete‑time approximation of the Fokker–Planck equation with diffusion coefficient ε. This connection provides a variational interpretation of the Schrödinger bridge as the most likely evolution of a noisy particle system conditioned on its endpoints.

Wasserstein Distance for Gaussian Processes and Its Applications in Bayesian Inference

A centered Gaussian process on a compact index set T is fully characterized by its covariance kernel K(s,t). The space of such processes can be equipped with the 2‑Wasserstein distance induced by the Cameron–Martin norm: W₂²(GP(0,K₁), GP(0,K₂)) = Tr(K₁) + Tr(K₂) − 2 Tr((K₁1/2 K₂ K₁1/2)1/2). This formula mirrors the Bures–Wasserstein distance between finite‑dimensional Gaussian measures. When the kernels are trace‑class operators on L²(T), the distance is finite and the space becomes a complete geodesic metric space. Geodesics are given by the operator interpolation Kₜ = (1−t)K₁ + t K₂ − 2√t(1−t) (K₁1/2 K₂ K₁1/2)1/2. In Bayesian nonparametrics, the posterior distribution over covariance kernels given data can be approximated by a Wasserstein barycenter of Gaussian process priors, yielding a computationally tractable variational family. The contraction rate of the posterior in W₂ is governed by the metric entropy of the kernel class in the operator norm; for Sobolev‑type kernels the rate is n−α/(2α+1) where α is the smoothness index. Recent work shows that the Wasserstein gradient flow of the Kullback–Leibler divergence on the space of Gaussian processes coincides with the solution of a matrix Riccati equation, providing a deterministic alternative to MCMC for posterior sampling.

  • Confusing the displacement convexity constant for anisotropic kernels with the isotropic case; the smallest eigenvalue of the anisotropy matrix must be included.
  • Assuming the optimal transport map on a tree is always a permutation of leaves; branching points create mass splitting that cannot be represented by a single map.
  • Neglecting the curvature condition when proving uniqueness of Wasserstein barycenters on manifolds; non‑positive curvature is essential for the contraction argument.
  • Treating the entropic bias as uniformly O(ε) for all costs; non‑smooth costs such as ℓ¹ exhibit a larger bias that must be accounted for in statistical guarantees.
  • Using the finite‑dimensional Bures formula for Gaussian processes without verifying trace‑class conditions; infinite‑dimensional kernels may lead to infinite Wasserstein distance.
  • Overlooking the role of the reference diffusion in the Schrödinger bridge; the bridge is defined relative to a specific reversible Markov process, not merely the static cost.
  • Believing that the Sinkhorn algorithm converges at the same rate for any cost matrix; diagonal dominance or scaling is required for linear convergence.

Wasserstein Distance in Distributionally Robust Optimization

Distributionally robust optimization (DRO) replaces a single nominal distribution by an ambiguity set of probability measures and optimizes the worst‑case expected cost over that set. When the ambiguity set is a Wasserstein ball of radius ρ centered at the empirical measure μ_N = frac1NΣi=1^N δxi_i, the resulting problem admits a finite‑dimensional convex reformulation. For a loss function ℓ(x,xi) that is convex in ξ and Lipschitz continuous with constant L, the worst‑case expectation equals frac1NΣi=1^N supxi {ℓ(x,xi) - λ c(xi,xi_i)} + λρ where λ≥0 is a dual variable and c is the transport cost. This dual representation, established by Blanchet and Murthy (2019) and Esfahani and Kuhn (2018), turns the infinite‑dimensional DRO into a tractable conic program when c is a norm and ℓ is piecewise linear or quadratic. The radius ρ can be calibrated from data using concentration inequalities: for a light‑tailed distribution on ℝᵈ, choosing ρ = big((log(1/β))/(c1 N)big)1/max(d,2) guarantees that the true distribution lies in the ball with probability at least 1−β. In portfolio optimization, the Wasserstein DRO model yields a robust allocation that hedges against distributional misspecification and automatically incorporates a regularization effect proportional to ρ. Recent extensions treat ambiguous chance constraints, multi‑stage decision processes, and ambiguity sets defined by the 1‑Wasserstein distance, which lead to linear programming reformulations. The key theoretical insight is that the Wasserstein metric metrizes weak convergence plus convergence of first moments, so the ambiguity set captures both shape and location uncertainty, unlike φ‑divergence balls which only control relative entropy.

Optimal Transport for Mean‑Field Games with Common Noise

Mean‑field games (MFG) with common noise describe large populations of indistinguishable agents whose dynamics are driven by both idiosyncratic Brownian motions W^i and a shared Brownian motion W0. The state of a representative agent follows dX_t = α_t dt + σ dW_t + σ0 dW0_t with control α_t. The common noise makes the limiting empirical measure a stochastic process μ = (μ_t)t∈[0,T] taking values in the Wasserstein space P2(R^d). The value function u(t,x,μ) becomes a random field on [0,T]timesR^dtimesP2(R^d) and satisfies the stochastic master equation derived by Carmona and Delarue (2018) and Cardaliaguet, Delarue, Lasry, and Lions (2019). This equation couples a forward stochastic Fokker–Planck equation for the conditional law μ_t = L(X_t|W0) with a backward stochastic Hamilton–Jacobi–Bellman equation for u. Existence and uniqueness of classical solutions require the monotonicity condition of Lasry–Lions and sufficient regularity of the Hamiltonian; under these assumptions the master equation is well‑posed in the space of C1 functions on the Wasserstein space equipped with the Lions derivative. Numerically, the conditional law can be approximated by a particle system with common noise, leading to a system of coupled McKean–Vlasov SDEs. Deep learning methods parameterize the value function as a neural network on the space of measures (using permutation‑invariant architectures) and train it by minimizing the residual of the master equation. The propagation of chaos result holds in the sense that the empirical measure of the N‑player game converges in law to the conditional law μ as N→∞, with a rate of order N-1/2 in Wasserstein distance when the coefficients are Lipschitz. This framework has been applied to systemic risk modeling, where the common noise represents a macroeconomic shock, and to crowd motion with environmental uncertainty.

Worked Example
Consider a mean‑field game with common noise where the state dynamics are dX_t = α_t dt + σ dW_t + σ0 dW0_t. Explain how the conditional law of X given the common noise path enters the master equation and why the value function becomes a random field on Wasserstein space.
The common noise W0 is observed by all agents, so their strategies can depend on its realization. The empirical measure of the N‑player system, conditioned on a path of W0, converges to a deterministic measure‑valued process μ_t = L(X_t|W0). In the limit, each agent solves a stochastic control problem where the state distribution μ is an exogenous stochastic process. The dynamic programming principle yields a value function u(t,x,μ) that depends on the current conditional law μ. Because μ itself is random (it is a functional of the common noise path), u is a random field on [0,T]timesR^dtimesP2(R^d). The master equation is obtained by applying Itô’s formula to u along the flow of μ and the optimal state trajectory; it couples a forward stochastic Fokker–Planck equation for μ with a backward stochastic HJB equation for u. The Lions derivative ∂_μ u appears in the drift of the HJB equation, reflecting the sensitivity of the value to changes in the conditional law. Thus the conditional law enters both as a state variable in the forward equation and as an argument of the value function, making the master equation a system on the Wasserstein space of random measures.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks about the master equation in MFG with common noise, first identify the two coupled equations: the stochastic Fokker–Planck for the conditional law and the stochastic HJB for the value function. Remember that the value function is defined on the product of time, space, and the Wasserstein space of conditional laws, and that its derivative with respect to the measure (Lions derivative) appears explicitly. If a numerical method is requested, mention particle approximations for the conditional law and neural network parameterizations that respect permutation invariance. Always check whether the monotonicity condition is assumed; without it, uniqueness of solutions may fail.

Wasserstein Gradient Flows for Nonlocal Interaction Energies with Anisotropic Kernels

The gradient flow of a nonlocal interaction energy E(μ) = frac12iint W(x-y) dμ(x)dμ(y) in the 2‑Wasserstein space generates the aggregation equation ∂_tμ = ∇·(μ∇ W*μ). When the kernel is anisotropic, W(z) = frac1p|Az|^p with a symmetric positive‑definite matrix A and p>1, the displacement convexity of E depends on the eigenvalues of A. Carrillo, McCann, and Villani (2003) showed that E is displacement convex if and only if the Hessian of W is positive semidefinite, which for the anisotropic power kernel holds when p≥ 2 or when 1

2. The JKO minimizing movement scheme μk ∈ argmin_μ big{E(μ) + (1)/(2τ)W22(μ,μk-1)big} converges to the gradient flow as τ→0 with a rate O(τ) in the energy dissipation sense. For anisotropic kernels, the proximal map can be computed via a semi‑discrete optimal transport problem where the target measure is a sum of Dirac masses weighted by the solution of a power diagram; the cell boundaries are ellipsoids determined by A. Long‑time behavior is governed by the confinement potential: if W(z)→∞ as |z|→∞, the flow converges to a unique stationary state which is a Dirac mass at the origin for purely attractive kernels, or to a compactly supported density for repulsive‑attractive kernels. Quantitative convergence rates in Wasserstein distance follow from the Evolution Variational Inequality (EVI) when the energy is λ‑convex along geodesics; the constant λ is the smallest eigenvalue of the matrix A^T A times a factor depending on p. Recent work by Figalli and Glaudo (2021) extends these results to kernels with singularities at the origin, showing that the flow preserves regularity and that the MTW tensor controls the formation of singularities in the density.

Optimal Transport on Metric Measure Spaces with Variable Dimension and Ricci Curvature Bounds

The Lott–Sturm–Villani theory defines synthetic Ricci curvature lower bounds via the curvature‑dimension condition CD(K,N) on a metric measure space (X,d,m). When the dimension N is allowed to vary with the point, one obtains the variable‑dimension condition CD(K,N(·)) introduced by Sturm (2018) and further developed by Gigli, Mondino, and Savaré (2020). The function N:X→[1,∞] must be measurable and essentially bounded; the condition requires that for any two absolutely continuous measures μ0,μ1 there exists a geodesic (μ_t) in P2(X) such that the Renyi entropy S_N(μ|m) = -∫ ρ1-1/N(x) dm is K‑convex along the geodesic. This formulation captures spaces like weighted Riemannian manifolds with a density that changes the effective dimension, metric cones over RCD*(K,N) spaces, and glued spaces where the dimension jumps across interfaces. The measure contraction property MCP(K,N(·)) is a weaker, more tractable condition that still yields a Bishop–Gromov volume comparison with a variable dimension function. On RCD*(K,N) spaces (the infinitesimally Hilbertian refinement), the heat flow coincides with the gradient flow of the Cheeger energy, and the Bakry–Émery calculus holds with a dimension‑dependent Γ₂ inequality. Applications include the study of Ricci limit spaces with singularities, where the dimension function reflects the local Euclidean rank, and the analysis of optimal transport on stratified spaces where the dimension drops on lower‑dimensional strata. The variable‑dimension framework also provides a natural setting for the Gaussian isoperimetric inequality on infinite‑dimensional Wiener space, where N(x)=∞ almost everywhere.

Wasserstein Barycenters for Unbalanced Measures and the Hellinger–Kantorovich Geometry

Classical Wasserstein barycenters require all input measures to have the same total mass. In many applications — medical imaging, single‑cell genomics, and resource allocation — the measures have different masses and mass creation or destruction is meaningful.
