The Hellinger–Kantorovich (HK) distance, also known as the Wasserstein–Fisher–Rao metric, lifts the optimal transport problem to the cone over the base space, allowing mass variation through a combination of transport and homogeneous Hellinger (Fisher–Rao) interpolation. For measures μ_i on a metric space (X,d), the unbalanced barycenter minimizes Σi=1^m λ_i HK2(nu,μ_i) over nuinM_+(X). The HK distance admits a dynamic Benamou–Brenier formulation with a continuity equation ∂_tρ + ∇·(ρ v) = ρ α where the source term α accounts for mass change, and the action functional is ∫01∫_X (|v|2 + frac14|α|2)ρ dx dt. Existence and uniqueness of the barycenter follow from the geodesic convexity of the squared HK distance on the cone, which is a CAT(0) space when the base space has non‑positive curvature. Numerically, the problem can be solved by a generalized Sinkhorn algorithm that alternates scaling of the transport plan and scaling of the marginal masses; convergence is linear under a diagonal dominance condition on the cost matrix. The barycenter inherits the support structure of the inputs: if all μ_i are discrete, the barycenter is discrete with at most the sum of the support sizes. In practice, the HK barycenter provides a principled way to average shapes with different areas, to align gene expression profiles across samples with varying sequencing depth, and to fuse probability maps from multiple sensors with different detection efficiencies.

  • Assuming the Wasserstein DRO ambiguity set is always a ball; in practice, moment constraints or divergence constraints are often added, changing the dual reformulation.
  • Confusing the conditional law in MFG with common noise with the unconditional law; the master equation lives on the space of conditional laws, not on deterministic measures.
  • Neglecting the role of the MTW tensor when checking displacement convexity for anisotropic interaction kernels; the tensor determines whether the energy is λ‑convex along Wasserstein geodesics.
  • Applying the standard CD(K,N) condition to spaces where the effective dimension varies, such as weighted manifolds with non‑constant density, without using the variable‑dimension formulation.
  • Treating the Hellinger–Kantorovich barycenter as a simple weighted average of masses; the barycenter involves a non‑linear interpolation that couples transport and mass change.
  • Remember to verify curvature assumptions when applying displacement convexity on manifolds.

The Master Equation for Mean Field Games with Common Noise

The master equation is a partial differential equation posed on the space of probability measures that encodes the value function of a mean field game when a common noise drives all agents. Unlike the standard mean field game system — a forward–backward pair of a Fokker–Planck equation for the law of the representative agent and a Hamilton–Jacobi–Bellman equation for the value function — the master equation collapses the system into a single equation for the function U(t,x,μ) where μ∈P₂(ℝᵈ) is the current distribution of states. The equation reads

∂ₜU + H(x,∇ₓU) + ∫ℝᵈ ∇_μU(t,x,μ)(y)·b(y,μ) μ(dy) + ½ Tr[σσᵀ ∇ₓ²U] + ½ ∫ℝᵈ∫ℝᵈ Tr[σσᵀ ∇_μ²U(t,x,μ)(y,y')] μ(dy)μ(dy') = 0

with terminal condition U(T,x,μ)=G(x,μ). Here H is the Hamiltonian, b the drift of the state dynamics, σ the common noise diffusion matrix, and ∇_μU denotes the Lions derivative (the linear functional derivative on the Wasserstein space). The term involving ∇_μ²U captures the effect of the common noise on the distribution; it is a second‑order operator on P₂(ℝᵈ) often written using the intrinsic derivative on the Wasserstein manifold.

Existence and uniqueness of classical solutions require strong regularity: U must be C¹ in time, C² in x, and twice Lions‑differentiable in μ with bounded derivatives. Under monotonicity conditions on the coupling (Lasry–Lions monotonicity) and uniform ellipticity of σ, Cardaliaguet, Delarue, Lasry and Lions (2019) proved global well‑posedness. When common noise is degenerate (σ=0) the master equation reduces to a first‑order PDE on P₂, and viscosity solution theory developed by Gangbo, Święch and others provides a framework for discontinuous value functions.

The master equation is central for several reasons. First, it gives a deterministic description of the stochastic game, enabling numerical schemes based on finite differences on the space of measures (e.g., particle methods combined with regression on the empirical measure). Second, it connects to the theory of propagation of chaos: the law of the N‑player system converges to the solution of the master equation as N→∞, with quantitative rates in Wasserstein distance. Third, it provides a natural setting for the analysis of equilibria in games with major–minor players, where the major player’s state appears as an additional argument in U.

Recent extensions include master equations for games with absorption, stopping times, and controls acting on the drift of the common noise. The probabilistic representation via the stochastic forward–backward system (the McKean–Vlasov FBSDE) remains a powerful tool for proving regularity and for designing Monte‑Carlo algorithms.

Wasserstein Gradient Flows for Nonconvex Interaction Energies: Convergence to Critical Points

Gradient flows in the Wasserstein space P₂(ℝᵈ) are curves (μₜ) satisfying the Evolution Variational Inequality (EVI) ∂ₜ½W₂²(μₜ,ν) + ℱ(μₜ) ≤ ℱ(ν) for all ν∈P₂, where ℱ is the driving energy. For convex energies (e.g., internal energy ∫U(ρ)dx with U convex, or interaction energy ½∫∫W(x−y)ρ(x)ρ(y)dxdy with W convex) the flow is uniquely defined and contractive. When the interaction potential W is nonconvex — typical in models of swarming, opinion dynamics, or phase separation — the energy ℱ fails to be λ‑convex along geodesics, and the standard JKO scheme may produce multiple limit curves.

Recent work by Carrillo, McCann, Villani and later by Daneri, Savaré, and others has developed a notion of “generalized gradient flow” based on the De Giorgi minimizing movement scheme with a relaxed energy. The key idea is to replace the global λ‑convexity by a local “one‑sided” condition: there exists a constant λ∈ℝ such that for every μ,ν in a neighbourhood, ℱ(ν) ≥ ℱ(μ) + ⟨∇ℱ(μ),ν−μ⟩ + ½λW₂²(μ,ν). Under this condition, the JKO iterates converge (up to subsequences) to a curve satisfying the Energy Dissipation Equality (EDE) ∂ₜℱ(μₜ) = −|∂ℱ|²(μₜ) − |μ̇ₜ|², where |∂ℱ| is the metric slope.

For nonconvex interaction energies of the form ℱ(μ)=∫Vρdx + ½∫∫W(x−y)ρ(x)ρ(y)dxdy with W∈C² and ∇²W bounded below, the metric slope can be computed explicitly: |∂ℱ|²(μ)=∫|∇V + ∇W*ρ|²ρdx. The resulting gradient flow is the continuity equation ∂ₜρ + ∇·(ρv)=0 with velocity v = −∇V − ∇W*ρ. This is precisely the aggregation–diffusion equation (with diffusion if an entropy term is added). Convergence to stationary states (critical points of ℱ) can be established using the Łojasiewicz–Simon inequality adapted to the Wasserstein setting: if ℱ is real analytic on a suitable submanifold of P₂, then any bounded trajectory converges to a single critical point with an explicit rate.

Applications include the Keller–Segel chemotaxis model (W = −log|x| in 2D), the Cucker–Smale alignment model (W(x)=|x|²), and the Patlak–Keller–Segel system with nonlinear diffusion. In each case, the nonconvexity of W leads to pattern formation (clustering, blow‑up, or flocking). The Wasserstein gradient flow framework provides a unified variational structure that yields a priori estimates, compactness, and a natural notion of generalized solution even beyond the blow‑up time.

Worked Example: JKO Step for a Nonconvex Interaction Energy
Consider the energy ℱ(μ)=½∫∫W(x−y)μ(dx)μ(dy) on ℝ with W(z)=−cos(z). Starting from μ₀ = δ₀, compute the first JKO iterate μ₁ with time step τ>0, i.e., minimize μ↦½τ⁻¹W₂²(μ,δ₀)+ℱ(μ) over P₂(ℝ). Show that the minimizer is a Dirac mass δx₁ and find x₁ as a function of τ.
For any μ∈P₂(ℝ), the Wasserstein distance to δ₀ is W₂²(μ,δ₀)=∫x²μ(dx). The interaction energy for a Dirac δ_x is ℱ(δ_x)=½W(0)=−½. Hence the functional to minimize becomes J(x)=½τ⁻¹x²−½. The unique minimizer is x₁=0, independent of τ. Therefore μ₁=δ₀. This reflects the fact that the cosine potential is minimized at zero separation; the JKO step does not move the mass because the energy is already at its global minimum for a single particle. If the initial measure were a sum of two Diracs, the nonconvexity would create multiple local minima and the JKO scheme would select one depending on τ.
Exam Strategy: Identifying λ‑Convexity in Nonconvex Energies
When a question asks whether a given interaction energy generates a unique Wasserstein gradient flow, check the Hessian of the potential W. If ∇²W ≥ −λI for some λ∈ℝ, the energy is λ‑convex along generalized geodesics (in the sense of McCann). This one‑sided bound is sufficient for the JKO scheme to converge to a curve satisfying the Energy Dissipation Equality. Remember that λ may be negative; the flow is still well defined but not contractive. In multiple‑choice settings, the presence of a uniform lower bound on ∇²W is the standard sufficient condition.

Variable‑Dimension Curvature‑Dimension Conditions and Their Applications

The classical curvature‑dimension condition CD(K,N) requires a constant dimension parameter N∈[1,∞]. Many natural spaces — weighted Riemannian manifolds with a density that changes the effective dimension, metric cones over RCD*(K,N) spaces, and glued manifolds where the dimension jumps across an interface — do not fit this framework. Sturm (2018) and Gigli–Mondino–Savaré (2020) introduced the variable‑dimension condition CD(K,N(·)) where N:X→[1,∞] is a measurable, essentially bounded function. The definition uses the Rényi entropy functional SN(·)(μ|m)=−∫ρ1−1/N(x)m(dx) for μ=ρm. A geodesic (μₜ) in P₂(X) satisfies the K‑convexity inequality

SN(·)(μₜ|m) ≤ (1−t)SN(·)(μ₀|m) + t SN(·)(μ₁|m) − ½K t(1−t)W₂²(μ₀,μ₁)

for all t∈[0,1]. The dimension function enters the entropy through the exponent 1−1/N(x), which varies along the geodesic. This formulation recovers the Bishop–Gromov volume comparison with a variable dimension: for m‑a.e. x, the ratio m(B_r(x))/vK,N(x)(r) is non‑increasing in r, where vK,N is the model volume in the space form of curvature K and dimension N.

On RCD*(K,N(·)) spaces (the infinitesimally Hilbertian refinement), the heat flow coincides with the gradient flow of the Cheeger energy, and the Bakry–Émery calculus holds with a dimension‑dependent Γ₂ inequality: Γ₂(f) ≥ ½K|∇f|² + (Δf)²/N(x). This yields sharp functional inequalities such as the logarithmic Sobolev inequality with a variable dimension constant. Applications include the analysis of Ricci limit spaces with singularities, where N(x) equals the local Euclidean rank, and the study of optimal transport on stratified spaces where the dimension drops on lower‑dimensional strata. The variable‑dimension framework also provides a natural setting for the Gaussian isoperimetric inequality on infinite‑dimensional Wiener space, where N(x)=∞ almost everywhere.

From a computational perspective, the variable‑dimension condition can be discretized on graphs by assigning a dimension weight to each vertex, leading to a generalized Ollivier–Ricci curvature that captures heterogeneous dimensionality. This has been used in network science to detect communities with different intrinsic dimensions.

Hamilton‑Jacobi‑Bellman Equations in Wasserstein Space for Stochastic Control

Stochastic control problems where the state is a probability measure evolving according to a controlled McKean–Vlasov dynamics lead to Hamilton–Jacobi–Bellman (HJB) equations on the Wasserstein space P₂(ℝᵈ). The value function V(t,μ) = infα 𝔼[∫_t^T f(s,μ_s,α_s)ds + g(μ_T)] satisfies the infinite‑dimensional HJB equation

∂ₜV + H(μ,∇_μV) = 0, V(T,μ)=g(μ)

where the Hamiltonian H(μ,p) = infa∈A {∫ p(x)·b(x,μ,a) μ(dx) + f(μ,a)} and ∇_μV denotes the Lions derivative (a function on ℝᵈ representing the linear functional derivative of V with respect to μ). When the control acts on the drift b and the diffusion is non‑degenerate, the HJB equation is a fully nonlinear second‑order PDE on P₂. Viscosity solution theory, developed by Lions, Souganidis, and later by Gangbo, Święch, and others, provides a robust notion of solution that does not require classical differentiability.

A key structural property is the “monotonicity” of the Hamiltonian in the measure argument, which ensures comparison principles. For example, if the running cost f(μ,a) is displacement convex in μ and the dynamics are linear in μ, then H is monotone and the value function is the unique viscosity solution. This monotonicity is closely related to the Lasry–Lions condition in mean field games. The HJB equation also appears in the dynamic programming principle for optimal transport with congestion, where the cost includes a term ∫|v|²ρdx + ∫Φ(ρ)dx with Φ convex; the corresponding Hamiltonian is H(μ,p)=∫|∇p|²ρdx + ∫Φ*(ρ)dx.

Numerical methods for these HJB equations include semi‑Lagrangian schemes on particle approximations of μ, deep learning approaches that parameterize V by neural networks on the space of measures (using permutation‑invariant architectures), and policy iteration algorithms that alternate between solving a linearized Fokker–Planck equation and updating the control via the Hamiltonian minimizer. Convergence rates for particle‑based schemes have been established under Lipschitz assumptions on the Hamiltonian and the dynamics.

Applications span economics (optimal monetary policy with heterogeneous agents), robotics (distribution steering of swarms), and finance (optimal execution with market impact depending on the distribution of orders). In each case, the Wasserstein HJB framework captures the feedback nature of the control: the optimal policy depends on the current distribution of states, not just on a single representative agent.

  • Confusing the master equation with the standard mean field game system; the master equation lives on the space of conditional laws given the common noise path, not on deterministic measures.
  • Assuming λ‑convexity of an interaction energy without verifying a uniform lower bound on the Hessian of the potential; nonconvex potentials may only satisfy a one‑sided bound, which changes the convergence guarantees.
  • Applying the constant‑dimension CD(K,N) condition to spaces where the effective dimension varies, such as weighted manifolds with non‑constant density, without using the variable‑dimension formulation.
  • Treating the Lions derivative ∇_μV as a standard gradient in ℝᵈ; it is a function on the base space representing a linear functional on the tangent cone of P₂.
  • Neglecting the role of the common noise diffusion matrix σ in the master equation; the second‑order term in ∇_μ²U vanishes when σ=0, reducing the equation to first order.
  • Overlooking that the JKO scheme for nonconvex energies may converge only along subsequences to a generalized gradient flow satisfying the Energy Dissipation Equality, not necessarily to the unique classical solution.

Optimal Transport for p‑Wasserstein Distances with p≠2: Geometry, Duality, and Regularity

The quadratic cost c(x,y)=|x−y|² yields the 2‑Wasserstein space whose Riemannian‑like structure underlies the Otto calculus. For a general exponent p∈[1,∞) the p‑Wasserstein distance is defined by

Wₚ(μ,ν) = ( infπ∈Π(μ,ν) ∫ |x−y|ᵖ dπ(x,y) )1/p.

When p≠2 the space (Pₚ(ℝᵈ),Wₚ) is no longer a length space with a formal Riemannian metric; its tangent cones are isometric to Lᵖ(μ) rather than L²(μ). The Kantorovich duality still holds:

Wₚ(μ,ν)ᵖ = supφ,ψ { ∫ φ dμ + ∫ ψ dν : φ(x)+ψ(y) ≤ |x−y|ᵖ }.

The optimal potentials are p‑concave functions; for p>1 they satisfy a p‑Laplacian type equation. Regularity of optimal maps has been studied by Figalli, Kim, and McCann: if μ and ν have densities bounded away from zero and infinity on convex domains, the optimal transport map for the cost |x−y|ᵖ is locally Hölder continuous for p>1, and C¹,α for p≥2 under a uniform convexity assumption on the domains. For p<2 the map may develop singularities even for smooth densities, reflecting the lack of uniform convexity of the cost. The displacement interpolation (McCann interpolation) remains a constant‑speed geodesic in (Pₚ,Wₚ), but the geodesic equations involve the p‑gradient of the potential. This geometry is crucial for gradient flows of functionals that are displacement convex only for certain p, such as the Rényi entropy which is displacement convex in (Pₚ,Wₚ) precisely when p≥2−2/d.

Optimal Transport on Non‑Compact Polish Spaces: Existence, Tightness, and the Role of the Cost Function

Classical existence theorems (Kantorovich, 1942) assume the underlying space X is compact or that the cost function c is continuous and bounded. On a non‑compact Polish space (e.g. ℝᵈ with the Euclidean metric) one must impose tightness conditions on the marginals and growth conditions on c. A standard sufficient condition is that c is lower semicontinuous and satisfies a coercivity estimate: there exist a reference point x₀∈X and a function φ:X→[0,∞) with φ(x)→∞ as x→∞ such that c(x,y) ≥ φ(x)+φ(y) for all x,y. Under this hypothesis the set of admissible couplings Π(μ,ν) is tight, and Prokhorov’s theorem yields a minimiser. When c(x,y)=|x−y|ᵖ on ℝᵈ, the condition reduces to requiring finite p‑th moments: ∫|x|ᵖ dμ(x) < ∞ and similarly for ν. If only one marginal has a finite p‑moment, the infimum may be zero but not attained; a classic counterexample uses μ = δ₀ and ν a heavy‑tailed distribution with infinite p‑moment. The dual formulation remains valid provided one restricts to potentials φ,ψ that are c‑concave and satisfy appropriate integrability. Recent work by Gigli and Tamanini (2021) shows that on proper metric measure spaces satisfying a curvature‑dimension condition CD(K,N), the p‑Wasserstein space is geodesic for all p∈[1,∞) and the existence theory extends without moment assumptions because the curvature bound forces a Gaussian‑type concentration that guarantees tightness of optimal plans.

Worked Example: Existence Failure without Moment Condition
Let μ = δ₀ on ℝ and ν have density f(x) = C/(1+|x|³) (C normalising constant). Show that the 2‑Wasserstein distance W₂(μ,ν) is finite but no optimal transport plan exists.
The second moment of ν is ∫ x² f(x) dx = ∞, so ν∉P₂(ℝ). For any coupling π∈Π(μ,ν) the first marginal forces π = δ₀⊗ν. The cost ∫|x−y|² dπ = ∫ y² dν(y) = ∞, hence W₂(μ,ν)=∞. If we instead consider the cost c(x,y)=|x−y|, the first moment of ν is finite, so W₁(μ,ν) < ∞ and the coupling δ₀⊗ν is optimal. This illustrates that finiteness of Wₚ requires the p‑th moment of both marginals; otherwise the infimum may be infinite or not attained.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a problem asks for existence of an optimal plan on a non‑compact space, first check whether the cost is coercive and whether the marginals have the required moments. Cite the tightness argument via Prokhorov and the lower semicontinuity of the cost. If moments fail, construct a counterexample using a Dirac mass against a heavy‑tailed distribution.

Stability of Optimal Plans under Perturbations of the Cost Function: Quantitative Estimates

In applications the cost function is often known only approximately (e.g. learned from data). Let c and c̃ be two lower semicontinuous costs on X×Y satisfying |c(x,y)−c̃(x,y)| ≤ ε for all (x,y). Denote by Π* and Π̃* the sets of optimal couplings for c and c̃ respectively. A fundamental stability result (Carlier, Duval, Peyré, 2017) states that the Hausdorff distance between Π* and Π̃* in the weak topology is bounded by C ε1/2 provided the marginals have finite second moments and the optimal potentials for c are Lipschitz with a uniform constant. The proof uses the fact that the Kantorovich dual potentials are 1‑Lipschitz with respect to the cost; a perturbation of size ε changes the dual objective by at most ε, which translates into a √ε deviation in the primal via the quadratic growth of the cost around the optimal plan. For the quadratic cost on ℝᵈ, a sharper bound holds: if μ,ν have densities bounded away from zero on convex sets, then the optimal transport maps T and T̃ satisfy ‖T−T̃‖L²(μ) ≤ C ε1/2. This quantitative stability underpins the convergence analysis of entropic regularisation (where c is replaced by c+ε log π) and of stochastic optimisation algorithms that minimise a sampled approximation of the cost.

Optimal Transport for Domain Adaptation with Label Shift: Wasserstein Barycenters and Reweighting

Domain adaptation seeks a predictor trained on a source distribution μ_S that performs well on a target distribution μ_T. When the label marginal changes (label shift) but the class‑conditional distributions remain invariant, the optimal transport framework provides a principled reweighting. Let μ_S = Σk=1^K π_S^k μ_S^k and μ_T = Σk=1^K π_T^k μ_T^k with μ_S^k = μ_T^k for each class k. The Wasserstein barycenter of the class‑conditional measures with weights π_T^k yields a reference distribution ν = argminν Σ_k π_T^k W₂²(μ_S^k, ν). The optimal transport maps T_k pushing μ_S^k to ν are used to transport source samples to the target domain: a source sample (x,k) is mapped to T_k(x). The resulting transported source distribution equals μ_T exactly. In practice the class‑conditional measures are estimated from finite samples; the empirical barycenter problem is solved via the iterative Bregman projection algorithm (Cuturi & Doucet, 2014) with entropic regularisation. Generalisation bounds (Redko et al., 2019) show that the excess risk on the target domain is controlled by the sum of the Wasserstein distances between corresponding class conditionals plus a term of order √(K log n / n). This approach outperforms importance‑weighting methods when the supports of μ_S^k and μ_T^k differ substantially, because the transport map aligns the geometries rather than merely reweighting.

Wasserstein Gradient Flow of the Fisher Information: The Heat Flow, Log‑Sobolev Inequalities, and the Bakry–Émery Criterion

The Fisher information functional I(μ) = ∫ |∇ log ρ|² ρ dx (with μ=ρ dx) is the metric derivative of the Boltzmann entropy along the heat flow. In the Otto calculus the gradient of I in (P₂,W₂) is the vector field v = −∇(Δ log ρ + |∇ log ρ|²). The corresponding gradient flow equation is the fourth‑order PDE ∂ₜρ = ∇·(ρ ∇(Δ log ρ + |∇ log ρ|²)), known as the Fisher‑information flow. However, the more common perspective is that the heat flow ∂ₜρ = Δρ is the gradient flow of the entropy, and its dissipation rate is exactly the Fisher information: d/dt Ent(ρ_t) = −I(ρ_t). The logarithmic Sobolev inequality (LSI) Ent(μ) ≤ (1/2ρ) I(μ) for a probability measure μ with density ρ is equivalent to the exponential decay of entropy along the heat flow with rate ρ. Bakry and Émery (1985) introduced the Γ₂ calculus: for a diffusion generator L = Δ − ∇V·∇, the curvature‑dimension condition CD(ρ,∞) (i.e. Γ₂(f) ≥ ρ Γ(f)) is equivalent to the LSI with constant ρ. In the Wasserstein language, CD(ρ,∞) means that the entropy is ρ‑displacement convex. This convexity yields the Evolution Variational Inequality (EVI) for the heat flow, which in turn implies contractivity of the flow in W₂ and the Talagrand inequality W₂²(μ,ν) ≤ (2/ρ) Ent(μ|ν). Recent extensions by Erbar, Kuwada, and Sturm (2015) show that on metric measure spaces the same equivalence holds: the heat flow is the gradient flow of the entropy, the LSI is equivalent to CD(ρ,∞), and the Fisher information appears as the metric slope of the entropy. These results provide a unified geometric proof of functional inequalities that are central to concentration of measure, isoperimetry, and the analysis of Markov semigroups.

  • Confusing p‑Wasserstein geometry with the quadratic case: the tangent cone is Lᵖ not L², and displacement convexity thresholds change with p.
  • Assuming existence of optimal plans on non‑compact spaces without checking moment or coercivity conditions: a Dirac mass against a heavy‑tailed marginal gives a finite distance but no optimiser.
  • Neglecting the √ε scaling in cost‑perturbation stability: the Hausdorff distance between optimal plan sets grows like the square root of the uniform cost error, not linearly.
  • Treating label‑shift adaptation as mere importance weighting: Wasserstein barycenters align the full geometry of class conditionals, which is essential when supports differ.
  • Mixing up the gradient flow of entropy (heat flow) with the gradient flow of Fisher information: the former is second order, the latter fourth order; only the heat flow satisfies an EVI under CD(ρ,∞).
  • Applying the constant‑dimension CD(K,N) condition to spaces where the effective dimension varies (e.g. weighted manifolds) without using the variable‑dimension formulation.
  • Overlooking that the Kantorovich dual potentials for p≠2 are p‑concave, not 1‑Lipschitz, which changes the regularity theory for optimal maps.

The Monge Problem for General Cost Functions: Twist Condition and c‑Convexity

The classical Monge problem asks for a map T pushing a source measure μ onto a target measure ν while minimizing the total cost ∫ c(x,T(x)) dμ(x). When the cost c is not quadratic, the existence and regularity of optimal maps hinge on two structural hypotheses: the twist condition and c‑convexity. The twist condition requires that for each x the mapping y ↦ ∇ₓc(x,y) be injective; equivalently, the graph of the optimal plan must be a graph of a function. This condition was introduced by Gangbo and McCann (1996) and guarantees that any optimal Kantorovich plan is induced by a map. The c‑transform of a function φ is defined by φᶜ(y)=infₓ[c(x,y)−φ(x)], and a function is c‑convex if it equals its double c‑transform. Under the twist condition, optimal maps are given by T(x)=∇ₓc*(x,∇φ(x)) where φ is a c‑convex potential. The Ma–Trudinger–Wang (MTW) tensor, introduced in 2005, provides a curvature‑type condition on the cost that controls the regularity of the potential; when the MTW tensor is non‑negative (the so‑called (A3w) condition), c‑convex potentials are locally semiconvex and optimal maps are continuous. For costs satisfying (A3s) (strict positivity), Caffarelli‑type global regularity holds on domains that are c‑convex with respect to each other. These results extend the quadratic theory to a broad class of costs including the relativistic cost c(x,y)=√(1+|x−y|²) and the cost on the sphere c(x,y)=arccos(x·y).

Displacement Convexity of Rényi Entropies and the Curvature‑Dimension Condition

McCann’s displacement convexity (1997) showed that the Boltzmann entropy is convex along Wasserstein geodesics in Euclidean space. This property extends to the family of Rényi entropies Sₙ(μ)=1/(1−n) log ∫ ρⁿ dx for n>0, n≠1. On a Riemannian manifold (M,g) with Ricci curvature bounded below by K and dimension bounded above by N, the Rényi entropy is displacement convex precisely when the curvature‑dimension condition CD(K,N) holds in the sense of Lott–Sturm–Villani. The proof uses the Jacobian of the optimal transport map and the Bishop–Gromov volume comparison. For n∈(0,1) the functional is convex if K≥0; for n>1 convexity requires K>0 and an upper bound on N. This equivalence underpins the Bakry–Émery Γ₂ calculus: the condition Γ₂(f)≥KΓ(f)+1/N (Lf)² is exactly the differential formulation of CD(K,N). As a consequence, functional inequalities such as the logarithmic Sobolev inequality (n→1) and the Talagrand inequality follow from displacement convexity. The sharp constants in these inequalities are attained by Gaussian measures on ℝⁿ and by the model spaces of constant curvature. Recent work by Erbar, Kuwada and Sturm (2015) shows that the same equivalence holds on arbitrary metric measure spaces, providing a synthetic notion of Ricci curvature that does not rely on a smooth structure.

Worked Example: Verifying the Twist Condition for a Non‑Quadratic Cost
Consider the cost c(x,y)=|x−y|⁴/4 on ℝᵈ. Show that the twist condition holds and compute the optimal map pushing the standard Gaussian μ=N(0,I) onto ν=N(m,I) with m∈ℝᵈ.
The gradient with respect to x is ∇ₓc(x,y)=|x−y|²(x−y). For fixed x, the map y↦|x−y|²(x−y) is strictly monotone because its Jacobian is 2(x−y)⊗(x−y)+|x−y|²I, which is positive definite for y≠x. Hence the twist condition holds. By Brenier’s theorem for this cost, the optimal map is T(x)=x−∇φ(x) where φ is c‑convex. For Gaussian marginals with equal covariance, the optimal map is the translation T(x)=x+m. Indeed, c(x,x+m)=|m|⁴/4 is constant, so any coupling with this displacement has the same cost; the twist condition forces the unique optimal plan to be the deterministic translation. The c‑convex potential is φ(x)=−m·x+|m|²/2, and one checks that ∇ₓc(x,T(x))=|m|²m equals ∇φ(x) up to an additive constant, confirming optimality.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks to verify the twist condition, compute ∇ₓc and check injectivity by showing the Jacobian is positive definite or by proving strict monotonicity. For displacement convexity of Rényi entropies, remember the threshold n=1 separates the logarithmic Sobolev regime from the power‑law regime; the curvature‑dimension condition CD(K,N) is the exact synthetic criterion. Memorise the statement: “Rényi entropy of order n is displacement convex on a metric measure space iff the space satisfies CD(K,N) with appropriate K,N depending on n.” This single line covers many multiple‑choice and short‑answer items.

The Schrödinger Bridge Problem: Entropic Interpolation and Large Deviations

The Schrödinger bridge problem, posed by Erwin Schrödinger in 1931, asks for the most likely evolution of a diffusion process between two prescribed marginal distributions μ₀ and μ₁ over a fixed time horizon T. Formally, one minimizes the relative entropy H(ℙ|ℝ) over all path‑space measures ℙ with given endpoints, where ℝ is the law of a reference diffusion (typically Brownian motion). The solution is a Markov process whose time‑marginals (ρₜ)ₜ∈[0,T] satisfy the forward‑backward system ∂ₜρₜ+∇·(ρₜ∇φₜ)=0, ∂ₜφₜ+½|∇φₜ|²=0 with boundary conditions ρ₀=μ₀, ρ₁=μ₁. This system is equivalent to the entropic regularization of the quadratic optimal transport problem: adding ε times the Boltzmann entropy to the Kantorovich functional yields the same interpolating curve as the Schrödinger bridge with ε=T. The large‑deviation principle for empirical measures of independent diffusions (Dawson–Gärtner, 1987) shows that the rate function is exactly the relative entropy with respect to the reference process, so the Schrödinger bridge describes the most probable macroscopic trajectory. In the zero‑noise limit ε→0, the entropic interpolation converges to the displacement interpolation of optimal transport, and the potentials φₜ converge to the Kantorovich potentials. Recent numerical schemes (e.g. the iterative proportional fitting procedure of Cuturi, 2013) compute the Schrödinger bridge efficiently, making it a practical tool for generative modeling and domain adaptation.

Quantitative Stability of Optimal Transport Plans under Cost Perturbations

Stability of optimal plans with respect to perturbations of the cost function is a central question for applications where the cost is learned or approximated. Let c and c̃ be two continuous costs on a compact metric space X×Y with ‖c−c̃‖∞≤δ. Denote by Π*(c) and Π*(c̃) the sets of optimal plans. A fundamental result by Carlier, Duval and Peyré (2017) shows that the Hausdorff distance between Π*(c) and Π*(c̃) in the weak topology is bounded by C√δ, where C depends only on the diameter of X×Y and the modulus of continuity of the costs. The √δ scaling is sharp: a simple one‑dimensional example with c(x,y)=|x−y| and a perturbed cost c̃(x,y)=|x−y|+δ·sin(x/δ) yields optimal plans whose supports shift by order √δ. If the costs satisfy a uniform twist condition and the marginals have densities bounded away from zero and infinity, the optimal maps T_c and Tc̃ satisfy ‖T_c−Tc̃‖L²(μ)≤C'√δ. These quantitative bounds are essential for statistical learning: they guarantee that an empirical cost estimated from n samples yields an optimal plan that is Oₚ(n-1/4) close to the true plan, a rate that matches the minimax lower bound for Wasserstein estimation. Extensions to unbalanced and multi‑marginal settings replace the Hausdorff distance by the appropriate divergence (e.g. the Hellinger–Kantorovich distance) and preserve the √δ scaling under analogous regularity assumptions.

  • Assuming the twist condition holds for all smooth costs; it fails for costs with flat regions such as c(x,y)=|x−y| on ℝ.
  • Confusing displacement convexity of Rényi entropy for n>1 with the logarithmic Sobolev inequality; the former requires positive Ricci curvature and an upper dimension bound.
  • Treating the Schrödinger bridge as merely an entropic regularization of optimal transport without recognizing its large‑deviation origin and the distinct time‑reversal symmetry.
  • Using a linear δ bound for cost‑perturbation stability instead of the sharp √δ rate; the square‑root scaling appears in both theoretical bounds and empirical convergence rates.
  • Neglecting the requirement that marginals have densities bounded away from zero when transferring stability from plans to maps; without this, the map may not exist or may be discontinuous.
  • Overlooking that the MTW tensor condition (A3w) is necessary for continuity of optimal maps for general costs; quadratic cost on convex domains satisfies it automatically, but many non‑quadratic costs do not.
  • Applying the curvature‑dimension condition CD(K,N) with constant N to spaces where the effective dimension varies (e.g. weighted manifolds) without using the variable‑dimension formulation.

These advanced topics illustrate how structural conditions on the cost, entropy, and curvature govern existence, regularity, and stability in optimal transport.
